The image has no uv.lock, so when the container starts, `uv run` has to resolve the dependencies again (which needs a package index) and install the dev group that `--no-dev` left out.  **The lockfile mismatch** The template `.gitignore` does not exclude uv.lock, so generated apps keep it. The justfile runs `uv run pytest`, which creates that lock. `dependencies_gate` (`src/factory/gates.py:124-130`) checks the versions locked in uv.lock. The image never uses that lock, so the versions shipped can differ from the ones the gate checked.  **Severity** I rate it medium, not high. It is a template or deployment defect: a container that starts without index egress fails or starts slowly. It does not corrupt data or open a security hole.

### A11. python-worker image does not include the worker package and never starts the consumer

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile:6` · **category:** docker/correctness · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** An app scaffolded for 'Consume order events from a queue' (tests/test_golden_paths.py:88-92) ships an image that answers GET /health with ok and never consumes a message. Even if an adapter is wired in, `import worker` raises ModuleNotFoundError inside the container because /srv/worker was never copied.
- **Suggested fix:** COPY worker ./worker and provide a consumer entrypoint (a separate CMD or process, or a FastAPI lifespan task that runs the loop). Add a `docker build` plus smoke step to the template CI.

**Evidence**

> python-worker/Dockerfile:6 `COPY app ./app` (no `COPY worker ./worker`). Line 8 starts only `uvicorn app.main:app`. app/main.py:1-15 does not import or start worker.run. The README (lines 6-8) presents `worker.run(inbox)` as the consumer. The golden CI (python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10-12) never builds the image, so nothing catches this.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. Every claim checks out against the files I read in the remote repo: - golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile:6 copies only `app` (`COPY app ./app`). Line 8 starts only `uvicorn app.main:app`. The file is byte-identical to golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:1-8, so it was carried over from the FastAPI template and never adapted. - The consumer lives in golden_paths/python-worker/worker/__init__.py (run at lines 45-56). The image never copies it, so `/srv/worker` does not exist inside the container. - app/main.py:1-15 only defines GET /health. It never imports or starts worker.run. The docstring on line 1 itself says the consumer is in worker/. - The template CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml:10-12) runs uv sync, ruff and pytest only. It never runs a docker build or a smoke test, so nothing catches the missing directory. - tests/test_golden_paths.py:88-93 shows a messaging idea ("Consume order events from a queue") is scaffolded from this path, and checks only that worker/__init__.py exists on disk, not in the image.  One mitigating point: the template deliberately leaves the broker adapter unwired (golden.toml:2-3, README:6-8), so an app built from it is expected to edit code anyway. That softens "never consumes" into "the starting image is structurally unable to consume". Even after someone writes an adapter, `import worker` inside the container fails unless they also notice and fix the Dockerfile. That makes this a real packaging defect in the template, but I rate it medium rather than high: it is a scaffold, and no consumer behaviour is wired for any app yet.
2. The code confirms the finding. golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile:1-8 is a copy of golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:1-8 line for line: line 6 `COPY app ./app` with no COPY of worker/, and line 8 runs only `uvicorn app.main:app`. app/main.py:1-15 never imports worker; its docstring (line 1) itself says "consumer is in worker/". worker/__init__.py:45-56 defines run(inbox), and nothing in the image calls it. The template CI (ci.yml:10-12) runs only uv sync, ruff and pytest, never docker build. tests/test_golden_paths.py:88-93 checks only that worker/__init__.py exists in the scaffolded app, not the image. The core claim stands: an image built from this template does not contain the worker package, so a consumer added later cannot be imported (ModuleNotFoundError) until someone also edits the Dockerfile. Two things lower the severity. First, it is by design that no consumer loop runs yet: golden.toml:2-3 and README:6-8 say the broker and its Inbox adapter come later from the design, so "never consumes" is partly expected for a skeleton. Second, a grep of src/factory/foreman.py for docker, image and deploy found nothing, and the repo has no deploy or cloudbuild files, so the factory itself never builds or ships these images. The defect is real but latent: it only shows up once a team containerizes the app. Medium, not high.

### A12. Claudo watchdog and checkpoint stop signal only the orchestrator PID: its agents and sandbox containers are orphaned

- **Location:** `src/factory/claudo.py:160` · **category:** subprocess-cleanup · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** claudo_timeout (default 3600 s, config.py:52) expires mid-task. The orchestrator is SIGKILLed, but its claude/docker children and their containers keep running and committing into the app repo, while the factory returns BuildResult('timeout') and the item is blocked or retried. Any grandchild that inherited the orchestrator's stdout pipe also keeps `for line in proc.stdout` (claudo.py:170) from reaching EOF, so the 'timeout' return itself is delayed until those grandchildren exit.
- **Suggested fix:** Popen with `start_new_session=True` (POSIX) / CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP (Windows), and kill the whole group (`os.killpg`) in expire() and _stop(). Sandbox containers also need labels so they can be reaped (`docker ps -q --filter label=...`).

**Evidence**

> run_build starts the orchestrator with `subprocess.Popen(argv, ..., stdout=PIPE, stderr=STDOUT)` and no `start_new_session`/process group (claudo.py:148-157). On timeout, `expire()` calls `proc.kill()` (claudo.py:160-162). At a checkpoint, `_stop(proc)` sends terminate/kill to the same single PID (claudo.py:175, 340-346). Under LAB_RUNNER=sandbox (foreman.py:1434-1435), Claudo's per-task agents are `docker run` children of that process.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I couldn't refute the timeout part. The checkpoint part is weaker. What I read: claudo.py:148-157 starts the orchestrator with subprocess.Popen and sets neither start_new_session nor a process group. On timeout, expire() calls proc.kill() (claudo.py:160-162), which SIGKILLs only that one PID. _stop() (claudo.py:340-346) also signals only that PID. foreman.py:1434-1435 adds sandbox.claudo_env() when build_where == "sandbox", and that env sets LAB_RUNNER=sandbox (sandbox.py:119). sandbox.py:97-99 describes the agent invocation as `docker run --rm -i`, "the same confinement as Claudo's sandbox runner". Under POSIX, a SIGKILL'd parent cannot clean up its children. Killing a docker CLI client does not stop the container it started. So on the timeout path, agent subprocesses and their containers can outlive the orchestrator after the factory has already recorded 'timeout'. Nothing in AI-factory reaps them: no killpg, no container labels or names, no cleanup. Limits on what I could check: the Claudo repo returned 404, so I could not see how its orchestrator spawns agents. I can't confirm the claim that grandchildren inherit the stdout pipe and delay EOF. I also can't confirm that agents are running when the checkpoint SIGTERM arrives: the orchestrator is paused waiting for approval then and may handle SIGTERM. The timeout case is enough to keep the finding: a bounded watchdog that leaves its work running is a real cleanup defect. It happens only on timeout under sandbox, so medium fits.
2. Confirmed from the code. claudo.py:148-157 starts the orchestrator with subprocess.Popen and sets no start_new_session or process group. On timeout, expire() at claudo.py:160-162 calls proc.kill() (SIGKILL) on that single PID, and nothing in the file uses killpg, setsid or process_group. _stop() at claudo.py:340-346 also signals only the one PID. The callers pass cfg.claudo_timeout (foreman.py:1498, 1556). For sandbox builds, _claudo_env sets LAB_RUNNER=sandbox via sandbox.claudo_env() (sandbox.py:119), so Claudo's agents run as `docker run --rm -i` client processes. SIGKILL cannot be caught, so the orchestrator gets no chance to clean up. Its child claude or docker CLI processes get reparented and keep running, and so do their containers. Nothing in the tests covers grandchild cleanup: test_claudo_build.py uses a fake run_build. Two parts I could not verify, because Claudo's orchestrator is not in this repo. First, whether the agents inherit the stdout pipe, which is what would delay EOF at claudo.py:170. Second, whether Claudo traps SIGTERM in the checkpoint _stop path and reaps its own children. Neither changes the timeout/SIGKILL path, which is the core defect. Medium severity holds.

### A13. Final Claudo run never stops at a checkpoint: a second checkpoint, or a token Claudo rejects, blocks for the full build timeout

- **Location:** `src/factory/claudo.py:173` · **category:** timeouts · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** An agent-written plan has CP-1 mid-plan and CP-2 at the end. IT approves CP-1, Claudo goes on, announces CP-2 and polls. The factory ignores that line and waits claudo_timeout (1 h by default), then reports 'Claudo did not complete after approval (timeout)'. The same happens when Claudo refuses the signed token (e.g. a nonce mismatch) and keeps waiting at CP-1.
- **Suggested fix:** In the final run, treat any checkpoint announcement as a stop. If it is the same CP that was just signed, the approval was refused (report it). If it is a new CP, record it as the next checkpoint instead of hanging.

**Evidence**

> claudo.py:172-176 only breaks on a checkpoint line `if found and stop_at_checkpoint`. _finalize_with_claudo calls run_build with `stop_at_checkpoint=False` (foreman.py:1551-1557). The run_build docstring (claudo.py:143) says Claudo 'BLOCKS (polls) at a blocking checkpoint until a signed human approval appears'. No check stops a plan from carrying more than one CP (the templates only ask the agent that the last section be a checkpoint, templates.py:173).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I couldn't refute this; the code confirms it. In claudo.py:172-176 the stdout loop breaks on a checkpoint line only when `found and stop_at_checkpoint`. _finalize_with_claudo calls run_build with stop_at_checkpoint=False (foreman.py:1551-1557). The run_build docstring (claudo.py:143) says Claudo blocks and polls at a blocking checkpoint. So when the final run hits a second checkpoint, the loop keeps reading and the only way out is the watchdog (claudo.py:164, timeout=cfg.claudo_timeout, default 3600 per config.py:52/122). The result is then 'timeout', and foreman.py:1562-1563 reports 'Claudo did not complete after approval (timeout)'. I found no factory-side check that limits a plan to one checkpoint. PLAN_FORMAT (templates.py:173-176) only requires the LAST section to be the CP-1 checkpoint and does not forbid earlier ones. guard.py:28 accepts any `CP-n` header, and grepping gates/agents/speclint/workitem turned up no checkpoint-count check. Two limits on this finding. First, the plan_review human approval and Claudo's own plan-lint might reject multi-CP plans, but nothing in this repo enforces that. Second, the 'token rejected keeps polling' variant depends on how the external Claudo behaves, which I could not read here. The multi-CP path alone is enough to confirm the hang, and the error message it ends with is misleading. The impact is a bounded one-hour stall followed by a 'blocked' result, with no corruption. Medium stays a reasonable rating.
2. I could not refute it; the code says what the finding claims. In claudo.py:172-176, run_build stops the orchestrator only on a CHECKPOINT_WAIT line (claudo.py:80) when `stop_at_checkpoint` is true. _finalize_with_claudo calls it with stop_at_checkpoint=False (foreman.py:1551-1557). The run_build docstring says Claudo "BLOCKS (polls) at a blocking checkpoint until a signed human approval appears". So if the final run announces a checkpoint again, the factory keeps reading output until the watchdog kills the process. That watchdog is claudo_timeout, 3600 s by default (config.py:52, 122). The run then returns outcome "timeout", and foreman.py:1562-1563 reports "Claudo did not complete after approval (timeout)". Nothing in the factory limits a plan to one checkpoint. PLAN_FORMAT (templates.py:173-176) only asks that the last section be a checkpoint, and the grep over src/ found no count check. Plan-lint runs in the external Claudo engine, whose rules I could not read. The rejected-token case also depends on Claudo's external behavior. It is consistent with the docstring but I could not confirm it from this repo. Overall impact: a one-hour hang ending in a "blocked" outcome with a misleading reason, not data loss, so medium holds.

### A14. A migrated approval secret is written with default (umask) permissions; a new one is world-readable until chmod

- **Location:** `src/factory/claudo.py:319` · **category:** security · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** On a shared Linux/macOS host with umask 022, a factory upgraded from the legacy `.factory/approval-secret` layout ends up with ~/.local/state/ai-factory/<key>/approval-secret at mode 0644. Any local user can read it and sign checkpoint approvals. On creation there is a short window where the new secret is world-readable.
- **Suggested fix:** Create the file with os.open(path, O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_EXCL, 0o600) (or os.umask(0o077) around the write), mkdir the parent with mode 0o700, and chmod in migrate_legacy_secret too (also when the target exists).

**Evidence**

> migrate_legacy_secret: `target.write_text(legacy.read_text(...))` (claudo.py:319) with no chmod. load_or_create_secret then returns the existing file as-is (claudo.py:328-329), so it never fixes the mode. On creation, `path.write_text(secret...)` runs before `path.chmod(0o600)` (claudo.py:332-334), and the parent dir is created with default mode (claudo.py:318, 330).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it does what the finding says. In src/factory/claudo.py, migrate_legacy_secret creates the parent folder with a default-mode mkdir (line 318) and copies the secret with target.write_text (line 319), with no chmod afterwards. load_or_create_secret hands back any existing non-empty file as it is (lines 328-329), so it never fixes the mode of a migrated file. Its own chmod(0o600) only runs on the create path, after write_text (lines 332-334). The only caller is foreman.py:1423-1424, which runs the migration and then the load, and nothing else in the repo calls chmod or umask on this file. The tests at tests/test_claudo_build.py:345-358 never check file mode. So under umask 022 a migrated secret ends up 0644 inside a 0755 folder.  I lowered the severity because the exposure needs all of these together: a factory upgraded from the legacy layout, a shared multi-user host, and a home folder other users can traverse. macOS creates home folders as 0750 by default, which already blocks them, and many Linux distros do the same. The window on the create path is a few microseconds. The docstring (lines 325-327) already accepts that same-user code can read the secret, but other local users are outside that stated limit, so this is a real defect, not intended behavior.
2. I could not refute this finding. I read src/factory/claudo.py:310-338 and confirmed three things. First, migrate_legacy_secret calls mkdir(parents=True) at :318 with the default mode. It then writes the target at :319 with target.write_text(legacy.read_text(...)) and never calls chmod. Second, on the next call, load_or_create_secret (:324) returns any non-empty existing file as-is at :328-329. The chmod(0o600) at :334 only runs on the create path, so a migrated secret keeps its umask mode (0644 under umask 022). Third, on creation the file is written at :332 before the chmod at :334, so there is a short window where it is readable. The caller is foreman.py:1423-1424: it calls migrate_legacy_secret(root) and then load_or_create_secret(secret_path(root)), so the upgrade scenario does happen. The chmod(0o600) on the create path shows that owner-only access is the intent, and the docstring says the secret is kept per-user, so a 0644 file is a defect, not documented behavior. The tests (test_claudo_build.py:345-358) check only the content and that the legacy file is gone. They never check the mode. One limit on impact: whether another local user can actually reach ~/.local/state/ai-factory/<key>/ depends on the permissions of the home directory and ~/.local, which is often 0750/0700 on modern distros and on macOS. I keep the severity at medium for the migration path. The creation race on its own would be low.

### A15. Approval secret written world-readable on migration and before chmod

- **Location:** `src/factory/claudo.py:319` · **category:** secret-leakage · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** security
- **Failure scenario:** On a shared host with umask 022, a migrated `approval-secret` stays mode 0644 permanently, and a freshly created one is 0644 for a short window. Any local user can read the HMAC secret and sign Claudo checkpoint approvals.
- **Suggested fix:** Create the file with os.open(path, O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_WRONLY, 0o600) (or set umask 077), chmod after migration, and create the state directory with mode 0o700.

**Evidence**

> claudo.py:318-319 migrate_legacy_secret creates the target with `target.write_text(...)` and never chmods it. load_or_create_secret (claudo.py:328-329) later just reads the existing file. In the create path (claudo.py:332-334) the file is written with the default umask and only chmod'ed to 0600 afterwards. The state dir is created with default permissions (claudo.py:318, :330).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. I read src/factory/claudo.py lines 290-337 remotely and the code says what the finding claims.  - **Migration path (claudo.py:309-321):** `migrate_legacy_secret` runs `target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` at :318 and `target.write_text(...)` at :319. It never chmods the file, so the new file takes the process umask (0644 under umask 022). - **Migrated file is never fixed later:** `load_or_create_secret` (:323-337) returns early at :327-328 when the file exists and is non-empty, so it never reaches the chmod at :333. A migrated secret keeps umask permissions for good. - **Create path:** :330 mkdir, :331 `write_text`, :333 `chmod(0o600)`. The file is briefly umask-readable before the chmod. This window is small. - **State directory:** `state_dir` (:291-301) is `~/.local/state/ai-factory` by default, and the mkdir calls create it with default mode 0755.  Whether another local user can actually read the file depends on the home directory's permissions. On macOS home is 0700, so it is safe. On Linux distros where home is 0755, any local user can traverse into it, so the scenario is real on a shared Linux host.  The docstring at :325-326 discusses build agents running as the same user. It says nothing that makes a world-readable file intended behaviour.  The main defect is the missing chmod on the migration path. The pre-chmod window is a minor TOCTOU. Severity stays medium: this is the HMAC approval secret, but exploiting it needs a multi-user host whose home directory other users can traverse.
2. I could not refute this finding; the code says what it claims. In src/factory/claudo.py, migrate_legacy_secret creates the parent directory with a plain mkdir (line 318). It then writes the target with target.write_text(...) (line 319), which takes the process umask (0644 under umask 022), and never chmods the file. load_or_create_secret only chmods in the create path (lines 330-336). When the file already exists it simply reads it back (lines 328-329). So a migrated secret never gets 0600, even if the legacy file had it, because the copy goes through read_text/write_text. That migration path is live: foreman.py:1423-1424 calls migrate_legacy_secret(self.cfg.root) and then load_or_create_secret(secret_path(...)) on every secret load. The test (tests/test_claudo_build.py:345-358) checks only content and that the legacy file is gone, not permissions. In the create path, write-then-chmod (332 then 334) leaves a short window at umask permissions. The state directory under ~/.local/state/ai-factory/<hash>/ is created without mode=0o700 (lines 318 and 330). Exploitation still needs another local user who can traverse the home directory, which is not the default on hardened distros or macOS. That makes the impact conditional, but the defect is real. The docstring at lines 325-327 only accepts same-user agents as the residual risk, not other users.

### A16. Any open change on an app silences `factory drift` for that app, even before the change has branched

- **Location:** `src/factory/cli.py:288` · **category:** governance · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** App `orders` depends on flask (hold), so `factory drift` exits 1. Someone runs `factory change orders "typo" --idea x --kind feature` and never runs it. `factory drift` now prints 0 drifted and exits 0, and the CI drift check goes green while the shipped app still violates the radar.
- **Suggested fix:** Only treat an app as in flight when its change branch exists or is checked out; otherwise judge the base branch (e.g. check_project on a worktree of base) and keep failing.

**Evidence**

> cmd_drift: `flying = {i.target for i in items if i.change_open}` (288). scan_drift skips those apps entirely (drift.py:118-119). The exit code is `1 if drifted or pending else 0` (307), and `pending` only counts migrations (296). change_open is true from intake on (workitem.py:113-115), before begin_change ever switches branch.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The code says what the finding claims. src/factory/cli.py:288 builds `flying = {i.target for i in items if i.change_open}`. scan_drift (src/factory/drift.py, the `if item.slug in in_flight: continue` right after the shipped/app filter) skips those apps entirely. The return at cli.py:307 is `1 if drifted or pending else 0`, and `pending` (cli.py:296) counts only `kind == "migration"`. WorkItem.change_open (workitem.py, the property right after `step`) is `kind != "app" and not merged and status != "abandoned"`, so it is true from the moment the item is saved. Foreman.intake_change (foreman.py:263-287) only saves the item and writes idea.md. It never touches git. The `factory/<slug>` branch is created later (foreman.py:960-966, where base_branch/base_sha are set). The documented reason for skipping (README:121 and the scan_drift comment: "the folder shows the change branch, not the delivered state") assumes the branch exists, and before branching it does not hold: the folder is still the delivered base state, yet it is not judged. Concrete case: a shipped app violates the radar, so `drift` exits 1. Someone opens a feature or bugfix change with `intake_change` and never runs it. `drift` then counts the app as neither drifted nor compliant, `pending` is empty, and the exit code is 0, so the CI drift check goes green with the violation still shipped. tests/test_drift.py:187-197 covers only the migration case, which correctly keeps failing. Nothing guards the feature/bugfix case. Mitigating factor: the "N app(s) with a change in flight" line is still printed, but the exit code that CI uses goes green.
2. I couldn't refute it; the code confirms the finding. In src/factory/cli.py:288, `flying = {i.target for i in items if i.change_open}` covers every open change, whatever its kind. scan_drift then skips those apps (drift.py, `if item.slug in in_flight: continue`). The exit code is `1 if drifted or pending else 0`, and `pending` counts only open changes whose kind is "migration" (cli.py:296). change_open (workitem.py) is `kind != "app" and not merged and status != "abandoned"`, so it is already true right after Foreman.intake_change (foreman.py:263-288) saves the item. That function never checks for drift and never switches branch. So a feature change taken in but never run makes `factory drift` exit 0 for a drifted app. `factory drift --open` cannot open a migration either: the app is skipped and never reported as drifted, and open_migration (foreman.py:289-291) returns None anyway while any change is open on that app. The only partial counter is a printed "N app(s) with a change in flight" line, which does not change the exit code. The code's own comment at cli.py:294-295 says a scheduled drift check "must keep failing instead of going green behind a pending change", but it applies that rule only to migrations. tests/test_drift.py:187-193 covers only the in-flight migration case. This is a real CI gap, not intended behavior. Severity stays medium: the app is still listed as in flight, and the gap lasts only while the change is open.

### A17. `factory check` on a nonexistent or mistyped path prints PASS and exits 0

- **Location:** `src/factory/cli.py:328` · **category:** cli-argument-validation · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** In a CI job, `factory check ./servcie --maturity prod` (typo) or a checkout step that did not run: the output is 'PASS' with exit 0, so the radar gate goes green on code it never scanned.
- **Suggested fix:** In cmd_check, raise FactoryError when `target` is not a directory (and when a --doc file is missing, see the separate finding).

**Evidence**

> cli.py:328 `target = Path(args.path).resolve()` with no existence check, then cli.py:330 `report = check_project(target, radar, args.maturity, docs=docs)` and cli.py:338-339 print PASS and return 0 when nothing blocks. detect.py:61 `if (root / ".git").exists()` is False for a missing dir, so detect.py:71 `for p in sorted(root.rglob("*"))` runs; rglob on a missing directory yields nothing (verified: `list(Path('/nonexistent-xyz').rglob('*')) == []`). No findings, no violations.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this; the code does what the finding says. In cli.py:325-339, cmd_check resolves `Path(args.path)` at line 328 without checking that it exists or is a directory. It then calls check_project at line 330 and prints PASS and returns 0 at lines 338-339 when nothing blocks. Nothing downstream guards against a missing path either. guard.py:167 check_project iterates scan_project(root), defined at detect.py:181-185. scan_project uses iter_files (detect.py:57-73): for a missing directory the `.git` check at line 61 is False, so it falls through to `root.rglob("*")` at line 71. I ran that on a nonexistent path locally and it returned []. A missing --doc path is skipped silently too (`if not doc.is_file(): continue` in guard.py). The result is zero findings, zero violations, PASS and exit 0. A typo in the path or a checkout step that did not run turns the radar gate green without scanning any code. This is a concrete fail-open defect in a gate, not a style issue.
2. I could not refute it. In cli.py, cmd_check runs from line 325 to 340. Line 328 resolves `args.path` and never checks that it exists. Line 331 calls check_project, and lines 339-340 print PASS and return 0 when nothing is blocking. The argparse entry for `path` (cli.py:531) is a plain string with no validation. guard.py:167 check_project goes straight to scan_project(root) without checking the directory exists. detect.py iter_files only takes the git branch if `root/.git` exists; otherwise it falls back to `root.rglob('*')`, which I ran on a missing directory and it returned []. Docs that are missing are skipped quietly (`if not doc.is_file(): continue`). So a mistyped path gives no findings and no violations, then PASS with exit 0. This is a fail-open in a gate that IT runs and CI can call. Severity is a judgment call: it needs an operator typo or a misconfigured pipeline, so I rate it medium rather than high.

### A18. Keyword triage reads negations and French 'j'ai' as needed capabilities, which changes stack and template

- **Location:** `src/factory/design.py:18` · **category:** triage · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** Idea 'API only, no UI, nothing stored': frontend and database are detected. The fullstack-react golden path (React, Node, Vite) is scaffolded and a database is put in the stack for an API-only app. A French idea 'J'ai besoin d'une API de ping' gets the 'ai' capability, so an AI tech is pulled from the radar, and if that tech needs approval the POC design review is no longer skipped.
- **Suggested fix:** Drop matches in negated clauses (reuse guard.NEGATION within the sentence), and remove bare `ai` from the regex or require an English context. Alternatively, let the analyst confirm or reject the keyword capabilities instead of only adding to them.

**Evidence**

> design.py:18-21 `"frontend": r"\b(ui|interface|...)\b"`, `"database": r"\b(stor(e|es|ed)|...)\b"`, `"ai": r"\b(ai|llm|...)\b"` are applied by design.py:80-82 `re.search(pattern, idea, re.I)` with no negation handling. Verified: `re.search(r'\b(ai|llm)\b', "J'ai besoin d'un tableau", re.I)` matches. The LLM analyst (foreman.py:575-597) can only ADD capabilities, so it cannot remove these. pick_golden_path (design.py:384-398) then prefers the template covering the most of these optional needs: fullstack-react has `frontend` and priority 2.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it backs the finding. In design.py, lines 17-22 define the keyword regexes, and detect_capabilities (lines 78-83) adds every capability whose regex matches anywhere in the idea through `re.search(pattern, idea, re.I)`. Nothing in that path handles negation. I ran the regexes in Python. "J'ai besoin d'une API de ping" matches the `ai` alternative at span (2,4). "API only, no UI, nothing stored" matches `ui`, and `stor(e|es|ed)` matches "stored". In foreman.py, _do_triage (line 600) sets the keyword capabilities first, then appends the analyst's. _llm_capabilities returns only `added = [c for c in accepted if c not in found]`, so the analyst can add capabilities but never remove a false keyword hit. Those capabilities feed pick_golden_path at foreman.py:807-808 and choose_stack at 694/812. pick_golden_path (design.py:384-398) drops templates whose optional capabilities the idea does not need. A false `frontend` therefore stops filtering out the frontend template, and the max key then prefers it. One claim I did not check: that fullstack-react has priority 2. The core defect does not depend on it. Medium severity is reasonable, because the false matches change the stack and template that get scaffolded.
2. I could not refute this, and I confirmed it from the remote code.  - **No negation handling.** design.py:17-22 defines the keyword regexes and design.py:78-83 applies them with `re.search(pattern, idea, re.I)` without checking for negation. - **The regexes match the cases in the finding.** I ran them locally:   - "API only, no UI, nothing stored" matches both `frontend` (on "UI") and `database` (on "stored").   - "J'ai besoin d'une API de ping" matches `ai`, because the apostrophe is a non-word character, so `\b` matches before "ai". - **The analyst cannot remove capabilities.** At foreman.py `_do_triage`, `item.capabilities = detect_capabilities(item.idea)` runs first, then `item.capabilities += self._llm_capabilities(...)`. `_llm_capabilities` returns only `added = [c for c in accepted if c not in found]`, and its docstring says "Keywords stay the floor". Fallbacks elsewhere in foreman also call `detect_capabilities`. - **The wrong capabilities change the template.** design.py:378-398 `pick_golden_path` drops any template that brings an optional capability not in `needed_optional`, which is exactly what "no UI" should keep out. Once `frontend` is falsely detected, a frontend template such as golden_paths/fullstack-react becomes eligible and is preferred.  Corrections: - The failure scenario says "React, Node, Vite". The template actually has a Python `app/` plus a React/Vite `web/`, so "Node" is slightly off. - I did not open golden.toml to check the priority value of 2.  Neither point changes the defect. Medium severity holds: the stack and template change on plausible inputs, including French ideas.

### A19. Non-git builds skip build/, dist/, venv folders in radar and secrets gates, then commit them at approval

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:71` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** POC build where the agent writes dist/requirements.txt containing `flask`, or build/settings.py with an API key. The radar and secrets gates never see them. IT approval commits them into the app, and publish pushes the flask manifest (only the secret is caught, at publish).
- **Suggested fix:** Gate a non-git app by git-initialising it first (as _seal_approved_head will), or make the walk honour only DEPENDENCY_DIRS and .gitignore, like the git path.

**Evidence**

> iter_files falls back to a walk that drops any path containing SKIP_DIRS (`build`, `dist`, `venv`...) at any depth (19-29, 71-73). This walk is used whenever the app has no .git, which is every POV/POC single-agent build. _seal_approved_head then runs `git add -A` (foreman.py:483-486). The golden path .gitignore (golden_paths/python-fastapi/.gitignore:1-8) does not ignore build/ or dist/. README.md:36-37 presents G-3 as solved ('scans everything git tracks').

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. Every link in the chain checks out in the code. (1) detect.py:57-74: when the app has no .git, iter_files falls back to a plain rglob walk. That walk drops any path with a SKIP_DIRS part (`build`, `dist`, `venv`, ... at detect.py:19-29). The git branch is different: it skips only DEPENDENCY_DIRS in tracked files, and its own comment says "Tracked build/ and dist/ ARE scanned: that is the app's own output" (detect.py:44-46). (2) The radar scan and the secrets gate both read files through iter_files: scan_project at detect.py:183 and secrets_gate at gates.py:92-94. (3) The single-agent path (POV/POC, below claudo_build_from, foreman.py:920-927) never calls prepare_project before gating. Only _build_with_claudo does (foreman.py:1478). _seal_approved_head's docstring states it outright: "An app the single agent built outside git is committed here" (foreman.py:477-485). At approval, prepare_project runs `git init` and then `git add -A` (project.py:96-112), and commit_leftovers commits everything else (project.py:221). (4) Nothing excludes build/ or dist/ in that commit: golden_paths/python-fastapi/.gitignore:1-8 covers only .venv, caches, .env and work runtime files, and RUNTIME_IGNORES (project.py:44-48) covers only work/* runtime state. So un-gated build/, dist/ or venv/ content becomes the approved HEAD. The scenario's own caveat also holds: _publish_app runs secrets_in_history at publish time (foreman.py:1078), so a committed secret is caught there, but a forbidden dependency in dist/requirements.txt is not. README.md:36-37 says "In a git project it scans everything git tracks", which is accurate for git projects and silent on this non-git gap. I am keeping the severity at medium: the effect is real (a gate bypass, and un-gated content committed and published), but it is limited to POV/POC single-agent builds, and secrets are caught again at publish.
2. Confirmed from the code. detect.py:57-73: iter_files only uses git's tracked-file list when root/.git exists. Otherwise it walks every file but skips any path whose parts include SKIP_DIRS (19-29: build, dist, venv...). In the git branch only DEPENDENCY_DIRS are dropped from tracked files (detect.py:44-46 says tracked build/ and dist/ ARE scanned), so the non-git walk is deliberately broader in what it skips. Both gates go through iter_files: secrets_gate (gates.py:92-94) and radar_gate -> check_project -> scan_project (guard.py:58, detect.py:183). foreman.py:478-486 (_seal_approved_head) says outright that a single-agent app is built outside git. When gated_sha is empty it runs prepare_project (project.py:96-111: git init, then `git add -A` and the initial commit) and commit_leftovers (git add -A via commit_all). Every file is committed at that point, including build/, dist/ and venv/. The golden path .gitignore (lines 1-8) ignores .venv but not build/, dist/ or venv/. _clean_tree_gate returns 'not applicable' for a non-git app (foreman.py, the _clean_tree_gate body). Concrete case: on a POC single-agent build, build/settings.py holding an API key, or dist/requirements.txt naming a blocked package, passes the secrets and radar gates and is committed at IT approval. The secret is caught only later, by secrets_in_history at publish (foreman.py:1078). The blocked dependency is never caught. README.md:36-37 claims full git-tracked coverage only for git projects, so no documentation makes this intended for the non-git path. Severity medium stands: the agent has to write into those folders, and the publish-time history scan backstops the secrets half.

### A20. Radar guard silently ignores common dependency declarations

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:90` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** An agent adds `"optionalDependencies": {"angularjs": "1.8"}` to web/package.json, or `[tool.uv] dev-dependencies = ["flask"]`, or a Pipfile with `flask = "*"`. check_project reports nothing and the radar gate passes at MVP/prod.
- **Suggested fix:** Parse optionalDependencies and tool.uv dev-dependencies; treat any *.txt/*.in under a requirements/ dir as requirements; add Pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg, environment.yml to UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (APPROVAL).

**Evidence**

> _pyproject reads project.dependencies, optional-dependencies, dependency-groups and poetry only (90-106); `[tool.uv] dev-dependencies` is not read. _package_json reads dependencies/devDependencies/peerDependencies only (116-121); `optionalDependencies` is missed. Requirements files count only when the basename starts with 'requirements' (162), so requirements/base.txt or prod.in are ignored. Pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg and environment.yml are not in UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (131-141), so they raise neither a block nor an IT-review violation. README.md:34-36 promises that unanalysed ecosystems need IT review.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The code says what the finding claims. In src/factory/detect.py, _pyproject (90-106) reads only project.dependencies, optional-dependencies, dependency-groups and tool.poetry (deps, dev-deps, groups). It never reads [tool.uv] dev-dependencies. _package_json (117-122) reads only dependencies, devDependencies and peerDependencies, so optionalDependencies is skipped. scan_file (162) handles a requirements file only when the basename starts with "requirements" and ends with ".txt", so requirements/base.txt and *.in files fall through to `return []` (178). UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (133-141) does not list pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg or environment.yml, so those files produce no finding at all.  Only two paths in guard.check_project (src/factory/guard.py:177-203) create blind-spot violations: kind=="error" gives BLOCK and kind=="unanalysed" gives APPROVAL. An import whose name is not on the radar is dropped on purpose (199-200: "only declared deps count as unknown"). So a package that is declared only in Pipfile, [tool.uv] dev-dependencies or optionalDependencies, and is not on the radar, produces no violation, even if the code imports it. At prod, "not on radar" should block (README.md:29). Imports partly cover the gap only when the package's import name maps to a radar entry, and they never cover dev tooling that is not imported.  README.md:34-37 says unparseable manifests block and manifests the radar does not analyse need IT review. These files are neither, so the gate passes silently. This is a real gap in the gate, not a style issue. Medium severity is reasonable: it is a gap in a policy gate, the scenario needs a less common manifest form, and imports mitigate it partially.
2. I couldn't refute the gap; it is in the code. In src/factory/detect.py, _pyproject reads [project] dependencies, optional-dependencies, [dependency-groups] and tool.poetry only, never tool.uv dev-dependencies. _package_json loops over ("dependencies", "devDependencies", "peerDependencies") only, so optionalDependencies is missed. scan_file treats a file as a requirements manifest only when its basename starts with "requirements" and ends in ".txt", so prod.in and requirements/base.txt are skipped. Pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg and environment.yml fall through to `return []`. In guard.py check_project, only kind=="error" (BLOCK) and kind=="unanalysed" (APPROVAL) produce blind-spot violations, so these files produce nothing. That goes against the detect.py comment saying silent ignoring "would be a blind spot", and against the README's statement that a manifest it cannot read blocks or goes to IT review. The finding's example overstates the impact. Flask and angularjs are both on the radar with ring "hold". If the code actually imports flask, the import scanner finds it through radar.find and blocks it, so "reports nothing" holds only when the package is declared and never imported. The real bypass is a package that is not on the radar. check_project skips unknown imports ("only declared deps count as unknown"), so a non-radar package declared only in a Pipfile, setup.py or optionalDependencies and imported in code gets neither IT approval nor a block. Per the README matrix, "not on radar" must block at prod and needs IT approval at MVP. Medium severity stands for that path.

### A21. detect.py silently ignores several common Python dependency declarations (radar never sees them)

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:162` · **category:** detection · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** An app declares `angularjs`/`flask`-style hold deps only in `requirements/prod.txt`, or via `-r deps.txt`, or in Pipfile or setup.cfg, and never imports them under that name. `factory check` and the radar gate report PASS on a dependency the radar forbids. The module claims fail-closed behaviour for unreadable manifests (detect.py:155-156) but has these silent paths.
- **Suggested fix:** Scan `requirements/*.txt` and any file referenced by `-r`/`-c`, report `-e`/VCS lines as unanalysed findings, and add Pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg and environment.yml to UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (or parse them).

**Evidence**

> detect.py:162 only treats `name.startswith("requirements") and name.endswith(".txt")` as a manifest, so `requirements/base.txt` (name 'base.txt') is ignored. detect.py:113-114 drop every line starting with '-' (`-r common.txt`, `-e git+...#egg=flask`, `-c`) without a finding. UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (detect.py:133-141) lists go.mod, pom.xml and others but not Pipfile, setup.py/setup.cfg (install_requires) or environment.yml, so these are neither analysed nor flagged as a blind spot.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code I read remotely matches the finding. detect.py:162 (and _is_analysed_manifest, 144-147) only treats files whose basename starts with "requirements" and ends with ".txt" as requirements manifests. So requirements/prod.txt (basename prod.txt) or deps.txt is never parsed. detect.py:113-114 drops every line starting with "-" (-r, -c, -e git+...#egg=flask) and emits no finding for it. UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (133-141) holds go.mod, pom.xml, build.gradle(.kts), cargo.toml, gemfile and composer.json. It does not hold Pipfile, setup.cfg or environment.yml, so those files match no branch in scan_file and produce nothing. setup.py is caught only as a .py file at 172-174: its imports are scanned, but its install_requires strings are not. The comment at line 132 says "silently ignoring them would be a blind spot", and lines 155 and 167 claim fail-closed handling. Both show the silent paths go against the module's own stated intent, so this is not documented scope. One mitigation: a dependency that the code imports under the same name is still caught by the import scan. The scenario in the finding (declared but not imported under that name) therefore still produces a false PASS. Medium severity holds.
2. The code matches the finding, so I could not refute it. In src/factory/detect.py:162 (and :146), a file is treated as a requirements manifest only if its basename starts with "requirements" and ends with ".txt". So requirements/prod.txt (basename prod.txt) falls through and returns no finding. Lines 113-114 throw away every line starting with "-", so -r, -c and -e (git+...#egg=flask) entries vanish without a finding. UNANALYSED_MANIFESTS (lines 133-141) has go.mod, pom.xml, build.gradle(.kts), cargo.toml, gemfile and composer.json, but not Pipfile, setup.py, setup.cfg or environment.yml. Those files reach scan_file and get no manifest or unanalysed finding. The comment at line 132 says silently ignoring an ecosystem's manifest "would be a blind spot", and lines 155-156 and 167 show the module means to report manifests it cannot read. That makes these silent paths a gap against the module's own stated design, not intended scope. A code search for Pipfile and setup.cfg in the repo returned nothing, so nothing documents them as deliberately excluded. Dependencies that the app also imports in .py files are still caught by the import scan. The gap is only for dependencies declared but never imported under the same name. Medium severity is fair.

### A22. Docker multi-stage aliases and ARG images are judged as unknown technologies, blocking prod

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:168` · **category:** false-positive · **found:** low · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** A Dockerfile with `FROM python:3.12-slim AS base` then `FROM base`, or `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}`, makes the prod radar gate fail with 'base (not on radar)', and needs IT approval at pov to mvp.
- **Suggested fix:** Collect `AS <name>` aliases and skip FROM references to them; skip operands containing `$`.

**Evidence**

> Every FROM operand becomes an 'image' finding (34, 168-169). check_project only skips unknown *imports* (guard.py:199-200), and UNKNOWN_POLICY prod = BLOCK (radar.py:30).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code on main and the finding holds. In src/factory/detect.py, the regex at line 34 (`^\s*FROM\s+(?:--\S+\s+)*(\S+)`) captures only the first operand after FROM. Lines 168-169 then turn every match into a Finding(kind="image"), skipping only `scratch`. So `FROM base` produces the image finding "base". `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}` is passed through `_image_name` (lines 83-87) unchanged, giving "${BASE_IMAGE}". Nothing collects `AS` aliases or filters `$`. In src/factory/guard.py, `check_project` skips an unknown finding only when `f.kind == "import"` (lines 199-200), so these image findings reach `verdict(None, maturity)`. In src/factory/radar.py, `verdict` returns `UNKNOWN_POLICY[maturity]` when the tech is unknown (lines 112-114), and line 30 sets that to APPROVAL at pov/poc/mvp and BLOCK at prod. The tests do not cover this: tests/test_guard.py:27 uses a single-stage `FROM ... AS base` and never references the alias in a later FROM, and no test covers ARG substitution. A normal multi-stage or ARG-parameterised Dockerfile therefore triggers a spurious IT approval or a prod block. This is a false positive with a manual workaround, so low severity is right.
2. Confirmed from the code. In detect.py:34, _FROM captures the first non-flag operand of every FROM line. detect.py:168-169 emits an "image" Finding for each operand. The only one it skips is "scratch". It never collects `AS <name>` aliases, and it never filters operands that contain `$`. _image_name (detect.py:83) only strips the digest, the registry path and the tag, so `FROM base` comes out as "base" and `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}` comes out as "${BASE_IMAGE}". In guard.py, check_project skips a technology missing from the radar only when f.kind == "import" (the `if tech is None and f.kind == "import": continue` line). An image finding therefore goes to verdict(None, maturity). That returns UNKNOWN_POLICY[maturity] (radar.py:113-114), and UNKNOWN_POLICY sets "prod": BLOCK and "mvp": APPROVAL (radar.py:30). A grep of radar.toml for a "base"/"builder" entry found nothing, so radar.find("base") returns None. Result: a standard multi-stage Dockerfile, or one whose base image comes from an ARG, raises a spurious unknown-technology violation. That means APPROVAL at pov/poc/mvp and BLOCK at prod. I found no test or doc saying this is intended. The workaround of renaming the stage after a radar entry is not reasonable. Severity stays low to medium: this is a false positive that blocks the gate, not data loss. I could not check whether the golden-path Dockerfiles use multi-stage builds because of the GitHub API rate limit.

### A23. Multi-stage Dockerfile stage references are reported as unknown images (BLOCK at prod)

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:169` · **category:** false-positive · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** A prod app with a standard two-stage Dockerfile fails the radar gate with '[block] builder (not on radar)', and no IT exception can clear a BLOCK.
- **Suggested fix:** Collect `AS <name>` aliases and skip FROM tokens that equal an earlier stage name (and `${ARG}` references).

**Evidence**

> `_FROM = ^\s*FROM\s+(?:--\S+\s+)*(\S+)` captures the first token. Checked: 'FROM python:3.12 AS builder\nFROM builder' gives ['python:3.12', 'builder']. guard.py:198-203 records 'builder' as an unknown tech, which UNKNOWN_POLICY (radar.py:30) makes BLOCK at prod.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. detect.py:34 defines `_FROM = ^\s*FROM\s+(?:--\S+\s+)*(\S+)`. detect.py:169 turns every captured token except 'scratch' into an "image" Finding. The only transformation is `_image_name` (detect.py:83-87), which strips registry, tag and digest, so a FROM that names an earlier build stage (`FROM builder`) yields the Finding 'builder'. Nothing collects `AS <name>` aliases. In guard.py:198-203, only kind=="import" findings are skipped when unknown. For an image, `radar.find('builder')` returns None (radar.toml has no 'builder' entry), so `verdict(None, maturity)` (radar.py:113-114) returns UNKNOWN_POLICY, which is BLOCK at prod (radar.py:30). `GuardReport.blocking` (guard.py:143) treats BLOCK as blocking regardless of exceptions, so no IT exception clears it. The repo's own golden paths do not trigger it: fullstack-react uses `FROM node:24-slim AS web` then `FROM python:3.12-slim`, presumably copying across with `COPY --from`. But the standard `FROM base AS x` / `FROM x` pattern in a user app does produce a false BLOCK at prod and an APPROVAL at lower rungs. Severity medium holds: it is a false-positive gate failure with no exception path, and the workaround is to restructure the Dockerfile.
2. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. The regex at src/factory/detect.py:34 (`^\s*FROM\s+(?:--\S+\s+)*(\S+)`) takes the first token after every FROM, and nothing records `AS <name>` aliases. detect.py:169 drops only `scratch`. I ran the regex locally on a two-stage Dockerfile and got ['python:3.12', 'builder']. In guard.py:198-203, `radar.find('builder')` returns None. Findings of kind 'image' skip the `continue`, which applies only to kind 'import' (guard.py:199-200), so the guard records them. `verdict(None, 'prod')` returns UNKNOWN_POLICY['prod'] = BLOCK (radar.py:30, 113-114). `blocking()` at guard.py:143 treats BLOCK as blocking whatever the exceptions set contains, so no IT exception can clear it. The only Dockerfile test (tests/test_guard.py:27) uses a single stage, so multi-stage builds are not covered. A `${ARG}` image reference is also kept as written (`${BASE}`), so it would show up as an unknown tech too. Severity medium: this is a false-positive BLOCK on a common, legitimate Dockerfile pattern at prod maturity.

### A24. Dockerfile `FROM <stage>` and `FROM ${ARG}` are reported as unknown base images (false violations at prod)

- **Location:** `src/factory/detect.py:169` · **category:** detection · **found:** low · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** A Dockerfile with `FROM python:3.12-slim AS base` and `FROM base AS runtime` gives the finding 'base', which is not on the tech radar: needs approval at MVP and blocks at prod. `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}` likewise yields '${BASE_IMAGE}'.
- **Suggested fix:** Collect `AS <name>` aliases while scanning and skip FROM operands equal to a prior stage name. Report `${...}` as an unanalysed finding instead.

**Evidence**

> detect.py:34 `_FROM = re.compile(r"^\s*FROM\s+(?:--\S+\s+)*(\S+)", ...)` captures every FROM operand, and detect.py:169 emits each as kind 'image', excluding only 'scratch'. Stage names declared with `AS name` are not tracked. In guard.py:198-203 an unknown name with kind 'image' (not 'import') is recorded, and UNKNOWN_POLICY makes it BLOCK at prod.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. I read every cited file remotely, and the code does what the finding says. In detect.py:34, `_FROM` captures every FROM operand, and nothing in it collects `AS <name>` aliases. In detect.py:169, every captured operand except `scratch` becomes a `Finding(_image_name(m), rel, "image")`. `_image_name` (detect.py:83-87) only strips a digest, the registry path and the tag, so `FROM base AS runtime` yields the name `base` and `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}` yields `${BASE_IMAGE}`. In guard.py:198-203, an unknown name is skipped only when its kind is `import`. An unknown `image` goes through `verdict(None, maturity)`, which returns UNKNOWN_POLICY (radar.py:30, 113-114): APPROVAL at pov/poc/mvp and BLOCK at prod. `radar.find` (radar.py:98-100) is an exact normalized lookup, so `base` gets no match unless the radar happens to list that alias. I found no code that guards against this. The only Dockerfile test (tests/test_guard.py:27) uses a single FROM with an AS alias, so a multi-stage reference is never exercised. The severity is low: the result is a false-positive APPROVAL or BLOCK that a user can work around, not a security bypass.
2. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. detect.py:34 `_FROM` captures every FROM operand. detect.py:169 emits `Finding(_image_name(m), rel, "image")` for each one and filters out only `scratch`. Nothing collects `AS <name>` stage aliases. _image_name (detect.py:83-87) only splits on `@`, `/` and `:`. So `FROM base AS runtime` yields the image name 'base', and `FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}` yields '${BASE_IMAGE}' unchanged. In guard.py (around lines 197-203), only kind 'import' is skipped when radar.find returns None. An 'image' finding falls through to `verdict(None, maturity)`. radar.find (radar.py:98-100) is an exact normalized lookup, and verdict (radar.py:110-114) returns UNKNOWN_POLICY. UNKNOWN_POLICY (radar.py:30) is {"pov": APPROVAL, "poc": APPROVAL, "mvp": APPROVAL, "prod": BLOCK}. The stage name is therefore recorded as an APPROVAL violation below prod and a BLOCK at prod. Multi-stage Dockerfiles are common, so this false BLOCK at prod is plausible in practice. I rate it medium rather than low, because it makes the prod gate fail on a correct project.

### A25. Exceptions granted on a change item are never carried to the app: drift reports a false violation after merge

- **Location:** `src/factory/drift.py:126` · **category:** exception-logic · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** A feature change at MVP adds Django (trial = APPROVAL). IT approves the change's design_review, so 'django' is excepted on the change item, the gates pass and it is merged. `factory drift` then judges the app item, which has no 'django' exception, so the app is reported drifted and a migration item is generated.
- **Suggested fix:** On merge, copy the change's exception keys and terms into the target app item, or have scan_drift union the active exceptions of merged changes targeting the app.

**Evidence**

> drift.py:116 judges only `item.kind == "app"` and drift.py:126 uses `report.blocking(item.active_exceptions(today))` of the APP item. foreman.py:408-409 and 534-535 write exceptions on whichever item is at design review / allow, including change items, and no code path copies them to the target app item (grep of it_exceptions across foreman/cli/delivery).

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from the code. drift.py:116 skips every non-app item, and drift.py:126 filters violations only through the APP item's `active_exceptions(today)`. The build gate for a change uses the CHANGE item's exceptions: in foreman.py `_do_gate`, `exceptions=item.active_exceptions(self.today())` runs against `app_dir(item)`, which for a change is the target app's folder (foreman.py:891). `allow` (foreman.py ~522-538) appends the key to whichever item it is given, change items included. `merge` (foreman.py 988-1022) and `_move_target_head` (1199-1206) set only `merged` and `approved_head`; nothing copies `it_exceptions` or `exception_terms` to the target. `it_exceptions` is written only at foreman.py:409, 535 and 555 (the last one clears it on promote), and cli.py and delivery.py only read it. Scenario: at MVP, IT runs `factory allow <change> django`. The change's gate passes because it honors the change's exception, IT merges, and `factory drift` then checks the app folder, finds django at APPROVAL, sees no django exception on the app item and reports the app drifted. `drift --open` would then open a migration. README:243 says drift honors "the exceptions IT granted per app", so this is not documented behavior for exceptions granted on a change. One detail of the finding is imprecise: a change's design.md comes from `render_change_design` on the existing stack (foreman.py:663-686), so a new tech may never appear at design review. The `allow` path reaches the same defect, though. IT can work around it by also running `allow` on the app item, so medium severity holds.
2. The finding holds; I read the code remotely. At drift.py:115-126, scan_drift skips everything except kind=="app" items. It runs check_project on apps_dir/item.slug, then report.blocking(item.active_exceptions(today)) using only the app item's exceptions. guard.py:138-144 counts an APPROVAL violation as blocking unless its key is in that exception set.  Exceptions are written onto whichever item is under review: - In Foreman.approve at design_review (foreman.py ~407-411), the granted keys go into the change item's own it_exceptions. - `allow` (foreman.py ~534-536) does the same. - The change's build gate (foreman.py:1706) uses the change item's exceptions, so the change passes.  Nothing moves those exceptions onto the target app: - Merge (foreman.py:988-1022) and sync (1272-1295) only call _move_target_head (1246-1253), which sets target.approved_head and nothing else. - A grep of it_exceptions, exception_terms and active_exceptions across cli, delivery, project, gates, guard and importer finds no copy. cli.py only reads the field (lines 70, 74, 222).  The change inherits the app's maturity (intake_change, foreman.py:279). Per radar.py:25, a trial tech at MVP gives APPROVAL. So once a change approved with a 'django' exception is merged, the app folder uses django and the app item has no exception for it. scan_drift then reports the app as drifted, and open_migration (foreman.py:289) would open a migration item. This contradicts README.md:242-243, which says drift honors the exceptions IT granted. No test covers a change-granted exception surviving into drift.  Medium severity is fair: the result is a false drift report and an unwanted migration item, not a security hole.

### A26. One failing issue comment aborts the whole inbox: new items are never printed and later items are never reported, on every run

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:224` · **category:** error-handling · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** gap3-inbox-issue-to-workitem
- **Failure scenario:** An admin deletes (or transfers out of reach) issue #5 that was imported earlier. Every later `factory inbox`: new issues are imported and saved, then `gh issue comment` on #5 fails (`Could not resolve to an issue`), the command exits 2 without listing the new slugs, and every item created after #5 never gets a progress comment again. Next run the new issues are already `known` (foreman.py:170,177), so their slugs are never printed to the operator either.
- **Suggested fix:** Catch DeliveryError per item in report_issues, collect it as a warning (and maybe mark the item so it is not retried forever), and continue; have inbox return the new items even when some reports failed.

**Evidence**

> report_issues raises on the first DeliveryError: `except DeliveryError as e: raise FactoryError(str(e)) from e` (foreman.py:221-224). inbox() calls it after the imports are saved (foreman.py:191-193) and only returns afterwards (194), so cmd_inbox never reaches its print loop (cli.py:176-178); main prints `error:` and exits 2 (cli.py:559-562). Items are iterated in created order (workitem.py:175).

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding holds up in the code. Its line numbers are off, though. On main, foreman.py:202 shows inbox() calling self.report_issues() once all new items are saved (loop at 186-201), and it returns only at 203.  report_issues (foreman.py:220-238) loops over store.all(), which workitem.py:171-175 sorts by created. On the first DeliveryError it re-raises with `except DeliveryError as e: raise FactoryError(str(e)) from e` (foreman.py:232-233, not 221-224 as the finding says). Nothing catches it per item.  GhCli.comment_issue (delivery.py:136-137) raises DeliveryError on any non-zero gh exit (delivery.py:63-66).  cmd_inbox (cli.py) calls f.inbox() before its print loop. So the new slugs are never printed, and main catches FactoryError, prints `error:` and returns 2.  The failed item's issue_reported is never updated, so its state stays different on every later run and the failure repeats. Items sorted after it are never commented on.  On the next run, new items already count as known through issue_url (foreman.py:186), so their slugs are never shown again.  tests/test_inbox.py only covers report_issues on the success path (lines 81, 84, 118). No test shows the abort is intended. The trigger needs the failing item's stage or status to have changed since its last comment, which a normal stage transition after the issue is deleted provides. Medium severity is fair.
2. The behavior is confirmed from the code, but the line numbers are wrong. On current main, report_issues is at src/factory/foreman.py:220-238. Its loop over store.all() (line 224, created order per workitem.py:175) re-raises on the first DeliveryError (`raise FactoryError(str(e)) from e` at foreman.py:233) before issue_reported is updated or the item is saved (234-236). inbox() saves each new item (line 200), then calls self.report_issues() at line 202 before returning at line 203. So the exception skips the return, and cmd_inbox never reaches its print loop (cli.py, `new, skipped = f.inbox()` followed by the for loop). main catches the FactoryError, prints `error:` and returns 2. On the next run the issue URLs are already in `known` (foreman.py ~177, issue_url set and saved), so the new slugs are never printed. comment_issue is a plain `gh issue comment` (delivery.py:136-137), so a deleted, transferred or locked issue raises every time. The failure only happens once that item's stage or status differs from issue_reported (line 227-228), which is normal as an item progresses. From then on every inbox run fails and later items are never reported. No test covers or documents this as intended (tests/test_inbox.py only checks success counts). The finding's cited lines (224, 191-194) are off by about 9 lines: the raise is at 233, the call at 202 and the return at 203.

### A27. Abandoned changes can be resumed by run/approve, giving two open changes on one app

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:300` · **category:** state-machine · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** Change A is abandoned at spec_review and change B is opened on the same app. `factory approve A --as business` goes through: A gets status waiting, then design and plan. At A's build, begin_change raises 'the app is on factory/B', or A branches first and B fails later. The 'one open change per app' rule is broken.
- **Suggested fix:** In run/approve/reject/allow, refuse items whose status is 'abandoned' (and 'shipped' for checkpoint decisions).

**Evidence**

> run() (300-321) only special-cases status 'blocked'. For an 'abandoned' item at a checkpoint it sets status='waiting' (319). _require_checkpoint (393-400) never checks status. change_open (workitem.py:113-115) becomes true again, but abandon has already freed the app for intake_change (272-274).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it does what the finding says. abandon() (foreman.py:1274-1300) sets status="abandoned" and leaves item.stage unchanged. It does not check the stage, so a change sitting at spec_review can be abandoned. change_open (workitem.py:113-115) becomes false, which is what lets intake_change (foreman.py:270-272) open change B on the same app. Nothing stops A from being resumed afterwards. _require_checkpoint (foreman.py:393-400) looks only at the role and step.kind, never at status. approve() (foreman.py:402-456) calls _advance, which sets status="active" (338), and then calls run(). run() (300-330) only special-cases "blocked", and on reaching a checkpoint it sets status="waiting" (319). Even a plain `factory run A` would turn "abandoned" into "waiting". reject() (488-500) likewise sets status="active", and the CLI wrappers (cli.py cmd_run/cmd_approve/cmd_reject) add no guard. Once A is out of "abandoned", change_open is true again, so the app has two open changes. When A reaches build, begin_change (project.py:249-270) raises "the app is on factory/<B>", or A branches first and B fails the same way later. No test covers approving or running after an abandon. The only post-abandon guard I found is in sync, at foreman.py:1230. Medium is the right severity: it needs a human to act on an abandoned item, but it breaks the one-change-per-app rule.
2. I could not refute it; the code supports the finding. abandon() (foreman.py:1274-1299) sets status="abandoned" but leaves item.stage alone. intake_change (foreman.py:272-274) treats the app as free because change_open (workitem.py:113-115) is false while status=="abandoned". Neither approve (402) nor _require_checkpoint (393-400) checks status: they only check that the role is valid, that step.kind is "checkpoint" and that the role matches. So an item abandoned at spec_review still passes both. approve then advances the item and calls self.run(item) (around line 456). In run() (300-321), the only status special case is "blocked" (310-312). Automatic stages run, and at the next checkpoint the status becomes "waiting" (319). That makes change_open true again, alongside the second change B that was opened in the meantime. When A reaches build, begin_change (project.py:249-270) creates factory/A: if the app is still on main it branches, and if B already holds the app on factory/B it raises "the app is on factory/B". Either way two open changes now exist on one app, which breaks the guard at foreman.py:273. cli.py cmd_run/cmd_approve (194-205) pass the loaded item straight through with no status check. The tests (test_change_flow.py:300-344, test_delivery.py:497-528) only check abandon's own preconditions. The only abandoned-status guard is sync's at foreman.py:1230. Severity: medium. The scenario needs a deliberate human approve or run on an item that was abandoned, and four-eyes may further limit who can do it, but nothing in the code blocks it.

### A28. run()/approve() resurrect an abandoned change

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:309` · **category:** state-machine · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** foreman
- **Failure scenario:** A change abandoned at plan_review is approved (`factory approve X --as owner`) or run. Its status becomes waiting/active, so change_open is True again: intake_change for that app is refused ('already has an open change'). At build, begin_change re-creates factory/X from the current base (abandon_change had deleted it). A change abandoned at stage 'shipped' becomes status 'shipped' after `factory run`.
- **Suggested fix:** Raise FactoryError early in run(), approve(), reject(), merge() and publish() when item.status == "abandoned".

**Evidence**

> run() special-cases only status 'blocked' (foreman.py:309-311). It never refuses 'abandoned', and the loop sets status to 'waiting' at a checkpoint (319) or 'shipped' at the terminal (316). _require_checkpoint (393-400) checks the stage, not the status. change_open is `status != "abandoned"` (workitem.py:113-115). sync() is the only entry point that guards it (foreman.py:1230).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it.  `abandon()` in foreman.py (around lines 1274-1298) does three things: it deletes the branch through `abandon_change`, sets `item.status = "abandoned"` and saves. It does not change `item.stage`.  `run()` (foreman.py:300-348) has no check for abandoned items. Its only status special case is 'blocked' (309-311). After that the loop sets status to 'shipped' at a terminal step (315-316) or to 'waiting' at a checkpoint (318-319).  `approve()` (402+) first calls `_require_checkpoint` (393-400), which checks only the step kind and the role, never the status. It ends with `self._advance(item)`, which sets `status = "active"`, and then `return self.run(item)`.  `cmd_run` and `cmd_approve` in cli.py load the item and call `run()` or `approve()` directly, with no status guard either. The only status guard I found is in `sync()` at foreman.py:1230.  `change_open` (workitem.py:113-115) is `kind != "app" and not merged and status != "abandoned"`. So an item resurrected this way counts as an open change again and blocks a new intake for its target. The `open_migration` idempotence check (around foreman.py:290) is blocked the same way.  Concrete case: a change is abandoned at plan_review, then someone runs `factory approve X --as owner`. Status goes to active, the build stage runs, and the factory/X branch that `abandon_change` deleted comes back.  I lowered the severity to medium. It needs a human to approve or run an item they (or another IT/owner) had just abandoned, so it is an operator mistake with no automatic trigger. It still breaks the abandon guarantee ("freeing the app") and rebuilds a deleted branch.  I did not finish checking the tests for abandon coverage: the remote grep over the test files timed out.
2. I could not refute it; the code confirms the finding. In foreman.py, run() (lines 300-347) only special-cases status "blocked" (309-311). It never checks for "abandoned". Its loop sets status "shipped" at a terminal step (315-316), "waiting" at a checkpoint (317-319), and "active" through _advance (357). abandon() (1274-1300) sets item.status = "abandoned" but leaves item.stage unchanged. approve() (402+) calls _require_checkpoint (393-400), which looks at the step kind and role, not the status. It then calls _advance and returns self.run(item) (453-455). So a change abandoned at plan_review passes every check and moves on to build. There, _do_build_change calls begin_change(app, item.slug) (957) and re-creates the branch that abandon_change had deleted. Once the status is no longer "abandoned", workitem.change_open (113-115) becomes True again, and that blocks open_migration (291) and the open-change check on intake. The CLI adds no guard either: cmd_run and cmd_approve (cli.py ~195-206) pass f.store.load(slug) straight through. The only status guard I found is sync() (foreman.py:1230). tests/test_change_flow.py:300-344 tests abandon itself but never runs or approves an abandoned item. One correction on severity: it takes a deliberate human command on an abandoned slug, and the history keeps the "abandoned" log entry. It can still revive a branch and block the app for new changes, so medium rather than high.

### A29. A human rejection reason is overwritten by the lint/gate failure text and lost for the next attempt

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:343` · **category:** correctness · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** gap4-llm-artifact-retry-loops
- **Failure scenario:** The business rejects spec_review with 'drop the export feature'. The agent fails speclint 3 times, so the item blocks. On rerun the spec prompt carries only the lint errors, and 'drop the export feature' never reaches the agent again. A spec that ignores the rejection reaches spec_review.
- **Suggested fix:** Keep the human reason in its own field (e.g. item.rejection) and build the prompt from rejection + latest machine feedback, or append the detail instead of replacing it.

**Evidence**

> reject() sets `item.feedback = reason` (foreman.py:506). _do_spec/_do_plan seed retries with item.feedback (654, 789). On exhaustion run() does `item.feedback = detail` (343), replacing the reason with 'spec failed the structural lint...' / 'plan rejected by Claudo plan-lint...'. The next `factory run` starts with `feedback = item.feedback` (630, 753), which now holds only the lint errors. The build path is affected the same way: build_prompt(item, forbidden, item.feedback) at 1376 gets the gate report after the first failed gate, no longer IT's ship_review reason.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it. The code I read confirms the finding. reject() stores the human reason only in item.feedback (foreman.py:506, after claudo_rejection at :504, which is only for ship_review with a Claudo checkpoint). _do_spec and _do_plan start from item.feedback (:630, :753) and use it to build each retry within one run (:654, :789). When retries run out, run() overwrites the field with the handler's failure text (`item.feedback = detail`, :343). That text starts with 'spec failed the structural lint ...' or 'plan rejected by Claudo plan-lint ...'. No other field keeps the reason. The prompts (templates.py:140-141 spec_prompt, :187 plan, :366 change_spec_prompt) take only that one `feedback` string and include no log or history. So on the next `factory run`, the agent sees only the lint errors, and spec_prompt even labels them 'The business rejected the previous draft'. The human reason never reaches the agent again. The build path has the same overwrite: build_prompt(item, forbidden, item.feedback) at :1376 and :982 frames feedback as gate failures (templates.py:217). After the first failed gate, :343 replaces the IT reason. That path matters only in the non-Claudo route; the Claudo route keeps claudo_rejection. The trigger needs a rejection followed by lint or gate exhaustion, which is plausible, so medium severity is reasonable.
2. I confirmed this from the code. reject() writes the human reason with `item.feedback = reason` (src/factory/foreman.py:506). _do_spec starts from `feedback, tries = item.feedback, 0` (630). Each lint retry inside the same run rebuilds the feedback from `item.feedback` (654), so the reason survives the in-run retries. Once spec_lint_retries runs out, the method returns `False, "spec failed the structural lint ..."` (659-660). run() then replaces the reason with `item.feedback = detail` (343) and marks the item blocked (353-354). The next `factory run` turns blocked back into active (318-320), and the item is still at the spec stage. _do_spec reads item.feedback again (630), which now holds only the lint text. spec_prompt then presents that text as "The business rejected the previous draft. Their feedback:" (templates.py:141). Nothing else brings the reason back: the log line at 505 goes into history, which the prompt never reads, and claudo_rejection (504) is only set for ship_review with Claudo. The plan path does the same at 753/789/781. The build/gate part of the finding is weaker: after a gate failure the gate report is arguably what the agent should act on, and the Claudo path keeps the reason separately in claudo_rejection. The spec/plan scenario alone is enough to confirm the defect: a business rejection reason silently disappears after the item blocks on lint and is rerun.

### A30. Exceptions inside run()/approve() discard all in-memory progress (approvals, cost, attempts); several are not caught by the CLI

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:347` · **category:** error-handling · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** foreman
- **Failure scenario:** Business approves spec_review. run() continues into design and plan, and Claudo plan-lint times out after 60 s with an EngineError. The approval, design hashes and log are never saved, so the item is back at spec_review/waiting and the business must approve again. Second case: a single-agent change build succeeds (cost added at 980), then commit_all raises ProjectError. The billed cost_usd and build_attempts are lost and the user sees a raw traceback.
- **Suggested fix:** Wrap the handler call in run() with try/except that logs, sets status=blocked with the error as feedback, and always saves (try/finally). Save in approve() before calling run(). Convert ProjectError/TimeoutExpired/TOMLDecodeError into FactoryError.

**Evidence**

> run() saves only at the end (347). approve() appends the approval (450-453) and then calls self.run(item) (456) without saving first. Uncaught raisers inside stages include: EngineError from lint_plan timeout (claudo.py:128-129, via _lint 730); subprocess.TimeoutExpired from engine.trajectory (claudo.py:219-229, via _trajectory_gate 1637); ProjectError from commit_all (965, 985), commit_paths (1330), prepare_project (1478) and commit_leftovers_split (1439); TOMLDecodeError (1687). cli.py:559 catches only ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError and KeyError.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code remotely and it confirms the finding. In run(), foreman.py:300-348 has no try/finally. The only save on the normal path is at 347, and the only except is around _shipped_target at 305. approve() appends the approval and logs it (450-453), calls _advance (455), then calls self.run(item) (456) without saving first, so an exception raised by any handler loses the approval, the log, cost_usd and build_attempts (incremented at 331-332).
