One mitigating point: the operator explicitly opted into host execution, so the main harm is a false audit trail and PR provenance, plus an unrecorded unsandboxed run. Severity medium is reasonable.

### A49. Failed Claudo rework after an IT rejection silently falls back to the single-agent builder

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:1485` · **category:** retry · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** foreman
- **Failure scenario:** IT rejects at ship_review and the Claudo rework times out. The item is blocked. On `factory run` the rework goes to the single agent instead of Claudo, while Claudo's state still has the reopened tasks pending. At the next ship approval, _finalize_with_claudo signs the old CP and the final run (stop_at_checkpoint=False) executes those reopened tasks after the approval, so post_approval_changes blocks the ship. The audited per-task build is bypassed.
- **Suggested fix:** Keep claudo_rejection (or a 'rework pending' flag) until run_build returns ok, or clear claudo_cp when the rejection is handed to Claudo, so a retry goes back through Claudo.

**Evidence**

> _build_with_claudo clears `item.claudo_rejection = {}` (1485) and saves (1496) before engine.run_build. When the outcome is 'failed'/'timeout' it returns False (1514), but claudo_cp still holds the old checkpoint (reject() never clears it, 494-495). The next build tests `self._uses_claudo(item) and (not item.claudo_cp or item.claudo_rejection)` (1363), which is now False.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding: the code I read confirms it. In src/factory/foreman.py, reject() (494-495) sets claudo_rejection only when claudo_cp is set, and it never clears claudo_cp. _build_with_claudo hands the rejection to Claudo (reject_checkpoint at 1484) and then clears it at 1485. It saves the item at 1496 before run_build. On any outcome other than checkpoint or done (failed or timeout), it returns False at 1514 without restoring claudo_rejection or clearing claudo_cp. The only places that set claudo_cp are 1505 (checkpoint) and 1512 (done). run() (300-346) responds to a failed build step by setting status=blocked and stopping, with no automatic retry, because the retry at 335-342 only covers a failed gate. The next `factory run` resumes at the build stage. There, _do_build at 1363 (and _do_build_change at 969) tests `not item.claudo_cp or item.claudo_rejection`, which is now False, so the build goes to the single-agent builder. The comment at 1360-1362 says a single-agent fix is intended only after a clean Claudo run hits a factory gate failure, and that "an IT rejection goes back through Claudo". This path contradicts that stated intent. At the next ship approval, line 435 still holds (claudo_cp is set and not consumed), so _finalize_with_claudo signs the old, already-rejected checkpoint (1548) and runs Claudo with stop_at_checkpoint=False (1550-1556). That run either fails, leaving the item blocked, or executes the reopened tasks after approval, which post_approval_changes (1568) then blocks. Either way the item cannot ship cleanly, and the audited rework was bypassed. I did not read Claudo's engine code, so I cannot say exactly how the final run behaves on a rejected checkpoint. The factory-side defect does not depend on that. Medium severity is reasonable: it needs a Claudo failure or timeout during a rework, and the downstream guard blocks the ship instead of letting unreviewed code through.
2. I read the code and it behaves as the finding says. In src/factory/foreman.py, reject() (lines 494-495) sets claudo_rejection and leaves claudo_cp alone. _build_with_claudo then sets claudo_rejection to {} at line 1485, after reject_checkpoint has already been called on the Claudo engine, and saves the item at line 1496, before run_build runs. If the outcome is neither 'checkpoint' nor 'done' (failed or timeout), the method returns False at line 1514 without touching claudo_cp, so the old checkpoint id is still there. In run() (lines 331-345), a failed build step is set to status 'blocked'; it is not retried, because only a failed gate step is retried. When the item is built again, the routing checks in _do_build (line 1363) and _do_build_change (line 969) both test `not item.claudo_cp or item.claudo_rejection`. Both conditions are now False, so the rework goes to the single-agent builder while Claudo's state still has that checkpoint rejected or reopened. At the next ship approval, line 435 (claudo_cp set, not consumed) calls _finalize_with_claudo. That signs the stale checkpoint (line 1548) and runs Claudo with stop_at_checkpoint=False, so Claudo can still run the reopened tasks; post_approval_changes (line 1568) would then block the ship. Nothing else that I read guards against this, and no comment says it is intended: the comment at 1360-1362 says an IT rejection goes back through Claudo. The finding stands as a real defect on the retry path. Medium severity is fair, because it needs a failed or timed-out rework after an IT rejection, and the outcome is a blocked ship or a bypassed audit rather than silent corruption.

### A50. Final Claudo run failing after the checkpoint was consumed leaves claudo_cp_consumed False, so a later rejection is not reopened

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:1562` · **category:** partial-failure · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** foreman
- **Failure scenario:** IT approves. Claudo verifies the token, consumes CP-1, then fails a post-checkpoint node, so the item is blocked with consumed=False. HEAD moved, so re-approve is refused by _check_gated_head and IT rejects. reject_checkpoint writes CP-1.rejected, but CP-1 is no longer pending in state.json, so Claudo can end 'done' without the rework. The rejection reason never reaches an agent and the same code goes back to the gates.
- **Suggested fix:** Treat a failed or timed-out final run as possibly consumed: on rejection, always call reopen_checkpoint (it is a no-op when cp is not in state, claudo.py:284-285).

**Evidence**

> _finalize_with_claudo: `if res.outcome != "done": return "blocked", ...` (1562-1563), and only then `item.claudo_cp_consumed = True` (1566). A 'failed'/'timeout' final run can happen after Claudo has verified the signed token and passed the CP. On rejection, _build_with_claudo reopens the checkpoint only `if item.claudo_cp_consumed` (1481-1483).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it matches the finding. In src/factory/foreman.py, _finalize_with_claudo sets `item.claudo_cp_consumed = True` (line 1566) only after the `if res.outcome != "done": return "blocked", ...` early return at lines 1562-1563. A final run that fails or times out after Claudo has verified the signed token therefore leaves consumed=False.  The blocked path in approve (lines 436-441) keeps the stage at ship_review and keeps claudo_cp set. reject (lines 488-499) only goes through _require_checkpoint, which checks the stage kind and not the status, so IT can still reject the blocked item. reject then records claudo_rejection with that cp.  On the rebuild, _build_with_claudo reopens the checkpoint only when `item.claudo_cp_consumed` is true (lines 1481-1483). Otherwise it just writes `<cp>.rejected` (line 1484). The docstring of ClaudoEngine.reopen_checkpoint (claudo.py:277-289) says a consumed checkpoint must be removed from .runs/state.json "so the next run re-enters it (and finds a `.rejected` file there)". The factory's own design therefore expects the .rejected file to be ignored for a checkpoint already recorded in state.json.  A failed final run can happen after the checkpoint was consumed. tests/test_claudo_build.py:153-155 scripts exactly this: CP-1, then a final run that fails with "T2 failed", a post-checkpoint task, leaving the item blocked at ship_review. Nothing in that test or elsewhere reopens the checkpoint on a later rejection.  The fix is cheap: reopen_checkpoint is already a no-op when the cp is not in the state (claudo.py:284-285). Medium severity stands: rework is silently skipped and the code goes back to the gates. Not critical, because the gates and IT review still run again.
2. I could not refute this from the code, so the finding stands at medium. foreman.py:1562-1563 returns "blocked" on any outcome other than "done", and that happens before item.claudo_cp_consumed = True at 1566. run_build (claudo.py:132-186) returns "failed" or "timeout" without saying how far Claudo got. In this final run stop_at_checkpoint=False, so a failure after Claudo verified the token and passed the checkpoint is a possible state. After the block the item stays at ship_review, a checkpoint stage, so reject() is still allowed (393-400). reject() at 488-496 sets claudo_rejection. Then _build_with_claudo at 1479-1485 calls reopen_checkpoint only when claudo_cp_consumed is true. Here it is false, so the code only writes CP.rejected. The docstring of reopen_checkpoint (claudo.py:277-288) says a consumed checkpoint stays in .runs/state.json and must be removed before the next run re-enters it and finds the .rejected file. Without that removal, the rejection reason never reaches the agents. Nothing elsewhere sets the flag after a failed run, so nothing guards this path. The fix is cheap: reopen_checkpoint returns False when the checkpoint is not in state (claudo.py:284-285). Re-approving with the same HEAD works, but _check_gated_head (458-476) forces a reject whenever HEAD moved. One part I could not check: whether Claudo writes the checkpoint to state.json before a later node fails. That code is in the external Claudo repo; the factory's own docstring implies it does.

### A51. Agent-writable app/golden.toml overrides the IT gate commands (tests/lint become no-ops)

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:1650` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** During a POC build the agent edits apps/<slug>/golden.toml to `[gates.commands] tests = "true"` and `lint = "true"` (or creates the file if the template had none). command_gate runs `true`, which exits 0, so the tests and lint gates PASS on failing code. No trajectory gate runs below MVP, and nothing hashes golden.toml.
- **Suggested fix:** Read gate-command overrides only from IT's golden_paths_dir/<chosen path>/golden.toml (load_golden_paths already parses gate_commands), never from the app folder; or add golden.toml to the immutability hash.

**Evidence**

> foreman.py:1650 `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`; foreman.py:1684-1688 reads `app / "golden.toml"` and returns its [gates.commands]; _scaffold (foreman.py:936-950) copies every golden-path file, golden.toml included, into the app folder the build agent writes to. The app copy wins over factory.toml, and the immutable gate (1615) only hashes spec/design/tasks.

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from the code. foreman.py:1650 merges `{**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`, so the app's own copy wins over factory.toml. golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, the file inside the app folder. _scaffold (foreman.py:929-951) copies every file of the golden path into that app folder, golden.toml included; tests/test_foreman.py:32 checks that `app/golden.toml` exists after scaffolding. Nothing protects that file: - _immutability_gate (foreman.py:1615-1636) hashes only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. - _restore_edited_plan (1313-1339) restores only tasks.md. - _clean_tree_gate (1455-1462) only requires the change to be committed, which the agent can do. - A repo-wide grep for golden.toml and for protected/deny/readonly found no other guard.  The sandbox (sandbox.py:6) mounts the app folder, so the agent can write to it. An agent that sets `[gates.commands] tests = "true"` turns the tests and lint gates into no-ops. Below the Claudo path (_trajectory_gate at 1638-1642 returns "not applicable"), at most a scope_drift ack is raised, and only when the build runs through Claudo. The threat model is explicit in the code: the immutable-gate docstring says an agent that weakens its own checks "would pass every other gate". So this is a real gate bypass and not intended behavior. Severity stays high.
2. I could not refute this finding; the code says what it claims. foreman.py:1650 merges `{**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`, so the app's commands win. golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, the copy inside the app folder, not IT's golden_paths_dir. _scaffold (foreman.py:936-950) copies every template file into the app, including golden_paths/fullstack-react/golden.toml, which carries a [gates.commands] tests override. Nothing guards that file. _immutability_gate (1615) hashes only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. _restore_edited_plan (1313-1338) restores only tasks.md. _clean_tree_gate (1455-1462) only requires a committed tree, so a committed edit to golden.toml passes it. A remote grep of every src/ and tests/ .py file found golden.toml read in only two places: design.py:363 (IT's templates) and foreman.py:1684 (the app copy). An agent that sets tests = "true" makes command_gate (gates.py:132, 165) run that string, so the tests gate passes. I am downgrading severity to medium for two reasons. An agent with write access to the app can already weaken the tests themselves; the tests_modified ack at foreman.py:1660-1669 applies only when kind != app and base_sha is set. And a human IT ship review still follows. The override path is still a real, silent hole in the mechanical gate.

### A52. Build agent controls the gate commands through the app's own golden.toml

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:1650` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** security
- **Failure scenario:** The build agent writes `[gates.commands]\ntests = "true"\nlint = "true"` into apps/<slug>/golden.toml. The tests and lint gates pass whatever the code does. In a POC host fallback, an offline run or --unsafe-host, the command runs with shell=True on the host (gates.py:70-73), so it can be any shell command. A malformed golden.toml also raises an uncaught TOMLDecodeError (foreman.py:1687) out of `run`.
- **Suggested fix:** Read gate overrides from the IT-owned golden_paths/<name>/golden.toml in the factory tree (record the golden path name on the item), never from the app's copy. At the least, refuse an app golden.toml that differs from the template.

**Evidence**

> foreman.py:1650 `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`. golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, which lives in the app directory the build agent writes (sandbox mount sandbox.py:108 `:/workspace:rw`; host builds cwd=app). The app's values override IT's factory.toml commands.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code says what the finding says. foreman.py:1650 builds the gate commands as {**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}, so the app's values win. golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads app/"golden.toml" from the app directory, the same directory the build agent writes to (sandbox.py:108 mounts it as /workspace:rw). It applies every [gates.commands] key with no check against IT's template under golden_paths/. The only other places that read golden.toml are design.py:359-363, which loads IT's templates for picking a path, and the tests: tests/test_golden_paths.py:85 and 93 exercise the override only through the app copy.  I found nothing that stops the agent editing golden.toml. The immutability gate (foreman.py:1609-1632) hashes only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. modified_tests flags rewritten test files, not golden.toml. So an agent that writes tests = "true" makes the tests gate pass.  A malformed golden.toml makes tomllib.loads at foreman.py:1687 raise, and nothing in _do_gate catches it. I have not traced every caller, so treat that part as plausible rather than confirmed.  I corrected the severity down because the host-RCE part overstates the impact. _gate_executor (foreman.py:1415-1419) runs the gate commands in the sandbox whenever the build ran there. They reach the host shell (gates.py:70-73, shell=True) only when the build already ran agent code on the host (host fallback or --unsafe-host). In that case the agent could already run anything on the host, so this adds no new capability there. What remains is a real bypass of the mechanical tests and lint gates, which is still worth fixing.
2. I could not refute it, and the main claim holds in the code. In foreman.py:1650 `_do_gate` passes `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`. Because the app's values come second, they override the factory.toml commands (factory.toml:105-107 sets `tests` and `lint`). `golden_gate_commands` (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, meaning the copy inside the app directory, not `cfg.golden_paths_dir`. Nothing compares it to the IT template: grepping foreman.py for "golden" finds only template reads at lines 802-951 and this function. The build agent writes in that directory: the host build runs with `cwd=app` and `permission_mode="acceptEdits"` (around line 1417), and the sandbox mounts the workspace `:rw`. The command then runs through gates.py:68-73, `subprocess.run(command, shell=True, cwd=...)`. The immutability gate (around lines 1610-1631) checks only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. `clean_tree` checks only for uncommitted changes. The scope_drift ack (lines 1438-1447) covers only uncommitted leftovers, so a committed golden.toml change is not flagged. The uncaught `tomllib` decode error at line 1687 is also real. I lowered the severity from high to medium for two reasons. First, the agent that could do this already runs arbitrary code in that same place: on the host in host mode, otherwise in the sandbox. Second, it controls the app's pyproject and test files, so it can already make `uv run pytest` pass, for example with a conftest.py. golden.toml is one more easy way to bypass the gates, not a new privilege, and the human ship_review stays in place.

### A53. Build agent can rewrite the gate commands: the merge gate reads the copy of golden.toml inside the app

- **Location:** `src/factory/foreman.py:1650` · **category:** security/gate-bypass · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** During a build the agent (or a change on an imported repo) writes `[gates.commands] tests = "true"` and `lint = "true"` into apps/<slug>/golden.toml. _do_gate then runs `true` for the tests and lint gates, both pass, and the item ships at mvp/prod with failing tests. The build prompt only says 'keep the golden-path structure' (templates.py:228). Nothing enforces it.
- **Suggested fix:** Take the overrides from the IT-owned manifest (load_golden_paths(cfg.golden_paths_dir) by the name recorded on the item at scaffold time, i.e. GoldenPath.gate_commands). Or add golden.toml, justfile and .github/ to the immutable gate's hashed set.

**Evidence**

> foreman.py:1650 `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`. foreman.py:1684 `manifest = app / "golden.toml"`. Values from the app override factory.toml. _scaffold (foreman.py:936-950) copies golden.toml into the app like any other file. _immutability_gate (foreman.py:1615) only protects spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. design.py:372 loads the IT-owned copy into GoldenPath.gate_commands, but no runtime code reads that field (grep: the only uses are design.py:355/372).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code says what it claims. I read every file remotely with gh api. I kept one temporary scratchpad copy of foreman.py for line-range reads and deleted it afterwards.  - **The app's copy wins.** foreman.py:1650 passes `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}` to run_gates. Values from the app therefore override factory.toml, which sets tests = "uv run --quiet pytest -q" and lint = "uv run --quiet ruff check ." (factory.toml:105-107). - **Where those values come from.** golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1689) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, the copy inside the agent's working tree, and returns whatever `[gates.commands]` it contains. - **The file is copied in like any other.** _scaffold (around foreman.py:936-951) walks the whole golden-path folder with rglob and writes every file into the app, golden.toml included. - **Nothing protects it:**   - _immutability_gate (foreman.py:1609-1632) hashes only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md.   - _restore_edited_plan (foreman.py:1313) restores only tasks.md.   - _clean_tree_gate (foreman.py:1455) checks only that the tree is committed, so a committed edit to golden.toml passes it. - **The IT-owned copy is never used.** design.py:355/372 loads GoldenPath.gate_commands from IT's own copy of the manifests, and no gate code calls it. - **The tests cover this path.** tests/test_golden_paths.py:85 asserts that golden_gate_commands(app) reads the app's golden.toml, so this is how the code is meant to work, and nothing in it guards against the file being edited.  Failure scenario: during the build, an agent commits golden.toml with `[gates.commands] tests = "true"` and `lint = "true"`. command_gate (gates.py:165) then runs `true` for both, both gates pass, and the item reaches ship with failing tests. The immutable gate's docstring (foreman.py:1609-1612) says prompt-only rules were made mechanical for exactly this threat, an agent weakening its own checks. golden.toml is the same kind of gap.  I keep high severity because the bypass fully disables the tests and lint gates. The IT ship review is the only remaining human check, and I did not confirm that it would catch a golden.toml diff.
2. I read the code and it matches the finding. At foreman.py:1650, _do_gate passes `commands={**self.cfg.gate_commands, **golden_gate_commands(app)}`. golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1688) reads `app / "golden.toml"`, the copy inside the app, and its values override factory.toml (factory.toml:105-107 defines tests and lint). _scaffold (foreman.py:936-950) copies every golden-path file into the app, and that includes golden.toml (golden_paths/fullstack-react/golden.toml carries [gates.commands] tests). gates.py command_gate runs the command it is given and passes on exit code 0, so `tests = "true"` would pass. _immutability_gate (foreman.py:1615) hashes only spec.md, design.md and tasks.md. A grep of foreman.py finds no other protection or diff check on golden.toml. GoldenPath.gate_commands in design.py is set at line 372 and never read at runtime. The scenario therefore holds: a build that edits apps/<slug>/golden.toml can swap the tests and lint gates for no-ops. I rate it medium rather than high because the build agent already controls the app's tests and its pyproject ruff config, so the gates were never fully independent of the agent. This override is still the most direct bypass, because one line replaces the IT-configured command outright.

### A54. OpenAI project/service keys (sk-proj-..., sk-svcacct-...) are not detected

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:20` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** An app commits `OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-proj-AbC..._xyz` in a .env or .py file: the secrets gate passes and the history check passes.
- **Suggested fix:** Use `\bsk-(?:proj-|svcacct-|admin-)?[A-Za-z0-9_-]{32,}`.

**Evidence**

> `\bsk-[A-Za-z0-9]{32,}\b` requires 32 alphanumerics right after `sk-`. Current OpenAI keys are `sk-proj-<...with - and _>`, and the `-` after `proj` breaks the run. Checked: a sk-proj key gives False. The Anthropic pattern on line 19 does not cover them either.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. I read src/factory/gates.py remotely and line 20 says exactly what the finding claims: `("generic API key", re.compile(r"\bsk-[A-Za-z0-9]{32,}\b"))`. Line 19 (`\bsk-ant-...`) only matches Anthropic keys. Both secrets_gate (lines 103-104, finditer over SECRET_PATTERNS) and secrets_in_history (lines 205-207) rely on these patterns alone. `.env` is in TEXT_SUFFIXES (line 41) and SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES (line 62), so those files do get scanned. I ran the line 19, 20 and 28 regexes on three strings: `OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-proj-...`, `OPENAI_API_KEY = "sk-proj-..."` and `sk-svcacct-...`. Every result was False. The hardcoded-credential pattern on line 28 does not catch them either: `\b` cannot match between `_` and `API_KEY` in OPENAI_API_KEY, and the unquoted .env form also fails its quote requirement. Nothing else guards this case, and nothing documents it as intended. Current OpenAI project and service-account keys therefore pass both the secrets gate and the history check. Medium severity is appropriate.
2. I confirmed this from src/factory/gates.py:16-29, which I read. Line 20 is `\bsk-[A-Za-z0-9]{32,}\b`. It needs 32 or more alphanumerics straight after `sk-`. In `sk-proj-...` the hyphen after `proj` breaks that run, and the `_` and `-` inside modern OpenAI keys break it again. Line 19 only matches `sk-ant-`. The hardcoded-credential pattern at line 27 cannot catch the key either, for two reasons: the `api_key` keyword needs a word boundary in front of it, and `OPENAI_API_KEY` has `_` (a word character) just before `API_KEY`; and the value must be quoted. I ran all three regexes locally against `OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-proj-...` (unquoted, as in a .env file) and `OPENAI_API_KEY = "sk-proj-..."` (as in a .py file). Every pattern returned False for both. Both scans use this same list: line 103 (the gate) and line 205 (the history check). So both pass the secret. It is a real fail-open gap in the secret detector. Medium severity is reasonable, because this is a heuristic gate and other key formats are still covered.

### A55. Hardcoded-credential regex misses the most common forms (SECRET_KEY, DB_PASSWORD, JSON keys, unquoted .env)

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:28` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** settings.py contains `SECRET_KEY = "django-insecure-..."` or config.json contains `{"password": "Prod#2026pass"}`. secrets_gate reports 'no secrets found' and secrets_in_history (same SECRET_PATTERNS) lets it publish.
- **Suggested fix:** Allow a prefix and suffix around the keyword (`[A-Za-z0-9_]*(?:password|secret|token|api_?key)[A-Za-z0-9_]*`), an optional closing quote before `[:=]`, and unquoted values in .env/YAML.

**Evidence**

> `(?i)\b(?:password|passwd|secret|api_key|apikey|token)\s*[:=]\s*['\"][^'\"\s]{8,}['\"]`. Checked with Python: False for `DB_PASSWORD = "hunter2hunter2"` (`_` is a word char, so there is no \b), `SECRET_KEY = "django-insecure-abc"` (`_key` sits between the word and `=`), `{"password": "supersecret1"}` (closing quote before `:`), `PASSWORD=hunter2hunter2` (unquoted), `client_secret = "..."` and `GITHUB_TOKEN: "..."`. Only bare `password = "..."` matches.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I couldn't refute it; the code says what the finding says. src/factory/gates.py:28 defines the regex exactly as quoted. I ran that exact pattern in Python: - `password = "hunter2hunter2"` matches (True). - These do not match (False): `DB_PASSWORD = "..."` (no \b before `password` after `_`), `SECRET_KEY = "..."` (`_KEY` sits between the keyword and `=`), `{"password": "..."}` (closing quote before `:`), `PASSWORD=hunter2hunter2` (unquoted value) and `db_password: "..."`.  Both callers use this list unchanged, so the blind spot carries through: - secrets_gate (gates.py:92-107, loop at 103) reports "no secrets found" and passes. - secrets_in_history (gates.py:176-210, loop at 205) is what the README (line 204) says guards publishing.  Nothing elsewhere guards against this. The README (line 33) calls the secrets scan a blocking gate and does not describe it as limited to vendor key formats. tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py has a "G-2: secrets the gate used to miss" section, but its secrets_in_history asserts cover only the AWS access key label, and that file contains no password or hardcoded-credential case.  I'm lowering severity to medium. The gate is a pattern heuristic: the vendor-specific patterns (AWS, GitHub, Anthropic, Stripe, etc., lines 17-25) still catch real provider keys. The gap is limited to generic credential assignments, so it is a coverage hole in a best-effort detector, not a bypass of a hard guarantee.
2. The finding holds. I read src/factory/gates.py:28 remotely and ran its regex in Python. It returns False for `DB_PASSWORD = "hunter2hunter2"`, `SECRET_KEY = "django-insecure-abc"`, `{"password": "supersecret1"}`, `PASSWORD=hunter2hunter2` and `client_secret = "abcdefghij"`. It returns True only for bare `password = "hunter2hunter2"`. The same SECRET_PATTERNS list drives both secrets_gate (gates.py:92-107), which returns ok=True with "no secrets found", and secrets_in_history (gates.py:176-215), which scans before a publish. So a Django SECRET_KEY or a JSON password passes both checks. I found nothing elsewhere that covers these forms, and nothing documents the gap as intended: tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py has no credential or password cases. I am lowering severity from high to medium. Lines 17-25 still catch provider-format keys (AWS, Anthropic, GitHub, Stripe, Google), whatever variable name they are stored under. A heuristic regex that misses key=value forms weakens the gate, but it does not make it fail open for every secret.

### A56. Secrets gate extension allowlist omits common source and config types (.mjs, .cjs, .vue, .go, .java, .rb, .php, .csv, extensionless credentials files)

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:95` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** web/src/client.mjs contains `const KEY = "sk-ant-..."`, or a file `.git-credentials` / `credentials` is committed. Neither is scanned and the gate passes.
- **Suggested fix:** Scan every file that decodes as text (skip only binary by NUL sniffing) rather than an allowlist of extensions.

**Evidence**

> gates.py:31-60 TEXT_SUFFIXES has no .mjs/.cjs/.vue/.svelte/.go/.rb/.java/.php/.kt/.cs/.rs/.csv/.lock. gates.py:62 prefixes cover only dockerfile/.env/.npmrc/.pypirc/id_*. detect.py:175 treats .mjs/.cjs as JS sources, so the factory knows these files are code.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The gate behaviour is confirmed, but there is a backstop at publish time. gates.py:92-99 (secrets_gate) skips every file whose suffix is not in TEXT_SUFFIXES (gates.py:31-60, which has no .mjs/.cjs/.vue/.go/.java/.rb/.php/.csv) and whose name does not start with SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES (gates.py:62: dockerfile, .env, .npmrc, .pypirc, id_*). A file `.git-credentials` or `credentials` matches neither. detect.py:175 does treat .mjs/.cjs as JS. So `web/src/client.mjs` containing `sk-ant-...` is never read and the secrets gate reports PASS (run_gates, gates.py:160-161). The impact is smaller than claimed, though. Both publish paths call _verify_publishable (foreman.py:1101 for apps, foreman.py:1182 for changes). That function runs secrets_in_history (foreman.py:1078), which scans every added `+` line of every commit in the range with no extension filter (gates.py:196-209), and raises FactoryError if it finds anything. The .mjs key is therefore blocked before it leaves the machine through publish. What remains: the working-tree gate fails open on these file types, so the item reaches IT approval with a false 'no secrets found' and only fails later at publish. That is a real gap in the gate, but not a leak, so the severity drops from medium to low.
2. The finding holds. In src/factory/gates.py, secrets_gate (lines 92-107) skips every file unless its suffix is in TEXT_SUFFIXES (lines 31-60) or its name starts with one of SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES (line 62, checked at lines 95-98). TEXT_SUFFIXES has no .mjs, .cjs, .vue, .svelte, .go, .java, .rb, .php or .csv. The prefixes do not match `.git-credentials` or `credentials`. Meanwhile src/factory/detect.py:175 parses .mjs and .cjs files as JS sources, so the factory does treat them as app code. iter_files (detect.py:57) returns those files, but the suffix filter drops them before any pattern runs. So a file web/src/client.mjs containing `const KEY = "sk-ant-..."` produces no hit, and the gate returns ok. I found nothing that makes the allowlist intended behaviour, and nothing that guards this case elsewhere. The G-2 tests in tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py (lines 82-101) cover only allowlisted suffixes and prefixed names. One partial mitigation: Go, Java, Ruby and PHP projects are flagged as unanalysed ecosystems and need IT approval (test lines 65-74). Once that exception is granted, their source files are still never scanned for secrets, and .mjs, .cjs and .vue files in an approved JS app are not covered by any mitigation. Medium severity fits: this is a fail-open gap in a heuristic gate, not a direct exploit.

### A57. Secrets gate silently skips any file that is not valid UTF-8

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:101` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** A .py or .env file contains one Latin-1 byte (e.g. a comment with `\xe9`) and an AWS key `AKIA...`. The decode fails, the file is skipped, and the gate says 'no secrets found'.
- **Suggested fix:** Read with errors="replace" (or latin-1), or report unreadable candidate files as a failure instead of continuing.

**Evidence**

> gates.py:99-102 `text = path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")` / `except (UnicodeDecodeError, OSError): continue`. The file is dropped from the scan without any report.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this. I read gates.py through `gh api` and did not clone the repo. secrets_gate (gates.py:92) walks iter_files and keeps the files that match TEXT_SUFFIXES or SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES. Line 100 is `text = path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")` and lines 101-102 are `except (UnicodeDecodeError, OSError): continue`. A .py or .env file with a single Latin-1 byte (\xe9) therefore raises UnicodeDecodeError and is dropped without any record. If no other file has a hit, line 107 returns GateResult("secrets", True, "no secrets found"), which is a fail-open. tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py has no test for decode errors. A grep for utf/decode/latin/binary/skip found only helpers that write UTF-8.  There is one mitigation, and it lowers the severity. Before publishing, foreman.py:1078 calls secrets_in_history (gates.py:176-190). That function runs `git log -p` with errors="replace", so a key committed in such a file is still caught there. Git only treats a file as binary if it contains NUL bytes, so a Latin-1 byte still produces a text diff. The leak to the remote is blocked at publish, as long as the file was committed. The part that stays real is the gate itself: it reports PASS ("no secrets found") on a tree that holds a live key, and maturity gating and approval rely on that report. I am lowering the severity from medium to low because publish has this second check.
2. I could not refute it. I read src/factory/gates.py:92-107 and the code does what the finding says. secrets_gate selects candidate files by suffix or name prefix (lines 95-98). It then calls path.read_text(encoding="utf-8") (line 100), and on `except (UnicodeDecodeError, OSError): continue` (lines 101-102) it drops the file. Nothing is added to `hits` and no "unreadable" note is recorded. If no other file has a hit, line 107 returns GateResult("secrets", True, "no secrets found").  So a .py or .env file containing an AKIA key (matched by the pattern at line 18) plus one stray Latin-1 byte such as 0xE9 fails the decode, is skipped, and the gate passes.  Nothing elsewhere guards against this. The gate never re-reads with errors="replace". By contrast, shell_executor at lines 76-77 does use errors="replace", which shows the author knew that option. The blind-spot tests (tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py:98-107, 133) cover file types and token types only, not non-UTF-8 content. I found no test or doc saying this skip is intended.  This is a fail-open path in a security gate, triggered by a realistic input (a legacy-encoded comment or config value). Medium severity holds.

### A58. secrets_in_history misses secrets in files git treats as binary (.gitattributes `-diff`/`binary`, or a NUL byte)

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:184` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** A commit adds `secrets.env` plus `.gitattributes` with `*.env -diff`, and a later commit deletes secrets.env. The working-tree gate no longer sees the file, the history scan prints 'Binary files differ', and publish pushes the key.
- **Suggested fix:** Pass `--text` (and `--no-textconv`) to git log so every blob's added lines are emitted.

**Evidence**

> `git log -p -m --no-color --no-ext-diff ...` without `--text`/`-a`. For a binary-classified path git prints only `Binary files a/x and b/x differ` and no `+` lines, so the loop at 197-209 sees nothing. The app (agent-written) controls its own .gitattributes.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. src/factory/gates.py:184 runs `git log -p -m --no-color --no-ext-diff --format=%x00commit %H <range>` with neither `--text`/`-a` nor `--no-textconv`. Git does not show a line diff for a blob it treats as binary: one that matches a `-diff` or `binary` attribute in .gitattributes, or one with a NUL byte in its leading bytes. It prints only a "Binary files ... differ" line, and that line does not start with "+". So the scan loop at gates.py:197-209 never tests that blob's content against SECRET_PATTERNS. The only caller, foreman.py:1078 (the publish pre-flight), treats an empty result as clean and goes on to publish. I found no guard elsewhere: searching the repo found no handling of .gitattributes or `--text`. The tests in tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py (315-367) cover only plain text .py files. In the failure scenario, the agent commits secrets.env together with a .gitattributes line `*.env -diff` (or simply a NUL byte in the file), then deletes the file in a later commit. The working-tree secrets gate no longer sees it, the history scan sees only "Binary files differ", and publish pushes the key. The severity of medium fits: the defect needs a binary-classified file, but it fails open on the exact case this function exists to catch.
2. I could not refute this finding. At src/factory/gates.py:183-190, `secrets_in_history` runs `git log -p -m --no-color --no-ext-diff --format=%x00commit %H <range>` without `--text`/`-a`. Git treats a path as binary when `.gitattributes` unsets `diff` (`-diff`/`binary`) or when the blob contains a NUL byte. For such a path git prints only "Binary files a/x and b/x differ" and no `+` lines. The loop at gates.py:197-209 only matches `+` lines that come after an `@@` hunk header, so such a path produces no hit. In the caller, src/factory/foreman.py:1077-1086, an empty `hits` list lets the publish go ahead. This history check is the only safeguard against a key that was committed and then deleted; the comment at gates.py:179-180 says so, and the working-tree secrets gate cannot see a deleted file. The app is agent-written, so it controls its own `.gitattributes`. A NUL byte in a committed file is also an accidental route. The tests I found by grep (tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py:315-367) only call the function with text-file cases. I found no `--text` flag and no `.gitattributes` handling anywhere in the Python sources. The fix (`--text --no-textconv`) is valid. Medium severity is fair: the bypass needs a binary-classified path, plus a commit-then-delete or a working-tree gate that misses the file, and IT approval still applies.

### A59. radar-import: JSON null or list values become literal 'None' / "['a', 'b']" strings

- **Location:** `src/factory/importer.py:129` · **category:** import · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** A BYOR JSON export with `"replaced_by": null` gives replaced_by 'none', and the import fails with "replaced_by 'none' is not on the radar". With `"category": null` the category is written as 'none' and the tech is not flagged in needs_category. With `"match": ["pg","postgres"]` the alias becomes "['pg', 'postgres']".
- **Suggested fix:** Map None to '' and join lists with ';' before stringifying.

**Evidence**

> importer.py:129 `{str(k).strip().lower(): str(v).strip() for k, v in row.items()}`. str(None) is 'None' and str(list) is its repr. Backstage rows do the same at importer.py:113 `str(entry.get("category", ""))`. importer.py:226 then slugifies replaced_by, and importer.py:232-234 rejects unknown replacements.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this; the code shows the defect. src/factory/importer.py:129 builds each BYOR JSON row with `str(v).strip()` and does not check for None or lists, so `"replaced_by": null` becomes the string 'None'. That string is truthy, so `_col` at line 136 (`if row.get(a)`) picks it up. Line 226 then slugifies it to 'none', and lines 232-234 add the blocking problem "replaced_by 'none' is not on the radar", which makes `ok` False. A `"category": null` becomes 'none' at line 152, so line 214 uses it as the category and line 215 never adds the tech to needs_category. A list in `match` becomes its repr, which lines 217-218 split on ';' only, giving the single alias "['pg', 'postgres']". On the Backstage side, line 113 has the same problem for `category: null` (`str(entry.get("category", ""))` gives 'None'). Line 114 does not: replacedBy uses `or ""`, so a null replacedBy is safe there; the finding's evidence lists only line 113 for Backstage, which matches. Nothing upstream guards these cases, since `_rows` passes values through unchanged. Severity stays medium: null values in a JSON export are realistic, and the replaced_by case fails the whole import with a misleading message.
2. I read src/factory/importer.py in full and the code behaves as the finding says. At importer.py:129, BYOR JSON rows are built with `str(v).strip()`, so a JSON null becomes "None" and a list becomes its Python repr. Nothing filters these out. `_col` (importer.py:136) accepts any truthy value, and "None" is truthy.  Here is what happens to each field: - **replaced_by: null.** It becomes "None", which `slugify` turns into 'none' (importer.py:226). The check at importer.py:232-234 then adds the problem "replaced_by 'none' is not on the radar", so the import fails. - **category: null.** `_entry` lowercases the value to 'none' (importer.py:152). Because raw["category"] is truthy, the tech is never added to needs_category (importer.py:214-216), and the TOML gets `category = "none"`. This failure is silent. - **match: ["pg","postgres"].** It becomes the string "['pg', 'postgres']". It contains no ';' or '|', so the split at importer.py:218 leaves it as a single wrong alias.  On the Backstage side, importer.py:113 has the same null-to-"None" problem for category. importer.py:114 does not, because its `or` chain handles null for replacedBy. Nothing upstream catches the null: import_radar only catches decode, attribute and type errors (importer.py:176).  Severity stays medium. A null replaced_by makes the import fail loudly. A null category corrupts the output silently and skips the warning that should have been raised.

### A60. radar-import: every tech name becomes a non-strict free-text matcher, so short names like 'Go' or 'R' block designs

- **Location:** `src/factory/importer.py:217` · **category:** import · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** An imported radar with 'Go' on hold, and an idea titled 'Let users go to their dashboard'. Triage notes 'business mentioned Go (hold)'. The title lands in design.md outside the radar:ignore block (design.py:140), so the design's radar check finds a BLOCK and _do_design fails with 'design violates the radar'.
- **Suggested fix:** Emit `text_strict = true` for names of 3 characters or fewer, or for common English words, and do not add the bare lowercase name as a text alias by default.

**Evidence**

> importer.py:217 `aliases = [raw["name"].lower(), tech_id]` puts the bare name in `match`, and _render (importer.py:159-167) never emits `text_strict`. radar.py:84-89 then compiles a plain word pattern `(?<![\w-])(?:go)(?![\w-])` for every alias of a non-strict tech, which scan_text applies to ideas and design.md.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. In importer.py, the alias list at line 217 (`aliases = [raw["name"].lower(), tech_id]`) adds the bare lowercase name to `match`. `_render` (importer.py:159-168) writes only id, name, category, ring, match, replaced_by and golden_path, and the word "strict" never appears anywhere in importer.py. So every imported tech has text_strict=False, which is the default set at radar.py:151.  For a tech that is not strict, radar.py:87-88 compiles `(?<![\w-])(?:go)(?![\w-])` with re.I. That pattern matches the ordinary English word "go" in "Let users go to their dashboard". At radar.py:27, hold is BLOCK at every maturity.  The path to the failure runs through foreman.py:689-710. `_do_design` renders design.md, then `_design_report` calls `check_project` with `docs=[design.md]`. At guard.py:208-211, `check_project` strips only the radar:ignore regions and then calls `scan_text` on the rest. The title is written as `# Design: {title}` at design.py:140, outside the ignore block (which only opens at design.py:188), so it gets recorded as a BLOCK violation. foreman.py:709-710 then returns "design violates the radar".  The triage note comes from the same `scan_text` call on the idea, at foreman.py:603-612. The hand-written radar.toml (lines 7 and 160) shows text_strict is the intended defence against everyday-word names like "requests", and the importer has no way to produce it. The failure needs an imported hold tech whose name is a common word, which is a realistic case, and it can be recovered by editing radar.toml by hand, so medium severity fits.
2. I confirmed the finding from the code. Its line references are correct, with one small correction (noted below).  1. **The importer makes every name a free-text matcher.** `importer.py:217` sets `aliases = [raw["name"].lower(), tech_id]`. `_render` (importer.py:159-167) writes `id`, `name`, `category`, `ring`, `match`, `replaced_by` and `golden_path`, but never `text_strict`. The header comment of the generated file asks the reviewer to check `category` and `replaced_by` only.  2. **Such a tech gets a bare-word pattern.** `load_radar` (radar.py:151) defaults `text_strict` to False. For non-strict techs, radar.py:84-89 then compiles `(?<![\w-])(?:go)(?![\w-])` with `re.I`, so the plain English word "go" matches. The hand-written radar.toml marks `requests` with `text_strict = true` (radar.toml:160). That shows the authors know about the everyday-word problem, but imported radars never get this protection.  3. **The title lands outside the ignore block.** `foreman.intake` stores `title` as given (foreman.py:152). `render_design` writes `# Design: {title}` at design.py:140. That is before the `radar:ignore` block, which only starts at design.py:188.  4. **The scan finds the word and blocks.** `_do_design` (foreman.py:689-710) writes design.md and runs `_design_report`, which calls `check_project` with `docs=[design.md]`. `guard.py:208-211` removes only the ignore block, then runs `radar.scan_text` on the rest and records the verdict. Hold is BLOCK at every maturity (radar.py:27), so foreman.py:709-710 returns "design violates the radar".  5. **Triage also picks it up.** foreman.py:603 runs `scan_text` on the idea text, so an idea containing "go" gets the note "business mentioned Go (hold)".  The scenario holds: an imported radar with "Go" on hold, plus an idea title or text containing the word "go", makes the design step fail.  Correction: the ignore-block reference should be design.py:188, not design.py:140; line 140 is where the title is written. This is a real false-positive blocker, not style. Medium severity is fair, since the imported TOML is meant to be reviewed but nothing prompts the reviewer about `text_strict`.

### A61. Judge output with null criteria or a non-string id raises TypeError out of the gate step

- **Location:** `src/factory/judge.py:231` · **category:** judge-parsing · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** The model answers `{"criteria": null, "summary": "..."}`. The advisory judge crashes the run after the gates passed: the item is not advanced or saved, and the judge's spend is not added to cost_usd.
- **Suggested fix:** Validate that criteria is a list and that id is a str before the set lookup, and wrap evaluate() in judge() so any error yields an 'unreliable' report.

**Evidence**

> judge.py:231 `for raw_c in data.get("criteria", [])`: with `"criteria": null` this is `for x in None`, giving TypeError. judge.py:235 `cid not in wanted`: with `"id": ["fidelity"]` this raises unhashable list TypeError (both checked in Python). judge() at 288 does not catch it, although its docstring says 'never an exception'. foreman.py:1675-1676 calls judge_artifact after gated_sha is set, so the exception escapes _do_gate before store.save.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I checked this against the code on main, read remotely without cloning. judge.py:231 is `for raw_c in data.get("criteria", []):`. When the JSON has `"criteria": null`, `.get` returns None and the loop over None raises TypeError. At judge.py:234-235, `cid = raw_c.get("id")` is tested with `cid not in wanted`, and `wanted` is a set (line 228), so an id that is a list raises an unhashable TypeError. extract_json only checks that the top level is a dict (line 206), so nothing validates criteria or id before the loop. judge() at 285-288 calls evaluate() with no try/except, although its docstring at 273 says it never raises. judge_panel (332-351) and combine add no guard either. In foreman.py, judge_artifact (857-887) calls judge_panel without a try. _do_gate calls it at 1676, right after gated_sha is set at 1675. The run loop (about lines 330-347) calls `handler(item)` without a try, so the exception skips _advance and the `self.store.save(item)` that follows. The cost_usd increment at 878 is never reached, so the judge's spend is lost. The gates have already passed by then. The scenario requires the model to return malformed JSON shapes, which is less likely than a clean answer but not guarded anywhere. Severity medium is reasonable: it is an advisory step that can crash the run and lose saved state.
2. I could not refute the finding; the code confirms it. In judge.py:231, evaluate() runs `for raw_c in data.get("criteria", [])`. extract_json (198-206) only checks that the top-level value is a dict, so `"criteria": null` makes this `for raw_c in None`, which raises TypeError. In judge.py:235, `cid not in wanted` tests membership in a set, so a list id raises an unhashable TypeError. In judge.py:288, judge() calls evaluate() with no try, although its docstring at 273 says 'never an exception'. judge_panel (345-351) does not catch it either. On the foreman side, judge_artifact (foreman.py:896-910) calls judge_panel with no try. In the current fetch, _do_gate calls judge_artifact at foreman.py:1736-1737, after `item.gated_sha = head_sha(app)`; the finding's 1675-1676 matched my first fetch of the file, so main appears to have moved between my reads. The step loop (foreman.py:331-348) calls the handler with no exception handling, so the TypeError escapes before `self.store.save(item)` at 348: the item is neither advanced nor saved, and the judge's spend is never recorded. Two things limit the impact. The judge is opt-in, needing a runner and judge_enabled or judge_from (889-902). It also needs a malformed but parseable JSON answer from the model. Medium severity is fair.

### A62. porcelain() returns C-escaped paths for non-ASCII names (and 'old -> new' for renames), so path-scoped commits fail

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:174` · **category:** git · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** A Claudo build leaves an untracked `docs/équipe.md` (or a test fixture with an accent). After res.ok, _commit_leftovers (foreman.py:1503) raises ProjectError 'pathspec ... did not match any files'. ProjectError is not in the CLI's except list (cli.py:559), so `factory run` dies with a traceback before store.save (foreman.py:347): claudo_cp is not recorded, and the next run re-runs Claudo.
- **Suggested fix:** Use `git status --porcelain=v1 -z -uall` and split on NUL (for renames, take the destination entry). Or set `-c core.quotepath=false` and handle renames explicitly. Also catch ProjectError in main() or in the stage handlers.

**Evidence**

> project.py:173-174 runs `status --porcelain -uall` and returns `line[3:].strip().strip('"')`. With git's default core.quotepath=true, a file `données.py` is printed as `"donn\303\251es.py"`, which becomes the literal `donn\303\251es.py`. A staged rename prints `old -> new`. commit_leftovers_split passes these to `git add -A -- <paths>` and `git commit -- <paths>` (project.py:201-202, 215-217).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. In src/factory/project.py, porcelain() (lines 171-174) runs `git status --porcelain -uall` without `-z` or `-c core.quotepath=false`. It then returns `line[3:].strip().strip('"')`. A search for "quotepath" in the repo finds nothing, and prepare_project's config calls (project.py:100-102) set only user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf, so git's default core.quotepath=true applies. Under that default, a path such as `docs/équipe.md` is printed as `"docs/\303\251quipe.md"`. Stripping the quotes leaves the literal octal-escaped string, which does not name any real file. _commit_paths (project.py:198-202) passes that string to `git add -A -- <paths>`, which fails with "pathspec did not match", and _git_raw (project.py:59-66) raises ProjectError on any nonzero exit. The call path is commit_leftovers_split (project.py:205-217) → foreman._commit_leftovers (foreman.py:1438-1439) → _build_with_claudo, which calls it when res.ok (foreman.py:1502-1503). That happens before claudo_cp is set (foreman.py:1504-1505). Nothing on the way catches the error. _build_with_claudo is called outside any try block from _do_build_change (foreman.py:1010; its try covers only begin_change at 996-999) and from the app build path (foreman.py:1415). The run loop calls handler(item) at foreman.py:332 with no except around it, and cli.main (the except list shown at cli.py ~559) catches ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError and KeyError, but not ProjectError, which subclasses Exception directly (project.py:37). So the run ends in a traceback, store.save at foreman.py:347 never runs, and claudo_cp is not recorded. The rename half of the claim (`old -> new`) is real but less likely, because Claudo's leftovers are mostly untracked or unstaged files. The non-ASCII case alone is enough to confirm the defect. I am keeping it at medium: it needs a non-ASCII filename, but the result is a crash and a lost checkpoint.
2. I could not refute it; the code does what the finding says. project.py:173-174 runs `git status --porcelain -uall` through _git_raw (project.py:59-66). That call passes no `-c core.quotepath=false` and no `-z`, and nothing in project.py or foreman.py sets quotepath (grep found nothing). With git's default quotepath, an untracked `docs/équipe.md` is printed as `"docs/\303\251quipe.md"`. `.strip('"')` turns that into the literal `docs/\303\251quipe.md`, which is not a real path. commit_leftovers_split (project.py:211-217) then passes it to _commit_paths (project.py:201-203): `git add -A -- <paths>` and `git commit -- <paths>`. That pathspec matches no file, so git exits non-zero and _git_raw raises ProjectError. Call chain: foreman.py:1502-1503 calls _commit_leftovers when res.ok, and foreman.py:1438-1439 calls commit_leftovers_split. The run loop (foreman.py:301-348) has no try/except around `handler(item)`. The ProjectError catches at foreman.py:958/1016/1046/1245/1294 are in other methods. cli.py:559 does not catch ProjectError. So the process dies with a traceback before `self.store.save(item)` (foreman.py:347), and `claudo_cp` (set at foreman.py:1505, after the commit) is never recorded. The `old -> new` rename part is less likely, since Claudo commits its own work, but the non-ASCII case is enough to trigger the bug. Medium severity is about right: it needs a non-ASCII filename, which is plausible for a French-speaking user.

### A63. porcelain() keeps git's C-quoted paths, so committing leftovers fails on non-ASCII or special-character filenames

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:174` · **category:** path-handling · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** The build agent creates `docs/données.md` or `app/café.py`. commit_leftovers_split calls `git add -A -- "docs/donn\303\251es.md"`, and the pathspec matches nothing, so a ProjectError is raised and the build/gate step fails. The bookkeeping/drift split can also misfile such a path.
- **Suggested fix:** Use `git status --porcelain=v1 -z -uall` and parse NUL-separated records (handle the extra field for renames), or pass `-c core.quotePath=false` and handle renames.

**Evidence**

> project.py:173-174 `_git_raw(app, "status", "--porcelain", "-uall")` then `line[3:].strip().strip('"')`. With git's default core.quotePath, non-ASCII bytes come out C-escaped ("caf\303\251.py"). Stripping the quotes leaves the backslash escapes, which is not a real path. Staged renames come out as `old -> new`. These strings feed `_git(app, "add", "-A", "--", *paths)` and `commit ... -- *paths` (project.py:201-202).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this one; the code backs it up. In project.py:173-174, porcelain() runs `git status --porcelain -uall` and only does `line[3:].strip().strip('"')` on each line. Nothing in the module sets core.quotePath: the only config writes are user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf (project.py:100-102), and _git_raw (59-66) passes no `-c` option. With git's default quotePath=true, an untracked `app/café.py` prints as `?? "app/caf\303\251.py"`. After the quotes are stripped, the string still holds the octal escapes. commit_leftovers_split (205-218) passes that string to _commit_paths, which runs `git add -A -- <path>` and then `commit -- <path>` (201-202). The pathspec matches no file, git exits non-zero, and _git_raw raises ProjectError (64-65). The real caller is Foreman._commit_leftovers, at foreman.py:1438-1439, and it does not guard against this. The rename part also holds for staged renames (`old -> new`). That case is less likely, because build leftovers are usually unstaged, and in that case git lists them as a deletion plus an untracked file. commit_all (177-185) only uses the list for display and runs a plain `git add -A`, so it does not fail. The split path does fail. I agree with medium severity: the trigger is a non-ASCII filename, which is plausible in a French-language project such as `données.md`.
2. I could not refute this finding; it holds. src/factory/project.py:173-174 runs `git status --porcelain -uall` through _git_raw (project.py:59-66, a plain `git` call with no `-c core.quotePath=false` or `-z`). It then returns `line[3:].strip().strip('"')`. prepare_project (project.py:96-99) sets only user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf, and no file in the repo sets quotePath. I reproduced the failure in a scratch repo (not a copy of this one). A file named café.py comes out as `?? "caf\303\251.py"`, and `git add -A -- 'caf\303\251.py'` fails with `fatal: pathspec ... did not match any files` (rc=128). _commit_paths (project.py:199-202) passes these strings straight to `git add -A --` and `git commit --`. _git_raw turns a non-zero exit into a ProjectError. The call chain is foreman.py:1439 `_commit_leftovers` -> commit_leftovers_split (project.py:205-218), and _commit_leftovers does not catch the error. The same quoted paths also reach other porcelain() callers, such as foreman.py:1329 `rel in porcelain(app)`, where the membership check silently misses. Paths with spaces are also quoted, but stripping the outer quotes recovers those correctly. Only escaped characters (non-ASCII, backslash, embedded quote, control characters) break. The rename case is weaker: `old -> new` needs staged renames, which this flow rarely produces. I keep severity at medium: an agent writing a file with an accented name is plausible in a French-language context, and when it happens the leftovers commit step crashes.

### A64. tests_modified only watches tests/, so rewritten frontend tests are never flagged

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:420` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** claims

> modified_tests runs `git diff --name-status -M base_sha HEAD -- tests` (420). The fullstack-react golden path keeps its frontend tests at web/src/health.test.ts. README.md:255: 'any existing test it rewrote, is committed apart and needs IT's --note'.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding holds. src/factory/project.py:413-427 modified_tests runs `git diff --name-status -M base_sha HEAD -- tests` (line 420), so it only sees files under tests/. The fullstack-react golden path keeps its frontend test at golden_paths/fullstack-react/web/src/health.test.ts, and golden.toml's gates.commands.tests runs `npm --prefix web test` (vitest), so that test is part of the gate but outside the diff pathspec. foreman.py:1661-1668 sets the tests_modified ship ack only from modified_tests(), so a rewrite of web/src/health.test.ts raises no ack. README.md:255 promises that "any existing test it rewrote, is committed apart and needs IT's --note", and this case breaks that promise. The docstring scopes the check to "(tests/)", which reads as an unexamined assumption rather than a documented exception for frontend tests. I found nothing that guards web/ tests elsewhere. Medium severity is fair: this is a gate bypass in one golden path, and the other gates (review, eval) still run.
2. The finding holds; I could not refute it. src/factory/project.py:414-427: modified_tests runs `git diff --name-status -M base_sha HEAD -- tests`, so it only sees paths under tests/. Its only caller, src/factory/foreman.py:1660-1669, raises the `tests_modified` ack from that list, and only for change items (`item.kind != "app"`). The fullstack-react golden path keeps vitest tests at golden_paths/fullstack-react/web/src/health.test.ts. Its golden.toml `[gates.commands] tests` runs `npm --prefix web test`, and golden_gate_commands (foreman.py:1680-1689) applies that command as the gate. So if a change item on a fullstack-react app rewrites web/src/health.test.ts, no ack is raised. The tests gate can then pass on weakened tests, which contradicts the README guarantee (Claudo section, about line 255): "any existing test it rewrote, is committed apart and needs IT's --note".
