### A65. A sandbox timeout kills only the docker CLI: the container (with --rm, no name) keeps running and keeps writing to the app

- **Location:** `src/factory/sandbox.py:165` · **category:** subprocess-cleanup · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** A build agent loops past 1800 s. The factory records 'timed out' and run() retries the build (foreman.py:337-343), or the gates run, while the first container is still alive with /workspace mounted rw. Two agents now edit the same tree concurrently, the gates and the clean_tree check judge a tree that is still changing, and the orphan keeps spending model credit through the proxy until claude exits on its own.
- **Suggested fix:** Start the container with `--name factory-<uuid>` (or `--cidfile`). On TimeoutExpired, or on any exception, run `docker kill <name>` (and `docker rm -f`) before returning. Same for the gate executor.

**Evidence**

> SandboxedRunner.run: `subprocess.run(self.sandbox.argv(cwd, command, extra), ..., timeout=self.timeout)` then `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired: return AgentResult(False, ..., 'sandboxed claude timed out ...')` (sandbox.py:165-176). Sandbox.executor does the same (sandbox.py:131-135). On timeout, subprocess.run SIGKILLs the `docker` client process. SIGKILL cannot be proxied to the container. argv (sandbox.py:98-114) gives no `--name`/`--cidfile`, so nothing can `docker kill` it afterwards.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it confirms the core defect. In src/factory/sandbox.py:98-114, argv builds `docker run --rm -i ...` with no `--name`, no `--cidfile` and no `--init`. SandboxedRunner.run (sandbox.py:165-176) and Sandbox.executor (sandbox.py:130-135) call subprocess.run with a timeout. On TimeoutExpired they only return an error. subprocess.run kills the docker CLI child, and Docker does not stop an attached container when its client dies. `--rm` takes effect only when the container exits. The prompt has already been fed on stdin, so the in-container claude keeps running with /workspace mounted read-write. No cleanup call follows, and nothing in the code can recover the container's identity to kill it.  One part of the failure scenario is wrong. In foreman.py:330-345, a failed build does not trigger a retry: only a failed gate retries, and a failed build sets the item to "blocked" and breaks. A build timeout therefore does not immediately start a second concurrent build agent. Two paths still hold: 1. After a build timeout, the orphan keeps editing the tree and spending model credit after the item is marked blocked. Any later run or inspection sees a tree that is still changing. 2. After a gate-executor timeout, foreman.py:336-342 retries the build while the orphaned gate container may still be running and writing to /workspace (.venv, caches, test artifacts).  The defect is real. I lowered the severity to medium because the immediate concurrent-build scenario described in the finding does not happen.
2. I could not refute the core defect. The code matches the finding. Sandbox.argv (sandbox.py:94-114) starts `docker run --rm -i` with no --name or --cidfile. SandboxedRunner.run (sandbox.py:165-178) and Sandbox.executor (sandbox.py:130-135) call subprocess.run(..., timeout=...). On TimeoutExpired they only return a failure result. subprocess.run SIGKILLs the docker CLI, and the CLI cannot proxy SIGKILL to the container. The prompt has already gone in through stdin, so closing stdin does not stop claude. The container keeps running with /workspace mounted rw, and --rm only removes it once it exits. Nothing in the code can find it afterwards to kill it.  One part of the scenario is wrong. A timed-out BUILD does not retry immediately. In foreman.py:331-345, only a failed 'gate' step loops back to build; a failed build sets status 'blocked'. The concurrency case still happens on the gate path. If a gate command in executor times out (an `npm ci` or pytest still writing into /workspace/.venv or the tree), foreman retries the build at foreman.py:336-342 while the orphaned gate container is still alive on the same rw mount. An orphaned build container also outlives a 'blocked' item, so a later re-run can overlap with it.  This is a real cleanup defect, but the trigger is narrower than claimed: it needs a timeout first. I would rate it medium rather than high.

### A66. plan_prompt tells change planners to use 'design.md section 3', which in a change design is 'Needs IT approval', not the stack

- **Location:** `src/factory/templates.py:206` · **category:** prompt-correctness · **found:** medium · **verified:** medium · **finder:** gap2-prompt-construction-untrusted-inputs
- **Failure scenario:** A feature change to an app whose whole stack is allowed: section 3 of design.md reads 'Nothing.'. The planner is told to use ONLY the stack in section 3, i.e. an empty set, or, when approval techs exist, only those techs and not the kept stack in section 2. The plan agent gets self-contradictory instructions, and the plan it writes can name or omit stack technologies based on the wrong table.
- **Suggested fix:** Branch the section reference on change_of: section 2 (plus the pre-approved deps subsection) for changes, section 3 for new apps.

**Evidence**

> templates.py:206 'use ONLY the stack in design.md section 3' (and :210). plan_prompt also serves changes: foreman.py:762-767 passes change_of=item.target when item.kind != 'app'. In render_change_design, section 2 is '## 2. Existing stack (kept)' (design.py:289) and section 3 is '## 3. Needs IT approval at this maturity' (design.py:296/299), which often contains just 'Nothing.'

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code says what the finding claims. templates.py:206 tells the planner to "use ONLY the stack in design.md section 3", and :210 says the golden path deps are "listed in design.md section 3". plan_prompt also serves changes: foreman.py passes change_of=item.target when item.kind != "app" (the plan_prompt call in the plan step, around lines 762-767). Nothing in plan_prompt switches the section reference on change_of. In an app design, section 3 really is "## 3. Stack" (design.py:161). In a change design, section 2 is "## 2. Existing stack (kept)" (design.py:289) and section 3 is "## 3. Needs IT approval at this maturity" (design.py:296/299), which reads "Nothing." when no tech needs approval. So for a change, the planner is pointed at the approval list and not the kept stack. Two things soften this. First, the "### Existing dependencies (pre-approved)" subsection sits under heading 3 in the change design, so the line-210 reference to dependencies still lands on the right content. Second, the change design's header says agents must not add a technology not listed in the document, and the agent reads all of design.md. The wrong reference is still a real contradiction in the prompt, not a matter of style. build_prompt (templates.py:224) and line 121 show the same pattern, but whether those are used for changes was not checked here. I lowered severity to low because the effect is a confusing instruction to the plan agent, softened by the rest of the document. It does not break anything deterministically.
2. I could not refute this: the code says what the finding claims. templates.py:206 (plan_prompt) reads "use ONLY the stack in design.md section 3". At :209-210 the prompt says to allow "the golden path dependencies listed in design.md section 3". foreman.py:762-767 calls plan_prompt with change_of=item.target whenever item.kind != "app", and the prompt text does not change with change_of.  In render_change_design, section 2 is "## 2. Existing stack (kept)" (design.py:289) and section 3 is "## 3. Needs IT approval at this maturity" (design.py:296/299), which reads "Nothing." when no approval techs exist. For a new app, section 3 is "## 3. Stack" (design.py:161), so the wording only fits new apps.  One correction to the scenario: section 3 of a change design is never fully empty. The "### Existing dependencies (pre-approved...)" subsection (design.py:305-310) is an h3 under section 3 and lists the declared manifest deps that are not blocked. So the claim that the planner sees "an empty set" is too strong. The real defect is narrower: the kept/allowed tech table in section 2 falls outside "the stack" the planner is told to use. And when approval techs exist, the prompt points at exactly the techs that still need IT approval as the allowed stack, which works against the governance intent.  build_prompt (templates.py:224) has the same "section 3" wording, so the implementer is misdirected the same way. The impact is a misleading LLM instruction, not a crash. The design.md banner ("MUST NOT add a technology not listed here") and the radar gates partly offset it, so medium is a fair rating, and it sits at the lower end.

### A67. Live about-endpoint calibration passes when the judge misses the invention but scores something else at 3

- **Location:** `tests/test_judge_calibration.py:175` · **category:** eval-cannot-fail · **found:** high · **verified:** medium · **finder:** tests
- **Failure scenario:** The judge returns fidelity=5, scope=5 (it did not notice the invented BHV-4) and unambiguity=3. compute_verdict gives `revise`, so the assertion holds and the regression the test is named for (`judge_notices_an_edge_case_the_idea_never_asked_for`) is reported as caught. Same if fidelity/scope are ungrounded (absent from `scores` and defaulted to 5) and another criterion scores 3.
- **Suggested fix:** Assert on the target criteria only: require `fidelity` or `scope` to be grounded and <= 3, and treat their absence as a failure instead of defaulting to 5.

**Evidence**

> `assert min(scores.get("fidelity", 5), scores.get("scope", 5)) <= 3 or rep.verdict == "revise"`. Missing criteria default to 5, and `revise` is reached whenever any criterion scores 3 (judge.py:209-217, compute_verdict).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. The code says exactly what the finding claims. tests/test_judge_calibration.py:175 asserts `min(scores.get("fidelity", 5), scores.get("scope", 5)) <= 3 or rep.verdict == "revise"`. A missing criterion falls back to 5. judge.py:209-217 (compute_verdict, with PASS_MIN=4 at judge.py:63) returns "revise" whenever the minimum score is 3, or the average is below 4 with no score at or below 2. That holds whichever criterion produced it.  So a judge that scores fidelity=5 and scope=5 but gives some other spec criterion a 3 gets "revise". The disjunction is then true and the test passes. The docstring at line 168 says the judge "must not wave this spec through on fidelity or scope", so this result is not what the test intends. Nothing else in the test guards the case.  I am lowering severity to medium. The test is opt-in (@pytest.mark.live, billed), so the hole only makes this calibration regression check unreliable. It does not touch production behavior.
2. I could not refute it. The code says what the finding claims. tests/test_judge_calibration.py:175 asserts `min(scores.get("fidelity", 5), scores.get("scope", 5)) <= 3 or rep.verdict == "revise"`. Missing or ungrounded fidelity/scope default to 5. In src/factory/judge.py:209-217, compute_verdict returns "revise" whenever min(scores) >= 3 and the result is not a pass (PASS_MIN=4, judge.py:63), so a single criterion at 3 is enough. judge.py:326 shows the panel verdict uses the same function on the medians. Concrete case: grounded fidelity=5, scope=5, unambiguity=3 and the rest at 4 or 5 gives verdict "revise", so the test passes even though the judge never flagged the invented BHV-4. The docstring at :168 ("must not wave this spec through on fidelity or scope") and the test name both target fidelity and scope. The oracle is also inconsistent: a "fail" verdict (some other criterion at 2) with fidelity=5 makes the test fail, while "revise" makes it pass, so the `or` clause does not mean "did not pass the spec" either. I am lowering the severity from high. This is an opt-in, billed `@pytest.mark.live` test (:166) for a judge the module itself calls ADVISORY and never a blocking gate (judge.py:1). The weak oracle can only give a false "caught" in a manual calibration run. It cannot let anything through to production.


## Low (111)

### A68. Configured runtime dirs (work, apps) are neither ignored by git nor excluded by ruff when renamed

- **Location:** `.gitignore:7` · **category:** config-mismatch · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap5-config-keys-vs-consumers
- **Failure scenario:** After a build, apps/<slug> is its own git repo (prepare_project) and work/<slug>/ holds item.json with approvals and requester names. A `git add -A` in the factory repo stages apps/<slug> as embedded gitlinks and commits the work items. If IT sets apps_dir = "shipped", `just check` (justfile:9-10, `ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .`) lints generated app code and fails on agent-written files.
- **Suggested fix:** Add work/ and apps/ (and radar.imported.toml, cli.py:502) to .gitignore, and note in factory.toml that renaming work_dir/apps_dir also requires updating pyproject.toml extend-exclude.

**Evidence**

> factory.toml:7-8 `work_dir = "work"`, `apps_dir = "apps"` (config.py:98-99) live inside the factory repo. .gitignore:1-7 ignores .venv, caches, dist and .factory/ only, not work/ or apps/. pyproject.toml:30 `extend-exclude = ["golden_paths", "apps", "work"]` hardcodes the default names instead of following the config.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute the facts in the finding. I checked each one against the remote files. .gitignore:1-7 lists only .venv/, __pycache__/, *.pyc, .pytest_cache/, .ruff_cache/, dist/ and .factory/; it does not ignore work/ or apps/. factory.toml:7-8 sets work_dir="work" and apps_dir="apps", and config.py:98-99 resolves both under the factory root. README.md (Layout section) says apps/<slug> is "each its own git repo once built through Claudo". The repo tree on main tracks no work/ or apps/ path, and nothing in src/factory writes an ignore entry for those directories: the only .gitignore/exclude handling is project.py:123-128, which covers the app's own repo. So running the README demo (`factory intake/run/approve`) leaves both directories untracked in the factory repo, and `git add -A` there would stage the work items plus nested app repos as gitlinks. pyproject.toml:30 hardcodes extend-exclude = ["golden_paths", "apps", "work"], and justfile:9-10 runs `ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .` at the factory root. If apps_dir or work_dir is renamed, generated code would be linted and `just check` would fail. The impact is limited. It only bites on a careless `git add -A` or on a non-default dir name. The project's own gates run inside the app folder and are unaffected. radar.imported.toml only exists after a manual radar-import. So this is real config hygiene, not a functional bug, and low severity fits.
2. I read everything remotely and confirmed the claims. .gitignore:1-7 lists only .venv/, __pycache__/, *.pyc, .pytest_cache/, .ruff_cache/, dist/ and .factory/. It does not list work/ or apps/. factory.toml:7-8 sets work_dir = "work" and apps_dir = "apps", and config.py:98-99 resolves both against the factory root. The repo tree on main tracks nothing under work/ or apps/, so these are pure runtime outputs. README.md:80-81 and 313-314 send the demo's output to work/<slug>/ and apps/<slug>/ ("each its own git repo once built through Claudo"). So after the documented demo, `git status` in the factory repo shows them as untracked, and an unscoped `git add -A` would stage the nested app repos as gitlinks and commit item.json. pyproject.toml:30 hardcodes extend-exclude = ["golden_paths", "apps", "work"], and justfile:9-10 runs `ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .` from the root. A renamed apps_dir/work_dir would therefore pull generated code into `just check`. I found nothing in the code or docs saying these dirs are meant to be committed, so this is not intended behavior. Severity stays low: the git scenario needs a careless manual `git add -A`, and the ruff scenario needs a non-default rename. It is a config-hygiene gap, not a runtime bug.

### A69. PROGRESS states CI never ran and later commits are unpushed; both are stale

- **Location:** `PROGRESS.md:139` · **category:** docs-vs-reality · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** The 'repo is the memory' file misreports what is published and verified.
- **Suggested fix:** Refresh both lines from `git log origin/main` and `gh run list`.

**Evidence**

> PROGRESS.md:139: 'CI workflow written but **never run** (needs a push)'; PROGRESS.md:17-18: '**Not pushed yet**: every commit after it [cebbbf6]'. PROGRESS.md:12 itself says 'CI is green on GitHub'. Remote main is at 3cda4775 (2026-10-09), and `gh run list` shows the last three ci runs on main completed with success.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read PROGRESS.md from remote main through raw.githubusercontent.com. The GitHub API hit its rate limit, so I could not run `gh run list` or the commit log myself. The file still shows both stale claims on its own. Line 139 says 'CI workflow written but **never run** (needs a push)', and line 12 of the same file says '**CI is green** on GitHub (Windows + Ubuntu for the factory)'. Line 17 says 'Pushed and green in GitHub CI on Windows and Ubuntu: `cebbbf6`', which also contradicts line 139. Lines 17-18 say every commit after cebbbf6 is '**Not pushed yet**'. But the copy of PROGRESS.md served from origin/main has a section headed '2026-10-09 (Opus): identity, dependency policy' (line 24), so work after cebbbf6 is on the remote. Line 131 also records that the red commit and its fix were 'squashed into one green commit before the push'. This is a documentation inconsistency only, with no runtime effect, so it stays low severity.
2. I read PROGRESS.md on remote main through raw.githubusercontent.com and confirmed the finding. Line 139 says "CI workflow written but **never run** (needs a push)". Line 12 of the same file says "**CI is green** on GitHub (Windows + Ubuntu for the factory...)", so the file contradicts itself. Lines 17-18 say "Pushed and green in GitHub CI on Windows and Ubuntu: `cebbbf6`. **Not pushed yet**: every commit after it". But `git ls-remote` shows remote main at 3cda47759c68, not cebbbf6. The pushed PROGRESS.md also has a "2026-10-09" section (line 24), which records work done after cebbbf6. So the claim that nothing after cebbbf6 is pushed is false on its face. I could not check `gh run list` myself because the GitHub API is rate-limited (HTTP 403). The contradiction with line 12 is enough on its own to show line 139 is stale. This is a docs-vs-reality defect with no runtime impact, so low severity is right.

### A70. README says drift re-checks the design doc; code deliberately does not

- **Location:** `README.md:242` · **category:** docs-vs-reality · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** A reader expects a design.md naming a now-hold tech to drift; it does not.
- **Suggested fix:** Remove 'design' from README.md:242.

**Evidence**

> README.md:242: '`drift` re-checks every shipped app (manifests, imports, Docker images, design)'. drift.py:123-125 calls check_project(app, radar, item.maturity) with no docs, and its comment says 'never its old design prose'.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it. README.md:242 says `drift` re-checks shipped apps on "(manifests, imports, Docker images, design)". In src/factory/drift.py:123-125, scan_drift calls check_project(app, radar, item.maturity) with no docs argument, and the comment there says it checks "never its old design prose". In guard.py:167, check_project takes docs=None, and only the loop at guard.py:205 (`for doc in docs or []`) scans design docs, so drift never reads design.md. The code omits design on purpose, which makes the README wrong. This is a documentation mismatch only, not a runtime defect, so it stays low severity.
2. I could not refute this finding, so it stands. README.md:241 (the finder cited line 242) says "`drift` re-checks every shipped app (manifests, imports, Docker images, design) against the current radar". In the code, drift.py scan_drift calls `check_project(app, radar, item.maturity)` and passes no `docs` argument. Its own comment says it checks "What the app USES (manifests, imports, images), never its old design prose", and explains that design.md records what was approved at the time. In guard.py:167, check_project only scans design docs when `docs` is given (guard.py:205, `for doc in docs or []`). So drift never reads design documents, and the README claims it does. The defect is limited to the documentation and the code behavior is intended, so severity stays low. GitHub's API was rate-limited, so I read the files through raw.githubusercontent.com instead.

### A71. README contradicts itself on whether migration items are runnable

- **Location:** `README.md:243` · **category:** docs-vs-reality · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** The drift section tells IT that migrations do nothing, while `run` actually builds them on a branch of the shipped app.
- **Suggested fix:** Delete README.md:243-244's stale sentence.

**Evidence**

> README.md:243-244: 'Migration items are tracked, not executed ... `run` refuses to push them through the new-app pipeline.' README.md:123 says '`factory drift --open` now opens migrations that you can simply `run`'. open_migration creates a runnable change via intake_change (foreman.py:289-297).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this. The contradiction is in the README and the code. README.md:243-244 says "Migration items are tracked, not executed: changing an existing app is ROADMAP P2-6, and `run` refuses to push them through the new-app pipeline." That conflicts with README.md:118-123, which describes changes running on a `factory/<slug>` branch and says "`factory drift --open` now opens migrations that you can simply `run`." The code agrees with line 123. In src/factory/foreman.py:289-297, `open_migration` calls `intake_change(..., "migration", requester="radar-drift")`, which saves a normal change WorkItem (foreman.py:275-287). In foreman.py:300-305, `run` does not refuse non-app kinds. It only checks that the target app is still shipped and then builds on it. So the sentence at 243-244 is stale and misleads readers about what `run` does with a migration. Severity stays low because this is a documentation defect only. Note: I read the files through raw.githubusercontent.com with the gh token because the API was rate-limited. Nothing was written to disk.
2. I couldn't refute this. I read README.md and src/factory/foreman.py through raw.githubusercontent.com because the gh API was rate-limited, and both say what the finding claims. README.md:123 says "`factory drift --open` now opens migrations that you can simply `run`". README.md:243-244 says "Migration items are tracked, not executed: changing an existing app is ROADMAP P2-6, and `run` refuses to push them through the new-app pipeline." In the code, foreman.py:289-297 `open_migration` calls `intake_change(..., "migration", requester="radar-drift")`. Line 266 describes intake_change as "A feature, bug fix or migration for an EXISTING shipped app, through the same governed pipeline". I grepped foreman.py for "refuse", "migration" and "P2-6" and found no guard that stops `run` from handling migration items. The only "refuse" hits (lines 459, 794, 1430) concern approval and plan checks. So the sentence at 243-244 is out of date and contradicts both line 123 and the code. This is a documentation inconsistency only, with no runtime impact, so the severity stays low.

### A73. The 'known-good' calibration baseline already invents rules the rubric and spec prompt forbid

- **Location:** `evals/judge_calibration/ping-service/spec.md:14` · **category:** calibration · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap4-llm-artifact-retry-loops
- **Failure scenario:** A correct judge scores the baseline's fidelity at 3 or less. For invented_requirement (targets fidelity, scope) the degraded fidelity cannot drop much further, so the calibration reports 'missed' or the base 'revise' for reasons unrelated to the degradation. test_the_judge_notices_degraded_specs measures noise against a non-faithful reference.
- **Suggested fix:** Regenerate a baseline that adds nothing beyond the idea plus the two mandated items (GET /health, radar invariant).

**Evidence**

> The idea is only 'GET /ping returns {"pong": true}, and GET /version returns {"version": "0.1.0"}' (ping-service/idea.md). The spec adds a '100-request smoke run' and 'p95 latency ... at most 200 ms' (line 14, EVAL-12 line 150). It also adds BHV-4 unknown paths 404, BHV-5 POST returns 405 and BHV-6 query strings (lines 53-68). The fidelity rubric (judge.py:28-32) says to score 3 or less for any invented number/limit, and spec_prompt forbids extra HTTP methods and invented latency limits (templates.py:151-154).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the files remotely and the finding holds. The idea (ping-service/idea.md:9) asks only for GET /ping returning {"pong": true} and GET /version returning {"version": "0.1.0"}. The baseline spec.md adds things the idea never asked for: a 100-request smoke run and a p95 latency of at most 200 ms (line 14, EVAL-12 at line 150), a 404 for unknown paths (BHV-4, lines 53-56 and 147), a 405 for POST (BHV-5, lines 58-61 and 148) and query strings being ignored (BHV-6, line 64).  The rubric at judge.py:28-32 says to score fidelity 3 or less when a spec invents any number, limit or target. Only GET /health and the tech-radar invariant are exempt. templates.py:151-154 forbids extra HTTP methods and near-miss paths, and forbids invented request counts or latency limits in the KPI. Yet test_judge_calibration.py:1 and calibration.py:3 call this fixture a "known-good spec", and test_the_judge_notices_degraded_specs (lines 117-148) uses it as the reference.  The impact is overstated, though. compare() (calibration.py:61-77) takes the largest drop across the target criteria (fidelity, scope) and also needs the average to fall. A baseline fidelity of 3 can still fall to 1 or 2, so "missed" is not certain; the measurement only gets noisier. The baseline would most likely come out "revise", and the test accepts that (line 145).  This is a flawed calibration fixture in an opt-in billed test, not a product defect. I lower the severity to low.
2. The core claim holds in the code. The idea (evals/judge_calibration/ping-service/idea.md) asks only for GET /ping -> {"pong": true} and GET /version -> {"version": "0.1.0"}. The baseline spec.md adds a 100-request smoke run and "p95 latency ... most 200 ms" (spec.md:14, again at EVAL-12, spec.md:150). It also adds BHV-4 unknown path -> 404 (53-56), BHV-5 POST -> 405 (58-62) and BHV-6 query strings ignored (64-68). The fidelity rubric (judge.py:28-32) says to score 3 or less for any invented number or limit. spec_prompt (templates.py:151-154) explicitly forbids extra HTTP methods and invented request counts or latency limits. tests/calibration.py:1-5 and test_judge_calibration.py:1 still call this spec the "known-good" baseline.  The consequence is weaker than the finding says. compare() (tests/calibration.py:72-77) only needs a drop of 1 or more on one target criterion, plus a lower average. A baseline fidelity of 3 can still drop to 2 or 1, and "scope" is also a target. The base-verdict assertion accepts "revise" (test_judge_calibration.py:145). So "missed" is not guaranteed: the calibration is noisier and biased, not broken outright. Impact is limited to the opt-in, billed live calibration test (@pytest.mark.live), not factory runtime. I am lowering the severity to low.

### A74. fullstack-react image: `COPY web ./` copies host node_modules over the container's (no .dockerignore)

- **Location:** `golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:5` · **category:** docker · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** A developer on macOS runs `npm --prefix web ci && npm run dev`, then `docker build .`. web/node_modules with darwin-arm64 native bindings (vite 8 / rolldown, esbuild) overwrites the container's Linux binaries, and `npm run build` (line 6) fails with a missing native binding. Context upload also includes .venv and web/dist.
- **Suggested fix:** Ship a .dockerignore (node_modules, web/node_modules, .venv, web/dist, __pycache__, .git, work/) in each golden path, or COPY only web/src, index.html, tsconfig.json and vite.config.ts.

**Evidence**

> Dockerfile:4 `RUN npm ci` installs Linux node_modules, then line 5 `COPY web ./` copies the whole build context web/ directory. The repo tree has no .dockerignore in any golden path (the full tree listing shows none). The README (line 8) tells developers to run `npm --prefix web ci` locally, which creates web/node_modules.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The code says what the finding says. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:4 runs `npm ci`, then :5 `COPY web ./` copies the whole web/ directory. The recursive tree listing shows no .dockerignore anywhere under golden_paths/fullstack-react. The .gitignore lists node_modules/ and web/dist/, but Docker ignores .gitignore. README.md:8 tells developers to run `npm --prefix web ci`, which creates web/node_modules on the host. So host node_modules, web/dist and .venv all go into the build context, and host node_modules lands on top of the container's /web/node_modules.  The specific breakage is overstated. Docker COPY merges directories and does not delete anything. The Linux-only optional packages (for example @rolldown/binding-linux-*, used by vite ^8.3.4 in package.json) stay in place next to the darwin ones, so `npm run build` failing is not certain. It fails only where a package with the same name holds a platform-specific file that gets overwritten (for example a postinstall-placed native binary), or where the darwin and Linux lockfile resolutions differ. That makes it a real hygiene and reproducibility defect: bloated context, a node_modules tree that mixes host and container installs, and possible build failure. A guaranteed break on macOS is not shown.  Fix: add a .dockerignore, or COPY only the source files.
2. I read the files remotely and the core claim holds. In golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile, line 4 runs `npm ci` and line 5 does `COPY web ./`. The recursive tree has no .dockerignore in any golden path, and grepping every .py file in the repo for "dockerignore" found nothing, so the factory never generates one. The path's .gitignore lists node_modules/ and web/dist/, but Docker ignores .gitignore. README.md line 8 tells developers to run `npm --prefix web ci`, which creates web/node_modules on the host. As a result, host node_modules, web/dist and other local files end up in the build stage.  The failure scenario is overstated, though. Docker COPY merges directory trees rather than replacing them. The container's Linux-specific optional binding packages, such as @rolldown/binding-linux-*, stay next to the host's darwin ones. Rolldown in vite ^8.3.4 would probably still find its Linux binding, so "npm run build fails with a missing native binding" is not guaranteed. Breakage happens only where a file at the same path is platform-specific. One example is a postinstall-replaced binary like esbuild's bin/esbuild, if it is in the tree. Another is a host lock or install that has drifted from the container's.  So this is a real hazard: host artifacts can overwrite container-installed files, the build context is bloated, and `npm ci` stops being reproducible. The deterministic build failure as described is not established, so I am lowering the severity.

### A75. All template images run as root

- **Location:** `golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:16` · **category:** docker/security · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** Every factory-built app serves HTTP as uid 0. An RCE in the app or a dependency gets root inside the container, can write /srv (including the venv `uv run` re-syncs at start) and fails clusters with a runAsNonRoot policy.
- **Suggested fix:** Create an unprivileged user (e.g. `RUN useradd -r -u 10001 app && chown -R app /srv`) and set `USER app` before CMD.

**Evidence**

> None of fullstack-react/Dockerfile (1-16), python-fastapi/Dockerfile (1-8) or python-worker/Dockerfile (1-8) has a USER directive. python:3.12-slim defaults to root.

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from code read remotely. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile lines 8-16 (runtime stage FROM python:3.12-slim, CMD at line 16), golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile lines 1-8 and golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile lines 1-8 have no USER directive, so the container process runs as the base image default, root. The template READMEs and golden.toml contain no mention of root, USER or Docker hardening, so this is not documented intended behavior. One caveat: the failure needs a separate RCE before root matters, and nothing in the repo shows a runAsNonRoot deployment target. That makes this a hardening gap more than a functional bug, so I lower the severity from medium to low.
2. Confirmed from the code. None of the three Dockerfiles has a USER directive: golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile lines 8-16, golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile lines 1-8 and golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile lines 1-8. Each final stage is python:3.12-slim, which runs as root by default. So the uvicorn process started at fullstack-react/Dockerfile:16 (and line 8 in the other two) runs as uid 0, and /srv and its venv (from `uv sync --no-dev` at line 12, or line 5 in the others) are owned by root and writable by it. I found nothing that intends or documents root, and no deploy manifest in the tree sets securityContext or runAsUser. Code search returned nothing, but that may only be search lag. The severity is overstated, though. This is defense-in-depth hardening, not a bug you can exploit by itself: it only matters after a separate RCE. The runAsNonRoot failure only happens on clusters that enforce that policy, and I found no such target in the repo. I did not verify the claim that `uv run` re-syncs the venv at start, so it is not established. Corrected to low.

### A76. Template CI uses `uv sync` without --locked, so it can test different versions than the uv.lock the factory gates

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10` · **category:** ci · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** A developer bumps pyproject.toml without relocking. CI silently re-resolves and passes, while uv.lock (what the radar's version constraints were checked against) is stale. The root workflow does use `uv sync --locked` (.github/workflows/ci.yml:17), so the templates are less strict than the factory itself.
- **Suggested fix:** Use `uv sync --locked` in the three template workflows.

**Evidence**

> python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10, python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10 and fullstack-react/.github/workflows/ci.yml:13 run `uv sync`. Apps carry a uv.lock (project.py lock_dependencies, foreman.py:1474), and the dependencies gate checks the versions locked there (gates.py:123-128).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I checked each claim against the code and they all hold. golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10 and golden_paths/python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10 run a plain `uv sync`, and so does golden_paths/fullstack-react/.github/workflows/ci.yml:13. The root .github/workflows/ci.yml:17 runs `uv sync --locked`. New apps get a uv.lock before their first commit: foreman.py:1473-1476 calls self.locker, which is project.py:430 lock_dependencies and runs `uv lock`. dependencies_gate (gates.py:124-130) checks the versions locked in uv.lock against the radar. So if someone edits pyproject.toml without relocking, the template CI re-resolves and can pass on versions the radar never saw, while uv.lock stays stale. `--locked` would fail in that case. The impact is CI hygiene only, so low severity is right. One caveat for the fix: lock_dependencies can fail and still log the scaffold (foreman.py:1476), which leaves an app with no uv.lock. Plain `uv sync` tolerates that today. I did not check whether `uv sync --locked` fails when no uv.lock exists.
2. I confirmed it from the code. All three template workflows run a bare `uv sync`: golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10, python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10 and fullstack-react/.github/workflows/ci.yml:13. The factory's own workflow runs `uv sync --locked` (.github/workflows/ci.yml:17). New apps get a uv.lock before their first commit (project.py:430-436 lock_dependencies, called at foreman.py:1474-1476). The dependencies gate checks the versions locked in uv.lock (gates.py:124-130, docstring "Versions locked in uv.lock against the radar's constraints"). Without --locked, a stale lock does not fail CI. uv re-resolves and rewrites uv.lock on the runner, so CI can test versions that differ from the committed lock the gate approved. This is a real gap, not style. Severity stays low: it needs the developer to edit pyproject.toml without relocking, and the factory gate would still re-check the committed lock at the next delivery. One caveat: if `uv lock` fails at scaffold time, the factory only logs it (foreman.py:1476) and the app ships without a uv.lock. In that case `--locked` would make template CI fail, so the fix should take that path into account.

### A77. Base images and uv binary unpinned (floating tags, `uv:latest`)

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:2` · **category:** docker/supply-chain · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** Two builds of the same commit produce different images. A breaking or compromised `uv:latest` changes the installer of every app with no diff in the repo, and the 'IT-approved template' is not reproducible for the audit trail the factory claims.
- **Suggested fix:** Pin uv to a version (ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:0.x.y) and pin base images by digest (python:3.12-slim@sha256:...), updated through Renovate/Dependabot.

**Evidence**

> python-fastapi/Dockerfile:2 and python-worker/Dockerfile:2 `COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest`, fullstack-react/Dockerfile:9 the same. `FROM python:3.12-slim` (python-fastapi:1, python-worker:1, fullstack-react:8) and `FROM node:24-slim` (fullstack-react:1) have no digest.

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed in code I read. golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:1 is `FROM python:3.12-slim` and :2 is `COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest /uv /usr/local/bin/uv`. python-worker/Dockerfile has the same two lines at 1 and 2. fullstack-react/Dockerfile:1 is `FROM node:24-slim AS web`, :8 is `FROM python:3.12-slim` and :9 is the same `uv:latest` COPY. None of them carries a digest or a version pin. The repo tree has no renovate or dependabot config, and the README's dependency policy (line 41) covers app dependency versions from the radar, not base images. So two builds of the same commit can use a different uv binary and different base layers. Nothing guards against that and no doc says it is intended. The defect is real hygiene/reproducibility, but no concrete breakage follows from these lines alone: the compromise scenario is hypothetical, and pyproject-only `uv sync` (no lockfile copied) is a separate and larger source of drift. Low is a fairer severity than medium.
2. I could not refute it; the code says what the finding claims. I read these lines remotely: golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:1-2, golden_paths/python-worker/Dockerfile:1-2 and golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:1,8,9. Lines 2, 2 and 9 use `COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest`. `FROM python:3.12-slim` and `FROM node:24-slim` carry no digest. The trees under golden_paths have no renovate or dependabot config. The README (lines 202-205) says what ships is "exactly the commit IT approved". That binds the commit, not the built image, so two builds of the approved commit can still differ.  I lowered the severity because the impact is smaller than stated. The python-fastapi template has no uv.lock (its tree listing has only pyproject.toml; the only uv.lock is at the repo root). Its Dockerfile runs `uv sync --no-dev` from pyproject.toml alone (line 5), so the app's Python dependencies already float on every build. Pinning uv and the base images would not make the image reproducible by itself. These are scaffolding templates, and the gap is supply-chain hardening, not a runtime bug. That makes it a valid but low-severity finding.

### A78. Production Dockerfiles ignore uv.lock, so the image ships dependency versions the dependencies gate never checked

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:4` · **category:** supply-chain · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** security
- **Failure scenario:** A package that the radar version constraint or licence policy would reject gets published as the newest release. The gate passed the locked version, but `docker build` resolves fresh and ships the new one into production.
- **Suggested fix:** `COPY pyproject.toml uv.lock ./` then `uv sync --locked --no-dev`; pin the uv image by version or digest; use `uv sync --locked` in the golden-path CI.

**Evidence**

> golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:2 `COPY --from=ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest` (unpinned) and :4-5 `COPY pyproject.toml ./` / `RUN uv sync --no-dev` (no uv.lock copied, no --locked). fullstack-react/Dockerfile:9-12 is the same. The dependencies gate checks versions in uv.lock (policy.py:75-85, gates.py:123-129). The golden-path CI also runs plain `uv sync` (golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read every cited file remotely and the finding holds. golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:2 copies uv from `ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest`, which is unpinned. Lines 4-5 copy only pyproject.toml and then run `uv sync --no-dev`. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:9 and :11-12 do the same. Since uv.lock never enters the build context, uv resolves the dependencies fresh at build time. The factory does create the lock: foreman.py:139 sets `locker = lock_dependencies` and foreman.py:1476 reports "uv.lock created with the scaffold". The dependencies gate then judges that lockfile and nothing else. gates.py:125-126 returns "not applicable" when uv.lock is absent, and policy.py:75-80 / 123-133 compare the radar constraints against the versions in uv.lock. So the gate checks one set of versions and docker build may install another. Nothing in the repo prevents this: the golden-path .gitignore does not cover the Dockerfile issue, there is no `--locked`/`--frozen` anywhere I searched, and golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:10 runs plain `uv sync`. The scenario needs a new upstream release that breaks a radar version or licence rule, so low severity is right.
2. The finding holds; I read every file it cites. golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:2 copies ghcr.io/astral-sh/uv:latest, which is unpinned. Lines 4-5 copy only pyproject.toml and then run `uv sync --no-dev`: no uv.lock is copied and there is no --locked. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:9,11-12 does the same. The web stage of that file does copy package-lock.json and runs `npm ci`, so only the Python stage has the problem. The template pyproject.toml:6-10 declares open-ended lower bounds (fastapi>=0.115, uvicorn>=0.30, pydantic>=2.7). A lockless sync inside the image therefore picks up the newest releases at build time. The gate checks something else: dependencies_gate (gates.py:123-129) only judges versions read from uv.lock (policy.py:75-85, 131-133), and it reads licences from the local .venv (policy.py:93-117). So whatever the gate approved is not necessarily what docker build installs. The golden-path CI also runs a plain `uv sync` (ci.yml:10). I found no other guard. Severity low is right: it is a supply-chain drift risk, not a direct exploit.

### A79. Golden path Dockerfile ignores uv.lock, so the image does not ship the versions the dependencies gate checked

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:4` · **category:** docs-vs-reality · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** The dependencies gate passes fastapi 0.115.x from uv.lock, but the delivered image resolves the newest fastapi at build time, outside the version IT pinned in the radar.
- **Suggested fix:** COPY uv.lock and run `uv sync --locked --no-dev` in the golden path Dockerfiles.

**Evidence**

> Dockerfile:4-5 runs `COPY pyproject.toml ./` then `RUN uv sync --no-dev`, without uv.lock or --locked; fullstack-react/Dockerfile:11-12 does the same. README.md:41-43 says versions are checked 'against the version actually locked in uv.lock'.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding. The code says what the finding claims.  1. golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:4-5 runs `COPY pyproject.toml ./` and then `RUN uv sync --no-dev`. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:11-12 does the same. Neither copies uv.lock or passes --locked or --frozen.  2. The golden path's pyproject.toml (lines 7-9) only sets floors: `fastapi>=0.115`, `uvicorn>=0.30`, `pydantic>=2.7`. Without a lockfile in the build context, `uv sync` resolves the newest versions available at build time.  3. Apps do have a uv.lock. In src/factory/foreman.py, `_build_with_claudo` calls `self.locker(app)` before the first commit and logs "uv.lock created with the scaffold".  4. src/factory/gates.py:123-128 (`dependencies_gate`) checks the radar constraints against that uv.lock.  5. README.md:41-44 says versions are checked against what is "actually locked in uv.lock". The image never uses that file.  So the versions the gate approved and the versions shipped in the image can differ. Example: the radar pins `fastapi <0.120`, the lock holds 0.115 and the gate passes, but a later Docker build installs fastapi 0.12x. Nothing elsewhere in the code guards against this (the golden path has no uv.lock and no .dockerignore). It is a real reproducibility and governance gap, not a style point. The severity stays low: it only happens at image build time, and the gate's own check is still correct.  Limit: GitHub API rate limits blocked the full tree listing, so I could not check every path. I read all the files above remotely through raw.githubusercontent.com.
2. I confirmed the finding from code I read. golden_paths/python-fastapi/Dockerfile:4-5 runs only `COPY pyproject.toml ./` and then `RUN uv sync --no-dev`. golden_paths/fullstack-react/Dockerfile:11-12 does the same. Neither copies uv.lock or passes --locked or --frozen. When uv.lock is missing from the build context, `uv sync` resolves the dependencies again at build time.  The app does have a lockfile. lock_dependencies (src/factory/scaffold.py:430-452) runs `uv lock` in each new app before its first commit, and foreman.py:1474-1476 calls it. The dependencies gate (src/factory/gates.py:124-126) then checks the versions in that app's uv.lock. README.md:41-43 says radar version pins are checked "against the version actually locked in uv.lock".  So the image installs whatever is newest inside the pyproject ranges, not the versions the gate checked. That is a real gap between what the gate approves and what ships. The finding stretches one point: the README never claims anything about the Docker image. The defect itself stands, and low severity fits.

### A80. golden.toml `techs` omit technologies the templates ship, so a held tech does not stop the template from being offered

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-fastapi/golden.toml:8` · **category:** manifest-consistency · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** IT moves httpx to `hold` (as it did for requests, radar.toml:153-160). pick_golden_path still offers python-fastapi. _scaffold copies a pyproject with `httpx>=0.27`, and the radar gate (guard.py:198-203) then blocks the factory's own scaffold. The item fails its gates on code no agent wrote, and the design compiler already listed httpx as 'pre-approved by the golden path' (design.py:169-176).
- **Suggested fix:** Derive techs from the template's manifests (pyproject, package.json, Dockerfile FROM) at load time, or add a test that every dependency found by scan_project(golden_path) maps to an id in golden.toml techs.

**Evidence**

> python-fastapi/golden.toml:8 `techs = ["python", "fastapi", "pytest", "ruff"]`, but pyproject.toml:9,13 declares pydantic and httpx and the template ships a Dockerfile and GitHub Actions (radar ids docker, github-actions). python-worker/golden.toml:7 has the same omission. fullstack-react/golden.toml:5 also lacks pydantic, httpx, docker and github-actions. pick_golden_path (design.py:387-389) filters only on gp.techs.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code confirms it. golden_paths/python-fastapi/golden.toml:3-4 states the contract: `techs` lists the radar ids the template uses, and if any of them is blocked the template is not offered. Line 8 lists only python, fastapi, pytest and ruff. The template's own pyproject.toml declares pydantic>=2.7 (line 9) and httpx>=0.27 (line 13), and both are radar ids (radar.toml:133 pydantic, :147 httpx). The template also ships a Dockerfile and .github/workflows/ci.yml, which map to radar.toml:188 docker and :180 github-actions. python-worker/golden.toml:7 and fullstack-react/golden.toml:5 leave out the same ids. pick_golden_path (design.py:386-389) filters only on `radar.get(t) for t in gp.techs`, so moving httpx to hold does not remove python-fastapi from the choice. check_project (guard.py:177-203) scans the manifests, resolves httpx through radar.find, and records a BLOCK verdict. design.py:169-176 still lists the golden deps as "pre-approved by IT". Tests do not catch this: test_golden_paths.py:39-41 only checks that templates pass today's radar, and :59-62 only exercises a tech that is listed (react). No test checks that the deps found by scanning a template are a subset of its golden.toml techs. Severity is medium or lower: the failure needs IT to change a ring, and when it happens it shows up as a gate failure, not a silent one. It is still a real mismatch between the manifest contract and the template.
2. I could not refute it; the code confirms it. golden_paths/python-fastapi/golden.toml:3-4 states the contract: techs are the radar ids the template uses, and if any is blocked the template is not offered. Line 8 lists only python, fastapi, pytest, ruff. The same template's pyproject.toml declares pydantic (line 9) and httpx (line 13), its Dockerfile:1 is FROM python, and it ships .github/workflows/ci.yml. pydantic, httpx, github-actions and docker are all radar ids (radar.toml:133, 147, 180, 188). pick_golden_path (design.py:387-388) checks only gp.techs, so a hold or block on httpx or pydantic would not stop the template from being offered. check_project (guard.py:177-203) resolves every scanned manifest dependency through radar.find, then would block the scaffold the factory produced itself. The design section would still label those dependencies as "pre-approved" (design.py:169-176). python-worker/golden.toml:7 and fullstack-react/golden.toml:5 have the same omission. Downgraded to low: every one of these ids is "adopt" today (radar.toml:136, 150, 183, 191), so nothing fails now. It takes a future radar change to trigger, and the failure would be a loud gate block, not a silent wrong result.

### A81. python-worker CI header names the wrong golden path

- **Location:** `golden_paths/python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:1` · **category:** consistency · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** golden
- **Failure scenario:** Every worker app ships a CI file claiming to be the python-fastapi standard, which misleads IT when checking which template an app came from. The file is also identical to the FastAPI one, so it has no worker-specific check (e.g. image build).
- **Suggested fix:** Correct the header to python-worker.

**Evidence**

> python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:1 `# IT standard CI (golden path python-fastapi). Do not edit per app.`, copied from python-fastapi.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I confirmed this from the code. golden_paths/python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:1 reads "# IT standard CI (golden path python-fastapi). Do not edit per app.", and the file matches golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml line for line (lines 1-12). That header is wrong for this template, which golden.toml:4 names "python-worker" and README.md:3 describes as "the IT golden path `python-worker`". So every worker app generated from the template carries a CI file that names the wrong source template.  The impact is cosmetic only. I found nothing that parses the comment: a code search for ci.yml returned nothing, and grepping foreman.py for the header text found no match. The finding's second claim, that the worker CI lacks a worker-specific check such as an image build, is not a defect. The FastAPI CI builds no image either, and the worker's justfile (test, evals, lint) lines up with the ruff and pytest steps in the CI. The mislabeled header is real but only low severity.
2. Confirmed but cosmetic. golden_paths/python-worker/.github/workflows/ci.yml:1 reads "# IT standard CI (golden path python-fastapi). Do not edit per app." It is byte-for-byte the same as golden_paths/python-fastapi/.github/workflows/ci.yml:1-12, so the worker template names the wrong golden path. No code reads this header. I grepped src/ and tests/ for ci.yml, "IT standard" and .github, and the only hit is src/factory/templates.py:228, which tells agents to leave .github/ untouched. That means the mislabel has no runtime effect, but it does get copied into every worker app. The second claim, that a worker-specific check such as an image build is missing, is an opinion: the README and Dockerfile do not show that a CI image build is required, and uv sync, ruff and pytest run fine for the worker layout. So this is a valid low-severity comment mislabel, not a behavioral bug.

### A82. Radar aliases miss real package names: npm `angular` (AngularJS, hold) and langchain-* packages (assess) fall to 'unknown'

- **Location:** `radar.toml:83` · **category:** wrong-comparison · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** package.json `"angular": "1.8.3"` at MVP gives 'needs_it_approval' (not on radar) instead of BLOCK, and IT can clear it with `factory allow ... angular`. A langchain-community-only app at MVP is likewise approvable although assess-ring tech is BLOCK at mvp.
- **Suggested fix:** Add `angular` and the langchain-* family to the match lists. Consider prefix matching for families (langchain-*).

**Evidence**

> radar.toml:83 `match = ["angularjs", "angular.js"]`, but the npm package for AngularJS 1.x is `angular`. radar.toml:216 `match = ["langchain", "langchain-core", "langgraph"]` misses langchain-community/langchain-openai. guard.py:198-203 maps an unmatched manifest name to verdict(None) = UNKNOWN_POLICY (radar.py:30): APPROVAL at pov/poc/mvp instead of BLOCK.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code confirms the finding. radar.toml:83 has `match = ["angularjs", "angular.js"]`, and no other entry contains "angular" (I grepped the file and only lines 79, 80 and 83 match). radar.toml:216 lists only `langchain`, `langchain-core` and `langgraph`. Radar.find (radar.py, `find`) does an exact lookup on the normalized name (`self._by_name.get(normalize(name))`), with no prefix or family matching. So npm `angular` and `langchain-community` / `langchain-openai` (or the import `langchain_community`) all return None. In guard.py:198-203 a manifest dependency with tech None is not skipped (only an import is skipped, at line 199), so verdict(None, maturity) returns UNKNOWN_POLICY, which radar.py:30 sets to APPROVAL at pov/poc/mvp. The hold ring (BLOCK everywhere) and the assess ring (BLOCK at mvp) are therefore bypassed: those apps get needs_it_approval with the hint "not on the tech radar". I lowered the severity to low for two reasons. radar.toml is an IT-owned sample data file (company = "Example Corp"), fixed by adding aliases. And the outcome is still an IT-approval gate rather than a silent allow, so a human still sees the package before it ships.
2. I could not refute this; the code confirms it. radar.toml:83 lists only "angularjs" and "angular.js" for the hold-ring AngularJS entry. Since modern Angular ships as @angular/*, the npm package `angular` is AngularJS 1.x. radar.toml:216 lists only "langchain", "langchain-core" and "langgraph". radar.py:98-100 `find` is an exact lookup on the normalized name, and radar.py:37-39 maps langchain_community to "langchain-community", which has no alias. In guard.py:198-203, a declared dependency with no match gets verdict(None), and radar.py:113-114 returns UNKNOWN_POLICY. At mvp, radar.py:30 gives APPROVAL. The alternatives would be BLOCK for hold at mvp (radar.py:27) and BLOCK for assess at mvp (radar.py:26). The design-doc text scan does not catch these names either: the regex at radar.py:88 requires (?![\w-]), so "langchain-community" and "langchain_community" do not match "langchain". Unmatched imports are skipped entirely (guard.py:199-200). I am lowering the severity because the gap is in the sample, IT-maintained data file ("Example Corp", radar.toml:13), not in the policy code. The outcome is also needs_it_approval, so a human gate remains; it is not a silent ALLOW. It is still a real governance downgrade (BLOCK becomes approvable), so I rate it low to medium. I settled on low.

### A83. Radar alias 'motor' (MongoDB, hold) matches the plain English word in design docs and blocks the build

- **Location:** `radar.toml:128` · **category:** false-positive · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** An idea about motor insurance whose design.md contains the word 'motor' fails the radar gate with MongoDB on hold, at every maturity.
- **Suggested fix:** Set text_strict = true for mongodb (as for requests), or drop 'motor' from the free-text aliases.

**Evidence**

> radar.toml:128 `match = ["mongodb", "mongo", "pymongo", "motor"]` without text_strict. radar.py:88 compiles a plain word pattern for it. guard.py:210-211 scans design.md text and records the tech with verdict BLOCK (hold is BLOCK at every rung).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it; the code confirms the finding. radar.toml:123-129 gives mongodb the aliases ["mongodb", "mongo", "pymongo", "motor"] with ring = "hold" and no text_strict; the only text_strict entry is requests, at radar.toml:160. In radar.py, a tech without text_strict gets the plain pattern `(?<![\w-])(?:aliases)(?![\w-])` with re.I, so scan_text returns MongoDB for any text containing the word "motor". POLICY sets hold to BLOCK at pov, poc, mvp and prod. guard.py:205-211 scans design.md with only the radar:ignore regions removed and records verdict(tech, maturity). The title is written outside any ignore block: design.py:140 writes `# Design: {title}`, and the radar:ignore blocks are at lines 188-195. So a title such as "Motor insurance quotes" counts. In foreman.py, after store.write(design.md) comes _design_report, and any BLOCK violation returns False with "design violates the radar". The build is therefore blocked at every maturity. The idea text is also passed to radar.scan_text(item.idea) as `mentioned`, which can affect stack choice. A narrow trigger (the word "motor" in the title or design text), so the severity stays low.
2. I could not refute it; the code confirms the finding. radar.toml:127-128 puts MongoDB on ring "hold" with match ["mongodb", "mongo", "pymongo", "motor"]. Unlike requests (radar.toml:160), it has no text_strict. Without text_strict, radar.py:86-89 builds a plain word-boundary pattern `(?<![\w-])(?:...|motor)(?![\w-])` with re.I, so a bare "motor" or "Motor" in prose matches. scan_text (radar.py:102-104) returns MongoDB. guard.py:205-211 runs scan_text on every doc and records the result with verdict(tech, maturity). POLICY["hold"] (radar.py:27) is BLOCK at pov, poc, mvp and prod. guard.py:215-222 then turns that into a BLOCK violation. foreman.py:657 calls check_project with docs=[d / "design.md"], and gates.py:113 and cli.py:330 also pass docs, so a design.md for a motor-insurance idea fails at every maturity. Two mitigations exist but require manual action: the `<!-- radar:ignore -->` block stripping (guard.py:19, 208) and the exceptions argument to blocking() (guard.py:150). Neither keeps the scan from matching the everyday word. The same over-match applies to "mongo", but "motor" is the realistic false positive. Low severity is right.

### A84. parse_claude_json reports success for exit 0 with empty or non-JSON stdout

- **Location:** `src/factory/agents.py:76` · **category:** exit-codes · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** The container exits 0 without running claude (e.g. an image entrypoint that ignores the command, or a wrapper that swallows the exit code). stdout is '' or plain text, AgentResult.ok is True, and _do_build logs 'built by agent ($0.00)' and goes on to the gates as if a build had happened.
- **Suggested fix:** With `--output-format json`, treat unparsable or empty stdout as a failure even with returncode 0.

**Evidence**

> agents.py:73-76: `if not isinstance(data, dict): if returncode != 0: return AgentResult(False, ...); return AgentResult(True, stdout)`.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The code does what the finding says. src/factory/agents.py:68-76: when json.loads fails or the result is not a dict, a returncode of 0 returns AgentResult(True, stdout) with cost 0.0. Both callers always ask for JSON output: claude_argv at agents.py:55-56 passes --output-format json. ClaudeRunner.run (agents.py:130) and SandboxedRunner.run (sandbox.py:179, which wraps the command in docker through self.sandbox.argv) both send their output to parse_claude_json. In foreman.py, _do_build checks only `if not result.ok` and then writes build-summary.md and returns "built by agent ($0.00)". _do_build_change does the same and then commits. Nothing upstream checks for empty stdout. The test at tests/test_cli_agents.py:118-123 covers only valid JSON and a non-zero crash, so it does not show this behavior is intended. The trigger needs a misbehaving wrapper or entrypoint that exits 0 without printing JSON. That is uncommon, so the severity stays low. Downstream gates might still catch a missing build, but the scaffold alone could pass them. The success report is still wrong.
2. I couldn't refute this one; the code does what the finding says. In src/factory/agents.py:68-76, `json.loads` fails on '' or plain text, so `data` becomes None. With returncode 0 the function then reaches `return AgentResult(True, stdout)` at line 76: ok=True, cost 0.0, no error. Every call passes `--output-format json` (agents.py:55-56), so stdout that won't parse is never a normal result. Both runners send their output straight to this function: ClaudeRunner at agents.py:130 and SandboxedRunner, the docker container, at sandbox.py:179. In foreman.py:1374-1378 (_do_build), the only check is `if not result.ok`. The function then writes an empty build-summary.md and returns `True, "...; built by agent ($0.00)"`. _do_build_change does the same at foreman.py:981-986 and also reports "built by agent". The unit test (tests/test_cli_agents.py:118-124) covers only valid JSON and the rc!=0 crash, never rc 0 with non-JSON output, so no test documents line 76 as intended. It stays low severity because the trigger is speculative: the claude CLI with --output-format json normally exits non-zero or prints JSON. The failure needs an abnormal wrapper or entrypoint, and later gates may still catch an app that was scaffolded but never built.

### A85. A timed-out agent or judge call records $0 cost

- **Location:** `src/factory/agents.py:129` · **category:** cost-accounting · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap4-llm-artifact-retry-loops
- **Failure scenario:** A spec/plan/build agent or a judge vote runs to the timeout (1800 s default) after consuming tokens. item.cost_usd and the panel's cost_usd add 0, so the billed spend of the exhausted attempt is missing from telemetry.
- **Suggested fix:** Mark the cost unknown (e.g. a cost_unknown flag or estimated tokens) instead of 0.0, and surface it in the item log.

**Evidence**

> `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired: return AgentResult(False, "", 0.0, f"claude timed out after {self.timeout}s")`. Callers add result.cost_usd (foreman.py:579, 598, 1383; judge.py:290).

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding holds. src/factory/agents.py:128-129 catches subprocess.TimeoutExpired and returns AgentResult(False, "", 0.0, ...). The CLI is killed before it can write its --output-format json result, so total_cost_usd, which parse_claude_json reads at agents.py:77, is never available. Nothing else estimates the cost, and no flag marks it as unknown. The callers add the value without any timeout handling: foreman.py:579, 598, 989 and 1383 (item.cost_usd += result.cost_usd), and judge.py:290/303 (report.cost_usd = result.cost_usd, then summed for the panel). The spend of a call that ran to the 1800 s default timeout (agents.py:87) is therefore recorded as $0. That figure is reported to the user at foreman.py:1173 ("Cost: $...") and in the "built by agent ($...)" messages. The impact is telemetry only: I found no budget gate that reads item.cost_usd (claudo_budget_usd at foreman.py:1452 is a separate Claudo budget), so low severity is right. Nothing in the code or comments says the $0 is intentional.
2. I confirmed this from the code. In src/factory/agents.py, ClaudeRunner.run calls subprocess.run with timeout=self.timeout (default 1800, set in __init__ at line 87). The except subprocess.TimeoutExpired handler at line 129 returns AgentResult(False, "", 0.0, "claude timed out after ...s"). The cost normally comes only from total_cost_usd in the CLI's JSON stdout (parse_claude_json, line 77). On a timeout the child process is killed and that JSON is never read, so the call reports cost 0.0 even though the tokens were already used and billed. The callers add this value directly: foreman.py lines 579, 598, 989 and 1383 do item.cost_usd += result.cost_usd. judge.py line 290 sets report.cost_usd from result.cost_usd, and line 303 sums the reports. No cost_unknown flag exists and no comment says the 0.0 is intended. So item and panel cost totals (for example the "Cost: $" line at foreman.py:1173) undercount every timed-out attempt. The real cost cannot be known exactly after a kill, so this is a telemetry and accounting gap, not a functional failure. Low severity fits.

### A86. trajectory() lets TimeoutExpired escape, and sign_approval has no timeout

- **Location:** `src/factory/claudo.py:219` · **category:** timeouts · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** On a large journal, or a hung guard, trajectory_guard.py exceeds 60 s. subprocess.TimeoutExpired propagates out of run_gates and the CLI (not caught at cli.py:559), the item is not saved and the gate report is lost. If approvals.py blocks (e.g. on a lock), `factory approve --as it` hangs with no bound.
- **Suggested fix:** Catch TimeoutExpired in trajectory() and return (False, 'trajectory guard timed out'). Give sign_approval a timeout and turn TimeoutExpired into EngineError.

**Evidence**

> trajectory: `subprocess.run(..., timeout=timeout, ...)` with no try (claudo.py:219-229). _trajectory_gate calls it directly (foreman.py:1637). sign_approval: `subprocess.run([... approvals.py sign ...])` with no timeout and no cwd (claudo.py:195-210).

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from the code. claudo.py:219-229 calls subprocess.run(..., timeout=timeout) with no try/except, so subprocess.TimeoutExpired is raised to the caller. foreman.py:1692-1696 (_trajectory_gate, not 1637 as the finding says) calls self.engine.trajectory() directly. That call goes through the `extra` lambda at foreman.py:1712, and gates.py:156-157 (run_gates) calls `extra[gate]()` with no exception handling. TimeoutExpired is a SubprocessError, which is not among the exceptions caught at cli.py:559 (ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError, KeyError). It therefore escapes as an uncaught traceback, before store.write of gate-report.md (foreman.py, right after run_gates) and before the item is saved. sign_approval (claudo.py:195-210) has no timeout argument, and its caller at foreman.py:1603-1606 catches only EngineError, so a hung approvals.py blocks with no bound. The tests file test_gates_immutable_trajectory.py contains no timeout case. Minor inaccuracy in the citation: the foreman line is 1695, not 1637. The defect itself holds, and low severity fits: it needs a hung or slow guard, and the effect is a crash or hang, not wrong approval.
2. I could not refute this finding. The code shows both defects as described.  1. **The trajectory timeout escapes.** `Claudo.trajectory` (src/factory/claudo.py:215-230) calls `subprocess.run(..., timeout=timeout, ...)` with timeout defaulting to 60 s, inside no try block. `Foreman._trajectory_gate` (foreman.py:1634-1638) calls it unguarded. `run_gates` (gates.py:156-157) calls `extra[gate]()` unguarded too, from `Foreman._do_gate` (foreman.py:1643-1657). `format_report` is only written to gate-report.md at foreman.py:1658, after `run_gates` returns, so a timeout means no gate report. `cli.main` (cli.py around 559) catches only ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError and KeyError, so `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` reaches the user as a traceback. This is an oversight, not a design choice: the repo's other subprocess calls catch TimeoutExpired (gates.py:80, claudo.py:128, claudo.py:344, agents.py:128, delivery.py:82, sandbox.py:39/134/175).  2. **The signing call has no timeout.** `sign_approval` (claudo.py:189-213) calls `subprocess.run` with no timeout. Its caller (foreman.py:1548-1549) catches only EngineError, so if approvals.py hangs, the approve command blocks with no time limit.  Not verified: whether the work item save is skipped. I did not read the code that calls `_do_gate` and saves the item.  Severity stays low: the failure needs a slow or hung guard script, and it ends in a crash or a hang, not in wrong data.

### A87. radar-import crashes with a traceback on a missing file or a non-UTF-8 (French Excel cp1252) CSV

- **Location:** `src/factory/cli.py:259` · **category:** cli-argument-validation · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** `factory radar-import radar.csv` on an Excel-exported file containing 'Évaluer' gives a UnicodeDecodeError traceback instead of an actionable error. The same happens on a typo in the path.
- **Suggested fix:** Catch OSError and UnicodeDecodeError and fall back to `utf-8-sig`, then `cp1252`, with a warning, or raise FactoryError with guidance.

**Evidence**

> cli.py:259 `source.read_text(encoding="utf-8")` is not guarded. FileNotFoundError and UnicodeDecodeError are not in cli.py:559's except tuple `(ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError, KeyError)`. The importer's own docstring (importer.py:73) targets French Excel, whose default 'CSV (séparateur: point-virgule)' export is cp1252.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code remotely and could not refute this finding. cli.py:259 calls `source.read_text(encoding="utf-8")` inside cmd_radar_import with no try/except around it. main() at cli.py:559 catches only (ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError, KeyError). FileNotFoundError, which is an OSError, and UnicodeDecodeError, which is a ValueError, both escape that tuple. So a path typo, or a cp1252-encoded CSV containing a byte such as 0xC9 for 'É', ends in a raw traceback instead of an `error:` line with exit code 2. importer.py:73 says "French Excel writes `;`", which confirms the importer targets French Excel exports. Nothing in importer.py handles encoding: grepping its first 100 lines for utf, bom, cp1252 and encod found nothing. The run fails before any write or billed call, so the impact is only a poor error message. Low severity is right.
2. Confirmed from code I read. cli.py:259 calls `source.read_text(encoding="utf-8")` inside cmd_radar_import with no guard. The only handler is cli.py:559, `except (ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError, KeyError)`, which catches neither FileNotFoundError/OSError nor UnicodeDecodeError. So a mistyped path or a non-UTF-8 file crashes with a raw traceback instead of the usual `error: ...` line and exit code 2. importer.py:73 does name French Excel as a target. tests/test_importer.py:20,60 cover only the UTF-8-with-BOM export ("CSV UTF-8"), which the code handles. No test covers a cp1252 file or a missing source file. The import makes no billed call and writes no file before it fails, so this is only a UX defect, and low severity is right.

### A88. Exception keys are not normalized: `--allow` in check and `factory allow` on unknown techs never match the violation key

- **Location:** `src/factory/cli.py:331` · **category:** cli-argument-validation · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** `factory check . --allow "django, Some_Lib"` still FAILs on both: ' django' has a leading space, and 'Some_Lib' does not equal the key 'some-lib'. `factory allow app Some_Lib --as it` records 'Some_Lib', so the radar gate keeps blocking an exception IT believes it granted.
- **Suggested fix:** Strip each --allow entry and apply radar.normalize to unknown names (and to tech ids) in both cmd_check and Foreman.allow.

**Evidence**

> cli.py:331 `exceptions = set(filter(None, (args.allow or "").split(",")))` keeps spaces and case (verified: 'fastapi, django' gives {'fastapi', ' django'}). guard.py:202 keys unknown techs as `normalize(f.name)`, and GuardReport.blocking (guard.py:143) compares `v.key not in exceptions` exactly. Likewise foreman.py:528-529 `known = self.radar.get(tech) or self.radar.find(tech)`, then `key = known.id if known else tech` stores the raw CLI string for an off-radar package.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it, so the finding stands. Every claim checks out against the code I read.  - **`--allow` parsing:** cli.py:331 splits on "," with no strip and no normalize, then passes the set to `report.blocking`. - **Exact matching:** guard.py:141-143 (`GuardReport.blocking`) tests `v.key not in exceptions` as an exact string comparison. - **How violation keys are built:** guard.py:202 uses `tech.id` for a known tech and `normalize(f.name)` for an unknown one. radar.py:37-39 defines `normalize` as strip, lower, and `[-_.]+` replaced by `-`. - **Result for `check`:** `--allow "django, Some_Lib"` gives the set {"django", " Some_Lib"}. The leading space comes from the split, and the key for that package is "some-lib", so the exception never matches and the violation stays FAIL. The same happens to any entry after ", " even for a known tech, because its key is `tech.id` with no space. - **Result for `factory allow`:** foreman.py:528-529 calls `radar.find(tech)`, which normalizes internally (radar.py:98-100). For an off-radar package it returns None, and `key = tech` keeps the raw string "Some_Lib". That raw key is appended to `item.it_exceptions` (foreman.py:534-535). Those exceptions are later passed to the same exact-match `blocking` through `active_exceptions` (foreman.py:1648), so the exception IT granted never matches "some-lib".  Severity stays low: - The user can work around it by typing the normalized key with no spaces. - An unmatched exception fails closed: the item is blocked, not let through.
2. The finding holds; I read the code remotely and could not refute it. cli.py:331 splits `--allow` on "," and does not strip or normalize the parts, so `"django, Some_Lib"` produces {'django', ' Some_Lib'}. guard.py:142-143 then tests `v.key not in exceptions` as an exact set lookup. Each violation key is `tech.id` for a radar tech or `normalize(f.name)` for an unknown one (guard.py key assignment). `normalize` (radar.py:37-39) strips, lowercases and collapses -_. to '-', so the key for Some_Lib is 'some-lib' and the key for a known tech is its id. The leading space (' django') and the case and underscore difference (' Some_Lib' vs 'some-lib') both make the lookup miss, so both entries still FAIL even though IT listed them as exceptions. Foreman.allow (foreman.py:528-529) is partly normalized: `radar.find` normalizes known techs, so those get their id. For an off-radar package, though, `key = tech` stores the raw CLI string. `factory allow app Some_Lib` therefore records 'Some_Lib', which never matches the 'some-lib' violation key, and the gate keeps blocking. This is a usability defect with a concrete wrong outcome: an exception IT granted does not apply. The workaround is to type the exact key, so low severity fits.

### A89. The CLI catches all KeyError, so internal bugs and a corrupt stage show as cryptic one-liners

- **Location:** `src/factory/cli.py:559` · **category:** error-handling · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** An item.json whose stage is not in STEPS (hand-edited, or written by another version) makes `factory board` print `error: 'deploy'` and exit 2, with no hint that the item file is the problem.
- **Suggested fix:** Raise a dedicated ItemNotFound(FactoryError) from Store.load and stop catching bare KeyError.

**Evidence**

> cli.py:559 includes `KeyError` to report Store.load's missing-item error (workitem.py:168), but it also catches every other KeyError: STEP_BY_NAME[self.stage] (workitem.py:110), dict lookups such as issue['body'], and others. cli.py:560 prints only `e.args[0]`.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The code matches the finding. cli.py:559 catches bare KeyError along with the factory errors, and cli.py:560 prints only e.args[0]. Store.load (workitem.py:168) raises KeyError for a missing item, which is the error this catch was written for. WorkItem.from_dict (workitem.py:136-139) never checks `stage`, and the `step` property (workitem.py:110) does STEP_BY_NAME[self.stage] (STEP_BY_NAME is built at workitem.py:39). cmd_board (cli.py:248) reads i.step for every item whose status is "waiting". So an item.json with stage "deploy" and status "waiting" makes `factory board` print `error: deploy` and exit 2. It does not name the item or the file. One small correction: the message has no quotes (`error: deploy`, not `error: 'deploy'`), because the code prints args[0] rather than str(e). The trigger also needs status "waiting". The scenario needs a hand-edited or foreign-version item.json, and the only effect is a poor diagnostic, so the severity stays low.
2. I confirmed this from the code. cli.py:559 adds bare KeyError to the caught exceptions, and cli.py:560-561 prints only e.args[0] before returning exit code 2. The intended source is Store.load (workitem.py:168), which raises KeyError for a missing item.
