> _do_gate sets `item.stage = "build"` before returning False (1672). run() then logs `item.log("failed", ...)` (332), and WorkItem.log stamps the current stage (workitem.py:129-130).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and the finding holds. In src/factory/foreman.py:1672, _do_gate sets `item.stage = "build"` when any gate fails and then returns False. Back in run(), foreman.py:332 logs `item.log("done" if ok else "failed", ...)` after the handler returns. WorkItem.log (workitem.py:130) stamps `"stage": self.stage`, which is already "build" at that point. So every gate failure is recorded in history as a 'failed' event under stage 'build', not 'gate'. run() already resets the stage itself on the retry path (foreman.py:342), so the early reset at 1672 is redundant there. It does matter on the blocked path: there, 1672 leaves the item at stage build. The effect is limited to how the audit history labels the event, so severity stays low.
2. I confirmed this from the code. In src/factory/foreman.py:1671-1674, _do_gate sets `item.stage = "build"` and then returns `(False, "gates failed: ...")`. Back in run(), foreman.py:330-332 calls the handler and then `item.log("done" if ok else "failed", detail[:600])`. WorkItem.log (workitem.py, `self.history.append({"at": now(), "stage": self.stage, ...})`) records whatever stage the item holds at that moment. So every gate failure lands in the history as a "failed" event with stage "build". The retry path makes this worse: on retry, run() sets `item.stage = "build"` itself at foreman.py:342, so setting the stage early inside _do_gate is redundant. Without a runner the item is blocked at stage build, and the "failed" entry still names build, not gate. The comment at 1672 ("the next build gets this detail as feedback") explains why the stage goes back to build. It does not justify logging the gate failure under build. The effect is limited to audit history and display: control flow and the feedback text are correct. I did not check how `factory show -v` renders the history, so the exact display impact is inferred. Low severity is correct.

### A137. Secrets gate skips .mjs/.cjs and other source files the factory itself treats as code

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:94` · **category:** gate-bypass · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** web/config.mjs containing `sk-ant-...` passes the ship-time secrets gate; it is caught only later, by publish's history scan, and never if delivery is off.
- **Suggested fix:** Scan every text-decodable file below a size limit instead of an allowlist of suffixes.

**Evidence**

> secrets_gate skips any suffix not in TEXT_SUFFIXES (31-60, 94-98). The set lacks .mjs/.cjs (which detect.py:175 scans as JS) and .go/.java/.rb/.php/.rs/.cs, although README.md:103 sells `factory check` on any repo.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The core claim holds in the code I read. In src/factory/gates.py:94-98, secrets_gate skips every file whose suffix is not in TEXT_SUFFIXES (gates.py:31-60), unless the name starts with one of SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES (gates.py:62). TEXT_SUFFIXES has no .mjs or .cjs, and no .go, .java, .rb, .php, .rs or .cs either. Meanwhile src/factory/detect.py:175 treats .mjs and .cjs as JS source. iter_files (detect.py:57) returns every tracked or untracked non-ignored file, so web/config.mjs is listed and then skipped before any regex runs. A file such as web/config.mjs containing an sk-ant- key therefore passes the gate. README.md:103 does advertise `factory check` on any repo. I could not check the secondary claim that publish's history scan catches the leak later: the GitHub API hit its rate limit before I could search for other callers or a publish-time scan. That part does not change the gap itself. Severity stays low, because coverage is missing for some file types while the main suffixes are still scanned.
2. I could not refute this finding, because the code says what the finding claims. In src/factory/gates.py, secrets_gate (lines 92-107) skips any file at lines 95-98 unless its suffix is in TEXT_SUFFIXES (lines 31-60) or its name starts with one of the SECRET_FILE_PREFIXES (line 62). TEXT_SUFFIXES contains .js, .ts, .tsx and .jsx but not .mjs or .cjs. It also leaves out .go, .java, .rb, .php, .rs and .cs. Meanwhile src/factory/detect.py:175 explicitly treats .mjs and .cjs as JS source when scanning imports. So a file named web/config.mjs that holds an `sk-ant-...` token is skipped before SECRET_PATTERNS (line 19) is applied, and the gate reports "no secrets found". README.md (lines 95-110 read) advertises `factory check` on any repo. tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py covers a G-2 section about secrets the gate used to miss, but the part I read does not justify leaving out .mjs or .cjs. I could not check the callers or the claim about publish's history scan because the GitHub API rate limit cut off further reads. That claim is secondary and does not change the core defect. Severity stays low: the gap is real, but only an allowlist of extensions is missing.

### A138. dependencies gate passes when uv.lock is absent, and never checks npm dependencies

- **Location:** `src/factory/gates.py:125` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** At MVP/prod the agent deletes uv.lock, or ships an AGPL npm package in web/package.json. The version constraints (radar.toml:44 fastapi >=0.115) and the forbidden-licence list are never applied, and the gate reports PASS.
- **Suggested fix:** At MVP+ treat a Python project without uv.lock as a failure, and add the npm lockfile and licence check (package-lock.json `license` fields).

**Evidence**

> gates.py:125-126 `if not (app_dir / "uv.lock").is_file(): return GateResult("dependencies", True, "not applicable...")`. policy.check_dependencies (policy.py:122-125) also returns [] when locked is None. Nothing reads package-lock.json, although golden_paths/fullstack-react ships web/package.json.

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding is half right. I read the files remotely with gh api and did not clone anything.  **Part 1 (uv.lock absent) is refuted.** - The code does say this. gates.py:125-126 returns PASS with "not applicable: the app has no uv.lock", and policy.py:124-125 returns [] when nothing is locked. - But it is intended and documented. README.md:44 says "No lockfile: not applicable; a lockfile without an environment: refused." - The "agent deletes uv.lock" scenario also does not work as written. factory.toml:101-102 puts the `tests` gate before `dependencies` at MVP and prod. The golden test command (golden_paths/fullstack-react/golden.toml:10) starts with `uv run`. In uv's normal behaviour (not checked in this repo), `uv run` rebuilds a missing uv.lock, so `dependencies` would then see a lockfile.  **Part 2 (npm never checked) stands.** - Nothing in gates.py or policy.py reads package.json, package-lock.json or node_modules. The only uv.lock or licence logic is gates.py:124-129 and policy.py:75-143, and it is all Python-only (uv.lock and the .venv site-packages). - radar.toml:16-18 says forbidden licences are ones "no app may ship from MVP up" (AGPL, SSPL, BUSL). - Yet the fullstack-react golden path ships web/package.json and web/package-lock.json. justfile:4 and golden.toml:10 run `npm ci`. - Failure scenario: at MVP or prod, a fullstack-react app adds an AGPL npm package. `dependencies` reports PASS ("locked versions and licences comply with the radar") and that licence is never checked. - README.md:41-44 describes the gate as Python-only, so this is a scope gap, not a crash. That gap still contradicts the radar's "no app may ship" wording.  **Severity: low, not medium.** The documented-and-unreachable part drops out, and what remains is an npm licence and version blind spot on one golden path.
2. I confirmed the code but only part of the scenario, so I'm downgrading it to low. gates.py:124-129 returns PASS ("not applicable: the app has no uv.lock") when uv.lock is missing. policy.py check_dependencies returns [] when locked is None. Nothing in the dependency or licence path reads package.json or package-lock.json. The fail-open on a missing uv.lock is intended and tested: test_policy.py:131 asserts `result.ok and "not applicable"`, so that path exists for apps that are not Python. The "agent deletes uv.lock" scenario mostly fails in practice. In factory.toml the mvp and prod gate lists run "tests" (`uv run --quiet pytest -q`) before "dependencies", and `uv run` re-locks and writes uv.lock when it is missing, so the gate finds a lockfile again. The npm half holds. radar.toml:16 says "Licences no app may ship from MVP up", yet the licence check only reads .venv dist-info METADATA (policy.py installed_licenses). An AGPL npm package in golden_paths/fullstack-react/web/package.json passes the dependencies gate. The radar's version constraint (radar.toml:44) covers fastapi only. The npm entries (react, vite, vitest) carry no version constraint, so no npm version check is skipped. The docstring and the factory.toml comment do scope the gate to uv.lock and .venv. This is a documented coverage gap with a real licence-policy hole on the frontend, not a medium fail-open.

### A139. files_touched lstrip('./') strips leading dots from dotfiles, producing false scope warnings

- **Location:** `src/factory/guard.py:98` · **category:** false-positive · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** files_touched lists `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and the prompt says 'update `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'. A warning says files_touched does not list it, and this goes into the planner re-prompt.
- **Suggested fix:** Use removeprefix("./") on both sides.

**Evidence**

> `t.strip().lstrip("./")` strips characters, not a prefix: '.github/workflows/ci.yml' becomes 'github/workflows/ci.yml' (checked). REPO_PATH (line 64) keeps the leading '.' on the prompt side.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. At src/factory/guard.py:98, files_touched entries are normalized with `t.strip().lstrip("./")`. That strips characters, not a prefix: running python3 on '.github/workflows/ci.yml'.lstrip('./') returns 'github/workflows/ci.yml'. On the prompt side, REPO_PATH (guard.py:64, `[\w./-]+\.(...|ya?ml|...)`) captures '.github/workflows/ci.yml' with its leading dot, and 'update' matches WRITE_VERB (guard.py:59-63). _in_scope (guard.py:74-77) then compares '.github/workflows/ci.yml' to 'github/workflows/ci.yml'. They are not equal, and neither the trailing-slash test nor fnmatch matches them, so line 113 emits '...files_touched does not list it'. The caller confirms the effect. foreman.py:736-737 adds these errors as warnings. foreman.py:764-768 treats them as scope warnings that trigger a planner re-prompt, up to plan_lint_retries, and then shows them to the owner at plan review. The check never blocks a plan (foreman.py:735, 768), so low severity is right. It only costs wasted re-prompts and noisy warnings for plans that touch dotfiles or dot-directories such as .github/ or .pre-commit-config.yaml.
2. The finding holds. I read src/factory/guard.py:98, which runs `touched = [t.strip().lstrip("./") for t in ...]`. `lstrip` removes characters, not a prefix: in Python, `'.github/workflows/ci.yml'.lstrip('./')` returns `'github/workflows/ci.yml'`. The prompt side does not strip anything. REPO_PATH at guard.py:64 is `[\w./-]+` followed by `ya?ml`, so it captures `.github/workflows/ci.yml` with the leading dot. "update" matches WRITE_VERB (guard.py:59-63). _in_scope (guard.py:74-77) then compares `.github/workflows/ci.yml` against `github/workflows/ci.yml`: they are not equal, the touched entry does not end in '/', and fnmatch does not match. So guard.py:113 reports "the prompt changes .github/workflows/ci.yml but files_touched does not list it" for a task that lists the file correctly. The caller is foreman.py:736-737. It adds these messages to result.warnings, and the comment at foreman.py:735 says scope warnings feed the planner re-prompt and warn the owner but never block a plan. That makes the impact a false warning plus a misleading re-prompt, so low severity is right. The tests I found in tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py do not exercise a dotfile path.

### A140. Plan negation filter skips sentences that order deletions/replacements and lines mixing use with any negation

- **Location:** `src/factory/guard.py:105` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** A task prompt 'Delete `src/legacy.py`.' outside files_touched produces no scope warning. 'Use Flask for the API (no auth needed)' produces no plan radar error (the build-time radar gate still catches the dependency).
- **Suggested fix:** Treat delete/remove/replace as write verbs, and apply the negation only to the clause that carries the technology or path.

**Evidence**

> NEGATION (guard.py:22-27) includes remove/delete/replace/drop, and guard.py:105 skips the whole sentence, so the WRITE_VERB entries delete/remove (59-62) can never fire. guard.py:45 skips a whole plan line for any 'no'/'without'. Checked: 'Delete `src/legacy.py`.' and 'Use Flask for the API (no auth needed).' both match NEGATION.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it, and part of the finding is confirmed by the code. NEGATION (src/factory/guard.py:22-27) matches remov(e|es|ed|ing), delet(e|es|ed|ing), replace[sd]? and drop. plan_scope_errors skips any sentence that matches NEGATION (guard.py:105) before it looks at clauses. WRITE_VERB (guard.py:59-63) lists delet(?:e|es) and remov(?:e|es), but every sentence containing those words has already been skipped at line 105, so those two alternatives never fire there.  Concrete case: a T-task prompt line 'Delete `src/legacy.py`.' with files_touched not listing that file returns no scope error. That goes against the stated purpose at guard.py:81-85: a scoped commit would leave the deletion behind and sweep it into a leftovers commit. The tests in tests/test_plan_radar.py:172-189 show the removal words were added to NEGATION on purpose, but for the radar check (a migration plan names what it removes). Nothing shows that sharing NEGATION with the scope check was meant to exempt deletions.  The radar half ('Use Flask ... (no auth needed)' skipped at guard.py:45) is a weaker claim. The docstring at guard.py:32-36 openly says only lines without a negation count, that it is a cost-saver, and that the build-time gate is the real enforcement. So that half is a documented heuristic limit rather than a bug.  Severity stays low: plan-lint only gives early warnings, and the deleted file would still show up downstream as a leftover.
2. The scope half is confirmed. In src/factory/guard.py:22-27, NEGATION matches delet(e|es|ed|ing), remov(e|es|ed|ing) and replace[sd]?. In plan_scope_errors, guard.py:104-106 skips any sentence that NEGATION matches, and that check runs before WRITE_VERB (guard.py:59-62) is applied at :108. WRITE_VERB lists delet(?:e|es) and remov(?:e|es), so those alternatives can never fire. A prompt line "Delete `src/legacy.py`." where src/legacy.py is outside files_touched returns []. The same happens for "Replace `app/x.py` with ...". The docstring at :83-85 says the check exists to catch edits that the scoped commit would leave behind, and a deletion outside files_touched is exactly such an edit.  No test covers a positive delete case. tests/test_gates_blind_spots.py:265-276 only covers "Do not modify" and "Never edit".  The radar half (guard.py:45, a whole line skipped for any 'no' or 'without') is intended, documented behavior. The docstring at :34-36 says "Only lines without a negation count" and that the build-time radar gate is the real enforcement. The removal words are deliberate there too (tests/test_plan_radar.py:169-185). So that half is a heuristic tradeoff, not a defect.  The finding stands on the scope half only, at low severity, because this check only produces warnings (test_gates_blind_spots.py:292-295 shows result.ok stays true).

### A141. `--doc` naming a missing file is silently skipped

- **Location:** `src/factory/guard.py:206` · **category:** cli-argument-validation · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** `factory check app --doc docs/desing.md` (typo) passes without scanning the design doc, so forbidden technologies named there are never checked.
- **Suggested fix:** In cmd_check, error out when a given --doc does not exist.

**Evidence**

> cli.py:329 builds `docs = [target / d for d in args.doc]`, and guard.py:205-207 `for doc in docs or []: if not doc.is_file(): continue`.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code remotely and it does what the finding says. cli.py:329 builds `docs = [target / d for d in args.doc]` from the user's `--doc` values. cli.py:330 passes that list to `check_project`, with no existence check anywhere in `cmd_check` (cli.py:325-339). In guard.py, lines 205-207 are `for doc in docs or []: if not doc.is_file(): continue`, so a missing file is skipped with no warning and adds nothing to the report. cmd_check then prints PASS when there are no blocking violations (cli.py:337-338). So `factory check app --doc docs/desing.md` (a typo) exits 0 without scanning the design doc, and any forbidden technology named in the real doc goes unchecked. Nothing documents the silent skip as intended for user-supplied paths: the only nearby comment (guard.py:18) is about ignore regions inside docs, not missing ones. Severity stays low: it needs a user typo and only causes a false PASS on the doc-scan part of the check.
2. I confirmed this from the code. cli.py:329 builds `docs = [target / d for d in args.doc]` and does not check that each file exists. cli.py:533 says `--doc` is "design doc to text-scan (relative to path)". In check_project (guard.py, around lines 205-207), the loop `for doc in docs or []: if not doc.is_file(): continue` skips missing files without saying so. Nothing in cmd_check (cli.py:325-339) prints which docs were scanned or warns about a missing one. So `factory check app --doc docs/desing.md` reports PASS based on manifests alone, and a forbidden technology named only in the design doc is never flagged.  The skip makes sense for the other caller: foreman.py:657 always passes `d / "design.md"`, which may not exist yet. For an explicit CLI argument, though, ignoring a missing file is a gap in argument validation. The tests (test_guard.py:80-89) only cover the ignore block, not missing docs. It is a real defect but a low-severity one: the user has to make a typo, and the output does not even claim the doc was scanned.

### A142. Roles keyed on mutable GitHub login, not account id

- **Location:** `src/factory/identity.py:47` · **category:** identity-spoofing · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap1-identity-roles-enforcement
- **Failure scenario:** An IT member listed as `alice` renames her GitHub account; the freed login `alice` is registered by someone else, who then passes _actor for every role alice held until IT edits roles.toml.
- **Suggested fix:** Store and compare the numeric user id (gh api user -q .id), keeping login for display.

**Evidence**

> Only `.login` is fetched (l.47) and Identity has no id field (l.27-29); roles.toml lists logins (roles.toml:5-7) compared case-insensitively (identity.py:75-76).

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding matches the code. src/factory/identity.py:47 runs `gh api user -q .login` and keeps only the login. Identity (l.27-29) has just `login` and `source`, with no numeric id. RoleMap.roles_of (l.74-76) matches the login against roles.toml case-insensitively, and roles.toml:5-7 lists only logins. Nothing in this file pins an account id. So if someone renames a listed GitHub account and another person registers the freed login, that person would pass role checks until IT edits roles.toml. GitHub does allow a released username to be claimed again. The risk is low because the attacker still has to be able to run the factory against this repo, and the takeover needs a rename to happen first. It is still a real gap in what the docstring promises at l.3-6 ("verified identity"). Low severity is right.
2. I could not refute this from the code. src/factory/identity.py:46-47 runs `gh api user -q .login` and nothing else. The Identity dataclass at l.26-29 holds only `login` and `source`, with no numeric id. RoleMap.roles_of at l.74-76 matches by lowercased login against roles.toml, which lists bare logins (roles.toml:4-7, "Logins are case-insensitive"). GitHub lets other people register a login that has been freed by a rename. If that happens, a new account with the old name, authenticated in `gh`, passes holds() for every role the old owner had, and nothing checks an immutable id. README.md:167 says that with identity on, issue import is also gated on the issue author's login. That is a remote path where a re-registered login gets accepted without any access to the factory machine. Two things limit the impact, so low severity is right. ROADMAP.md:398 plans to replace this provider with OIDC/SSO in phase 4. And the attack needs a rename followed by IT not updating roles.toml. The weakness is still real and can be shown from the code, so it is not just style.

### A143. Malformed [roles] crashes with an uncaught exception instead of IdentityError

- **Location:** `src/factory/identity.py:93` · **category:** error-handling · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap1-identity-roles-enforcement
- **Failure scenario:** roles.toml written as `roles = ["alice"]` (array instead of table): l.92 builds 'unknown role' problems, then l.93 raises AttributeError ('list' has no 'items') before they are reported; `roles = 1` raises TypeError at l.92. Every `factory` command (load_roles runs in _foreman, cli.py:34) dies with a traceback. Fail-closed, but undiagnosed.
- **Suggested fix:** if not isinstance(raw, dict): raise IdentityError(f'{path}: [roles] must be a table').

**Evidence**

> raw = data.get('roles', {}) (l.91) is not type-checked. l.92 iterates raw, l.93 calls raw.items(). cli.main only catches (ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError, KeyError) (cli.py:559).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I confirmed this against the code. In src/factory/identity.py, line 91 sets raw = data.get("roles", {}) and never checks its type. Line 92 iterates raw. Line 93 calls raw.items().  - If roles.toml has roles = ["alice"], line 92 builds an "unknown role" problem. Line 93 then raises AttributeError, because a list has no .items(), so that problem is never reported. - If it has roles = 1, iterating the int at line 92 raises TypeError.  cli.py:33-34 calls load_roles from _foreman whenever identity_provider == "github". cli.py:559 catches only ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError and KeyError. AttributeError and TypeError are not in that list, so the command ends with a traceback instead of an "error:" line and exit code 2. Nothing else guards against this.  The impact is diagnostic only: the run fails closed, with no security bypass. Low severity is correct.
2. Confirmed from the code. In src/factory/identity.py:91, `raw = data.get("roles", {})` is never type-checked. With `roles = ["alice"]`, l.92 iterates over the list and records an "unknown role 'alice'" problem. Then l.93 calls `raw.items()` on that list and raises AttributeError, so the l.94-95 IdentityError path is never reached. With `roles = 1`, iterating over an int at l.92 raises TypeError. cli.py:559 catches only ConfigError, RadarError, FactoryError, AgentError, EngineError, IdentityError and KeyError, so neither exception is caught there. load_roles is called from _foreman at cli.py:34 whenever identity_provider == "github", so commands that build a Foreman would end in a traceback instead of `error: ...`. Nothing stops anyone, so the impact is a missing diagnostic, not a security hole. Low severity is right.

### A144. radar-import: slugify collapses distinct technologies (C++, C#, C) and rejects the import

- **Location:** `src/factory/importer.py:207` · **category:** import · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** A company radar listing 'C++' and 'C#' gives 'c#' duplicates 'C++' (id 'c'). Two non-Latin names both become 'item'. A legitimate radar cannot be imported without hand-renaming.
- **Suggested fix:** Map '+' to 'plus' and '#' to 'sharp' before slugifying, and on a collision suffix the id instead of refusing.

**Evidence**

> importer.py:207 `tech_id = slugify(raw["name"])`, and workitem.py:48-50 drops non-ASCII and every non-alphanumeric character, falling back to 'item'. importer.py:208-212 then reports a duplicate and skips the row.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it confirms the finding. In workitem.py, slugify() turns text into NFKD ASCII, then replaces every character outside [a-z0-9] with '-' and strips the dashes, falling back to 'item'. So 'C++', 'C#' and 'C' all become 'c', and any two fully non-Latin names both become 'item'. In importer.py:207 the code sets tech_id = slugify(raw["name"]). Lines 208-212 then add a 'duplicates' entry to result.problems and skip the row. importer.py:61 marks problems as blocking ('# block the import') and line 69 returns ok as `not self.problems`, so the whole radar import is refused. I found nothing in the code that disambiguates or guards against this. The severity of low is fair: the error message is explicit and a user can rename the row to get around it.
2. The code confirms the finding. importer.py:207 sets tech_id = slugify(raw["name"]). In workitem.py:47-50, slugify drops non-ASCII (NFKD, then encode ascii ignore) and turns every run of [^a-z0-9] into '-', then strips the dashes. That maps 'C++', 'C#' and 'C' all to 'c'. A name that is entirely non-Latin (for example CJK) becomes '' and then falls back to 'item'. importer.py:208-212 then appends a 'duplicates' entry to result.problems. Per importer.py:61 ('problems ... block the import') and :69 (ok = not self.problems), that entry blocks the whole import, not just one row. There is no override or suffixing, so a real radar listing both C++ and C# cannot be imported until someone renames entries by hand. Low severity fits: the failure is loud and has a manual workaround.

### A145. ABSENT: grounding accepts any made-up element name, and rejects true absences whose heading remains

- **Location:** `src/factory/judge.py:163` · **category:** judge-parsing · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** (a) The judge scores examples=1 with `ABSENT: an examples section` on a spec that has one, and the verdict becomes 'fail' (judge.py:213). (b) The evals table is deleted but its heading kept: the judge's correct 1 is discarded and the verdict can be 'pass'.
- **Suggested fix:** Map the absent element to a known section or ID vocabulary and check it structurally (e.g. reuse speclint) instead of a free-text substring test.

**Evidence**

> `return len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`. Any phrase not occurring verbatim counts as proof of absence. Checked: `ABSENT: an examples section` gives True against a spec that has '## 5. Examples'. Conversely, `ABSENT: Evals` fails whenever the word 'evals' survives (e.g. the heading '## 7. Evals' with the table deleted), so the low score is dropped as ungrounded at judge.py:244/251.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and both halves of the finding hold. src/factory/judge.py:159-163: absence_holds normalises the quote, removes the "absent:" prefix and returns `len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`. That is a substring test on whatever words the judge chose.  (a) A paraphrase passes the check. `_norm("ABSENT: an examples section")` gives the element "an examples section". A spec containing "## 5. Examples" normalises to "5. examples", which does not contain that phrase, so the function returns True. evaluate() then counts the score as grounded (judge.py:243). That score is 1 or 2, so compute_verdict returns "fail" (judge.py:213). This defeats the safeguard the module docstring describes at judge.py:6-7 ("code checks the quote really occurs there"). The judge can ground any low score by naming the missing part in words that differ from the spec's own.  (b) If the evals table is deleted but its heading is kept, "evals" still appears in the text, so `ABSENT: Evals` is rejected. An is_grounded quote cannot rescue it either: "7. Evals" is shorter than MIN_GROUNDED_CHARS=24 (judge.py:128). The correct score of 1 is then dropped as ungrounded (judge.py:244), and the verdict is computed from the remaining scores (judge.py:251-260).  tests/test_judge.py:183-212 only covers exact names ("Evals", "Behaviors"). No test covers a paraphrase or a kept heading, so neither behaviour is guarded against.  Severity stays low. The judge is advisory and never a blocking gate (judge.py:1). In case (b) the judge could still quote other text from the artifact. Case (a) requires the model to paraphrase.
2. I read src/factory/judge.py in the repo, and the code says what the finding claims. absence_holds (judge.py:154-163) normalises the quote, removes the "absent:" prefix, and returns `len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`. That is a plain substring test against the normalised artifact, which is computed at judge.py:229.  (a) Take `ABSENT: an examples section`. After normalisation the element is "an examples section". A spec headed '## 5. Examples' normalises to "5. examples", which does not contain that phrase, so the check returns True. The low score then counts as grounded (judge.py:243), and compute_verdict returns 'fail' when min <= 2 (judge.py:213).  (b) The prompt's own example tells the judge to write `ABSENT: Evals` when there is "no evals table" (judge.py:187-189). If only the table is deleted and the heading '## 7. Evals' stays, "evals" is still in the haystack. The correct score of 1 is then marked ungrounded and ignored (judge.py:244-245, 251), so the verdict can come from the remaining criteria and can be 'pass'.  The docstring says the absence must hold "only when the named element really does not occur". That intent is not met in either case. The report is labelled advisory (judge.py:103), so low severity is right.

### A146. absence_holds grounds a false 'ABSENT:' claim whenever the paraphrased element name is not a literal substring

- **Location:** `src/factory/judge.py:163` · **category:** untested-code-path · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** tests
- **Failure scenario:** Spec contains `## 7. Evals` with a full table. The judge answers eval_coverage=1 with quote `ABSENT: evals table` (or `ABSENT: executable evals`). The phrase 'evals table' never occurs in the normalised spec, so absence_holds returns True. The hallucinated 1 is counted as grounded (judge.py:243) and compute_verdict returns `fail` on a good spec. No test has a present section under a paraphrased name.
- **Suggested fix:** Add a test with the Evals section present and quote 'ABSENT: evals table' expecting False. In src, check the element's significant words (or a fixed vocabulary of section names) rather than the whole phrase.

**Evidence**

> judge.py:163 `return len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`. That is a substring test on the judge's own wording. tests/test_judge.py:186-191 only checks it against an artifact with no evals at all (`absence_holds("absent: evals table", 2, artifact)` where artifact lacks evals), and :188 only against an element named exactly as it appears ('Behaviors').

**Skeptic notes**

1. The finding holds; I lowered its severity from medium to low.  What the code does: judge.py:162-163 takes the judge's own wording after "absent:" and does a whole-phrase substring test (`len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`) against the normalised artifact. The docstring at :155-158 says the point of this check is to throw out false absence claims "like a made-up quote".  Why a false claim gets through: `_norm` (:121-125) only lowercases, removes markdown characters and collapses whitespace. A spec with a section "## 7. Evals" holding a table therefore normalises to text containing "evals", but almost never the literal phrase "evals table". Take a judge that wrongly answers eval_coverage=1 with the quote "ABSENT: evals table". `absence_holds` returns True, evaluate() at :243 marks the score as grounded, and compute_verdict at :213 returns "fail" because the lowest score is 2 or less.  What the tests cover: tests/test_judge.py:185-191 only use an artifact that has no Evals section. Line 187 even asserts that "absent: evals table" is accepted. Line 188 checks a section that is present only under its exact name ("Behaviors"). No test puts a present section against a reworded name, so the loophole is untested.  Why low and not medium: the report is labelled advisory only (judge.py:103). The failure also needs the LLM to make two mistakes at once: claim an absence that is false, and word it differently from the section heading. The prompt at :188 steers it toward the exact name ("ABSENT: Evals").
2. Confirmed from the code. judge.py:162-163 strips the `absent:` prefix and returns `len(element) >= 4 and element not in haystack`, which is a plain substring test of the judge's whole phrase against the normalised artifact. In `## 7. Evals` followed by a table, `_norm` (judge.py:121-125) turns the text into "7. evals" plus the cell text, so "evals table" or "executable evals" never appears. absence_holds then returns True for score 1 or 2. evaluate (judge.py:243) marks that score as grounded, and compute_verdict (judge.py:213) returns "fail" as soon as any grounded score is 2 or less.  Nothing elsewhere guards against this. The prompt at judge.py:188 shows `ABSENT: Evals` only as an example and does not require an exact section name. The tests confirm the gap: tests/test_judge.py:187 asserts that "absent: evals table" holds, but only on an artifact that has no evals section. No test covers a section that is present under a paraphrased name.  I am lowering the severity because the module is explicitly advisory (judge.py:1-5: "ADVISORY, never a blocking gate"). The harm is a misleading advisory "fail" shown at the human checkpoint and fed into telemetry; nothing is blocked. The panel median in combine() can also soften a single false run.

### A147. With an even judge_votes, median_low turns the panel into the minimum and one outlier run fails the artifact

- **Location:** `src/factory/judge.py:316` · **category:** aggregation · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap4-llm-artifact-retry-loops
- **Failure scenario:** judge_votes=2, examples scores [2, 4]: the panel's examples is 2, so the verdict is fail. One noisy run decides alone, harsher than a single run.
- **Suggested fix:** Use statistics.median and round, or reject or warn on even judge_votes in load_config.

**Evidence**

> `median = statistics.median_low(sorted(...))`. For 2 scores median_low is the lower one, and for 4 it is the 2nd lowest. config.py:107 accepts any judge_votes >= 1. The docstring (297-299) and test_judge_panel.py:55 ('one outlier run no longer fails the artifact') assume odd panels. In combine, votes=2 also needs both runs reliable (307).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it. The code says what the finding says. src/factory/judge.py:316 takes `statistics.median_low` over the grounded scores from the reliable runs. With two scores [2, 4] that gives 2, and judge.py:318-321 records it as the criterion score. The verdict then comes from compute_verdict (judge.py:326), and test_judge_panel.py:61 shows that a 2 on "examples" yields fail.  Nothing blocks an even panel: - config.py:107 only clamps judge_votes to at least 1. - The README (line 277), PROGRESS.md (line 70) and the shipped factory.toml (line 21) recommend 3 but never forbid an even number. - The docstring (judge.py:297-299) promises a median that absorbs noise, and test_judge_panel.py:55 says "one outlier run no longer fails the artifact". Both only hold for an odd count.  The combine claim also holds: with votes=2 and one unreliable run, the check at judge.py:307 (1*2 <= 2) makes the whole panel unreliable.  The problem reaches further than even configs. With the default judge_votes=3, the count becomes even whenever one run is unreliable (combine still proceeds with 2 reliable runs) or a criterion is ungrounded in one run (judge.py:314 filters on `grounded`). In those cases the lowest score decides.  Severity stays low. The effect is a stricter verdict (a possible false fail), never a false pass, and it only applies when the count of reliable or grounded scores is even.
2. I confirmed this from the code. In src/factory/judge.py, combine() sets `median = statistics.median_low(sorted(...))` at line 315 (the finding says 316, one line off). With two scores, median_low returns the lower one. compute_verdict (judge.py:209-217) returns "fail" whenever min(scores) <= 2, so scores [2, 4] give a criterion of 2 and the artifact fails. config.py clamps judge_votes with `max(1, int(...))` and never rejects an even value. The docs describe the panel as keeping "the median" (factory.toml:18 and README.md:277-281), and nothing there says the panel must be odd. The test at tests/test_judge_panel.py also assumes 3 runs ("one outlier run no longer fails").  The problem is wider than the finding says. With the default judge_votes = 3, a criterion can still end up with only two scores in two ways. First, one run can be unreliable: reliable stays at 2 of 3, which is not a majority of unreliable runs, so the panel goes on (line 307). Second, one run's score for that criterion can be ungrounded and get filtered out (line 313). In both cases median_low again takes the lower score, so one outlier run fails the artifact even on the default config.  I keep the severity at low: it only makes the verdict stricter (a false fail), it never lets a bad artifact pass.

### A148. In combine's 'too few criteria' branch the panel is unreliable but the raw answers are dropped

- **Location:** `src/factory/judge.py:322` · **category:** auditability · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap4-llm-artifact-retry-loops
- **Failure scenario:** 3 runs, each reliable on different criteria. Fewer than 4 spec criteria survive across the panel, so the verdict is unreliable, and judge-spec.md has no raw answer to audit, unlike every other unreliable path.
- **Suggested fix:** Set out.raw = next((r.raw for r in reports if r.raw), '') before returning in that branch too.

**Evidence**

> Lines 322-325 set verdict 'unreliable' and return without setting out.raw. Only the majority-unreliable branch copies raw (309). markdown() prints the raw answer only `if self.verdict == "unreliable" and self.raw` (116).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read src/factory/judge.py remotely and could not refute the finding. In combine(), the majority-unreliable branch copies a raw answer at line 309 (`out.raw = next((r.raw for r in reports if r.raw), "")`). The 'too few criteria' branch at lines 322-325 sets verdict 'unreliable' and the summary, then returns without setting out.raw. The constructor at line 302 leaves raw at its default. The 'unreliable' verdict is reachable in that branch: the panel can have a majority of reliable runs whose grounded criteria do not combine to more than half the rubric. markdown() prints the raw answer only when `self.verdict == "unreliable" and self.raw` (line 116), so in this case judge-spec.md gets no raw answer to audit. Each reliable run does carry raw, because the single-run judge sets report.raw = result.text[:6000] at line 289 whenever the call succeeds, so the data exists and is dropped. Nothing guards against this elsewhere and it is not documented as intended. It is a real gap in auditability, but it only affects the report, not the verdict, so severity stays low.
2. I read src/factory/judge.py remotely and the code matches the finding. In combine(), the branch where most runs are unreliable (lines 304-308 set the verdict) copies a raw answer at line 309: out.raw = next((r.raw for r in reports if r.raw), ""). The 'too few criteria' branch at lines 322-325 sets verdict "unreliable" and the summary, then returns without setting out.raw, so out.raw stays at its default of an empty string. markdown() prints the raw answer only when `self.verdict == "unreliable" and self.raw` (line 116), so this unreliable panel's report has no raw audit block. Each reliable run does carry raw, because judge() sets report.raw = result.text[:6000] at line 189, so a raw answer is available to copy. The scenario is reachable: the runs are mostly reliable, but grounded criteria do not reach the majority threshold at line 322, either because individual runs grounded too few or because few runs grounded any given criterion. The impact is limited to auditability. The surviving grounded criteria and the merged problems are still reported, so low severity is right.

### A149. Version constraint with '.*' and an ordering operator is accepted at load and never enforced

- **Location:** `src/factory/policy.py:58` · **category:** wrong-comparison · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** IT writes `version = "<1.*"` for fastapi. A locked fastapi 5.0 satisfies it and the dependencies gate passes.
- **Suggested fix:** Reject '.*' with any operator other than ==/!= in parse_spec.

**Evidence**

> policy.py:58-63: for a target ending in '.*', only `==` and `!=` are evaluated, then `continue`. parse_spec (_CLAUSE line 17 allows '*') accepts `>=1.*` or `<2.*`, so load_radar (radar.py:157-162) raises no error.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it confirms the finding. In policy.py:17 the `_CLAUSE` version character class `[0-9A-Za-z.*+!-]` allows `*` with any operator, so `parse_spec("<1.*")` at policy.py:44-52 returns `[('<', '1.*')]` without raising an error. The radar load check at radar.py:157-162 only calls `parse_spec`, so no RadarError is raised. In `satisfies` (policy.py:58-63), a target ending in `.*` only rejects in the `==` and `!=` cases. For `<`, `>=` and the other ordering operators it runs `continue`, so the clause is skipped. If that is the only clause, `satisfies("5.0", "<1.*")` returns True. `check_dependencies` (policy.py:131-133) then reports no problem, and the gate passes. PEP 440 disallows `.*` with ordering operators, so a typo like this should be rejected at load. Instead the constraint is dropped without any message. Severity is low: it needs a constraint that is invalid under PEP 440, and the bad pin fails open.
2. I confirmed this from the code. In policy.py:17, `_CLAUSE` lets any operator take a target containing '*', so parse_spec (policy.py:44-52) accepts `<1.*`. The only load-time check is in radar.py:157-162, which calls parse_spec and nothing else, so no error is raised. At evaluation time, satisfies (policy.py:58-63) takes the '.*' branch, acts only on `==` and `!=`, and then hits `continue` for any other operator. That makes `<1.*` always true, and a locked fastapi 5.0 passes. PEP 440 does not allow '.*' with ordering operators, so the user error is real, but the code silently drops the constraint instead of rejecting it. Severity stays low because it needs a malformed constraint written by IT.

### A150. A lockfile without an environment is accepted when the radar has no forbidden licences

- **Location:** `src/factory/policy.py:134` · **category:** docs-vs-reality · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** A company radar imported without a [licenses] section: an app with uv.lock and no .venv passes the dependencies gate, contrary to the README.
- **Suggested fix:** Either align the README ('refused only when a licence policy exists') or refuse unconditionally.

**Evidence**

> check_dependencies only reports a missing .venv inside `if radar.forbidden_licenses:` (134-138). README.md:44: 'a lockfile without an environment: refused.'

**Skeptic notes**

1. I couldn't refute this; the code matches the finding. In src/factory/policy.py:134-138 the missing-.venv refusal sits inside `if radar.forbidden_licenses:`. When the radar has no forbidden licences, check_dependencies only compares locked versions (lines 126-133) and returns [] for an app that has uv.lock and no .venv. The radar loader makes that state reachable: src/factory/radar.py:74 defaults forbidden_licenses to (), and lines 163-166 fall back to [] when the [licenses] section is missing or invalid. README.md:44 says "lockfile without environment: refused" with no condition. The only test (tests/test_policy.py:122-126) uses the repo radar.toml, whose [licenses] section is filled in (radar.toml:17-18), so the no-licence-policy case is never tested. Mitigating context: the README sentence follows the licence-policy description at line 43, so it can be read as scoped to licence checking. The code behaviour itself is coherent: with no licence policy, nothing needs the environment. So this is a small docs-versus-code mismatch, and severity stays low.
2. The code matches the finding. In src/factory/policy.py:134-138, check_dependencies looks for a missing .venv ("no installed environment (.venv)...") only inside `if radar.forbidden_licenses:`. src/factory/radar.py:74 defaults forbidden_licenses to (), and radar.py:163 reads it as data.get("licenses", {}).get("forbidden", []). So a radar with no [licenses] section gives an empty tuple. An app with uv.lock and no .venv then gets only the version checks (lines 126-133) and no refusal. README.md:44 says without condition: "No lockfile: not applicable; a lockfile without an environment: refused." The impact is small. The shipped radar.toml:17-18 defines [licenses] forbidden, so the default setup refuses as documented. The README sentence also sits in the licence context (line 43), which suggests the code's behaviour is intended, since a .venv is only needed to read licences. That makes this a documentation imprecision rather than a functional bug, so severity stays low.

### A151. Licence check passes packages whose METADATA carries no License/License-Expression/classifier

- **Location:** `src/factory/policy.py:141` · **category:** fail-open · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** An AGPL package that ships only a LICENSE file (no License header or classifier) passes the forbidden-licence policy at MVP/prod.
- **Suggested fix:** Treat an empty licence text as 'unknown' and require IT review, or read the License-File contents.

**Evidence**

> installed_licenses (policy.py:109-116) builds the text only from those headers. A package that declares its licence only through License-File gets ''. Line 141 then finds no forbidden substring and reports nothing.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it does what the finding says. In src/factory/policy.py, installed_licenses (lines 100-116) builds the licence text only from License-Expression, from License (with UNKNOWN dropped) and from "License ::" classifiers. A package with none of those is stored as "" (line 116). tests/test_policy.py:89 asserts this directly: {"c": ""}. check_dependencies at policy.py:139-143 does a substring match of each forbidden term against that text. On "" nothing matches, so the package passes and no "unknown licence" problem is recorded. No caller guards against this: README.md:43 and ROADMAP.md:367 only describe a forbidden-list check against licence metadata, and no test covers an unknown or empty licence at the check level. Concrete case: a package that declares AGPL only through License-File, or a dist-info with no licence headers at all, passes at any maturity. Because the policy is a denylist, letting unknown licences through could be a deliberate choice, but nothing in the code or docs says so. It is a real but narrow fail-open, so low severity stands.
2. I read the code remotely and it does what the finding says. In src/factory/policy.py:93-117, installed_licenses builds the licence text only from the License-Expression and License headers (UNKNOWN is dropped) and from the "License ::" classifiers. A package whose METADATA has none of these is recorded with "". In check_dependencies (policy.py:139-143), line 141 runs `f.lower() in text.lower()` over the forbidden list. That never matches an empty string, so the package is reported compliant. tests/test_policy.py:89 even asserts that a package with no licence headers maps to "c": "". No test flags such a package and nothing documents it as unknown or needing review. Nothing upstream guards against it either: the only early exit is when there is no .venv at all (policy.py:136-138). The failure scenario is realistic: a package that ships only a License-File, with no licence header or classifier, slips past the AGPL/SSPL denylist. Severity stays low. This is a denylist matched on self-declared metadata, so it is best-effort by nature, and the gap needs a package that leaves its licence metadata empty.

### A152. Factory commits still use the operator's global git config (hooksPath, gpgsign)

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:100` · **category:** git · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** An operator with global `commit.gpgsign=true` (pinentry), or a global core.hooksPath (pre-commit/husky), runs a build. Every factory commit tries to sign with the operator's key, or runs their personal hooks: a hook can reject the scope-drift commit, or pinentry can block a headless run. When it works, the 'AI Factory' commits carry the operator's signature.
- **Suggested fix:** Pass `-c commit.gpgsign=false -c core.hooksPath=/dev/null` (and tag.gpgsign) to every factory git invocation, or set them in the local config in prepare_project.

**Evidence**

> The module docstring (project.py:5-6) promises commits 'never borrow the operator's personal git configuration', but prepare_project only sets user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf locally (project.py:100-102). Every commit goes through _git without `-c commit.gpgsign=false -c core.hooksPath=...` (project.py:112, 147, 184, 202, 266).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute it. project.py:59-66 (`_git_raw`) runs `subprocess.run(["git", *args], cwd=app, ...)` with no `env=` and no `-c` overrides. prepare_project sets only user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf locally (project.py:100-102). The commits at project.py:147, 184, 202 and 266 pass only `-q -m` (202 adds `-- paths`). None of them passes `--no-verify` or `--no-gpg-sign`. Repo code search for `gpgsign`, `hooksPath` and `GIT_CONFIG` returned no hits. A grep of foreman.py, claudo.py, cli.py, sandbox.py and delivery.py for GIT_, gpg, hooks, NOSYSTEM and `git -c` found nothing either. So a global commit.gpgsign=true or core.hooksPath set by the operator applies to every factory commit. That breaks the docstring's promise (project.py:5-6) that commits never borrow the operator's personal git configuration, and it can stall a headless run on pinentry or let a personal hook reject the commit. One caveat: the docstring may mean only the identity (it says "LOCAL identity so..."), and both failures need a non-default operator config. Low severity is right.
2. The code does what the finding says. `_git_raw` (project.py:59-66) runs plain `["git", *args]` and passes no `-c` overrides and no env such as GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL or GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM. `prepare_project` sets only user.name, user.email and core.autocrlf locally (project.py:100-102). The commits at project.py:147, 184, 202 and 266 go through `_git` unchanged. I fetched the 88 .py/.sh/.toml/.cfg/justfile files on main and grepped them for GIT_CONFIG, gpgsign, hooksPath and GIT_AUTHOR/GIT_COMMITTER. The only hit is tests/test_project.py:37, which sets GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL just to check identity, so nothing elsewhere neutralizes signing or hooks. As a result, a global `commit.gpgsign=true` or `core.hooksPath` still applies to factory commits. That can block a headless run on pinentry, let a personal hook reject the scope-drift commit, or put the operator's signature on 'AI Factory' commits. Severity stays low for two reasons. The docstring (project.py:5-6) and the test (test_project.py:36-40) scope the promise mainly to identity, so the finding reads the docstring more broadly than its evident intent. And the failure needs a specific operator setup.

### A153. _untrack_runtime_state commits the whole index, sweeping in any change the operator had staged

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:147` · **category:** state-persistence · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** An existing app with tracked work/*/.runs/state.json has a file staged by the operator. Starting a change commits that file under 'chore: stop tracking Claudo runtime state', so it misses the 'baseline' commit meant to hold it, and the audit trail is misleading.
- **Suggested fix:** Commit only the removed paths (`git commit -m ... -- *tracked`), as _commit_paths does.

**Evidence**

> project.py:146 `git rm -q --cached -- *tracked` then project.py:147 `_git(app, "commit", "-q", "-m", UNTRACK_RUNTIME_COMMIT)  # index holds only removals`. That assumption is not checked. prepare_project is called from begin_change (project.py:254) before the baseline commit.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I confirmed this from the code. In src/factory/project.py, line 146 runs `git rm --cached -- *tracked` and line 147 runs `git commit -q -m UNTRACK_RUNTIME_COMMIT` with no pathspec. Git therefore commits the whole index, and nothing checks the inline comment's claim that the index holds only these removals. prepare_project reaches this path for an app that already has commits (lines 114-115). begin_change calls prepare_project at line 254, before the porcelain check and the BASELINE commit (lines 264-266). Anything the operator had staged is committed under 'chore: stop tracking Claudo runtime state' and never reaches the baseline commit. _commit_paths (lines 199-202) shows the module's own pattern of passing `-- paths` to commit, and this call does not follow it. The trigger is narrow: it needs runtime state committed by an earlier run plus files the operator staged. The result is a mislabelled commit in the audit trail; no data is lost. Severity stays low.
2. I read the code and it does what the finding says. In src/factory/project.py:146-147, `git rm -q --cached -- *tracked` is followed by a plain `git commit -q -m UNTRACK_RUNTIME_COMMIT`. That commit has no pathspec and takes the whole index. The comment says "the index holds only these removals", but nothing checks it. The same file already uses the safer pattern: `_commit_paths` (project.py:199-202) passes `-- *paths` to commit.  Path to the bug: prepare_project (project.py:82, the else branch at 115) calls `_untrack_runtime_state` whenever HEAD exists. begin_change calls prepare_project at project.py:254, before the baseline step at 264-266 (porcelain, add -A, commit BASELINE_COMMIT). foreman.py:484, 1091 and 1478 also call prepare_project.  Failure case: an existing app tracks work/<x>/.runs/state.json and the operator has also staged a file. The staged file then lands in the "stop tracking Claudo runtime state" commit, not in the baseline commit whose docstring (227-232) promises to hold anything uncommitted when the change begins.  The final tree content is the same either way, so the damage is limited to a commit that mixes in unrelated content. Low severity is right.

### A154. The `or "main"` fallback for factory-base is dead code: merge/abandon/resume fail when the branch config is absent

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:257` · **category:** path-handling · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** The app repo is re-cloned, or the change branch is fetched from the published remote (the branch.*.factory-base key lives only in the local .git/config). `factory merge`, `factory abandon` or a resumed build fails with "git config --get branch.factory/x.factory-base failed in ...: " and an empty message, instead of falling back to main.
- **Suggested fix:** Use a non-raising helper (subprocess.run, return '' when returncode is 1) for config lookups.

**Evidence**

> project.py:257, 279 and 319 use `_git(app, "config", "--get", f"branch.{branch}.factory-base") or "main"`. `git config --get` exits 1 when the key is missing, and _git_raw (project.py:64-65) raises ProjectError on any non-zero exit, so the `or "main"` fallback is never reached.

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from the code. In project.py, lines 257, 279 and 319 each call `_git(app, "config", "--get", f"branch.{branch}.factory-base") or "main"`. `_git` (line 69) calls `_git_raw` (line 59), and `_git_raw` raises ProjectError whenever returncode != 0. I checked `git config --get` on a missing key in an empty throwaway repo: it exits with rc=1 and prints nothing. So the `or "main"` fallback could only run if the key existed with an empty value, which makes it effectively dead code. The error message is also empty, because both stderr and stdout are empty.  Each of the three callers checks `branch_exists`, and that only looks at the local ref `refs/heads/factory/<slug>` (line 240). A local branch can exist without the key. For example, after a fresh clone, `git switch factory/x` creates the local branch from the remote, but the factory-base key is only written by begin_change at line 269 into the local .git/config. In that case merge, abandon and resume raise "git config --get ... failed in ...: " instead of falling back to main.  Severity stays low. The scenario needs a branch that was not created by begin_change in the same clone, and the result is a clear failure, not data loss.
2. I could not refute this finding; the code confirms it. In project.py:59-66, _git_raw raises ProjectError whenever git exits non-zero. `git config --get` exits 1 when the key is missing, so at project.py:257, 279 and 319 the `or "main"` fallback only fires if the stored value is an empty string. It never fires for the missing-key case it was written for.  The only place the key is written is project.py:269, inside begin_change, and it goes to the local .git/config. branch_exists (project.py:240-246) only checks that refs/heads/factory/<slug> exists. It does not check the config key.  That leaves a realistic way to have the branch without the key: re-clone the repo, then run `git switch factory/x` from origin. This creates a local branch with no factory-base key. After that, merge_fast_forward, abandon_change and a resumed begin_change all raise "git config --get branch.factory/x.factory-base failed in ...:" with an empty message instead of falling back to main.  The finding's own description does not overstate this. The defect is real but confined to an edge case, so severity stays low.

### A155. A change started on a detached HEAD records base 'HEAD', so a later merge cannot switch back

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:261` · **category:** path-handling · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** core
- **Failure scenario:** The app folder is checked out at a tag or SHA when `factory run <change>` begins. The change builds, but `factory merge` fails on `git switch -q HEAD` (invalid reference).
- **Suggested fix:** In begin_change, refuse with a clear error when HEAD is detached.

**Evidence**

> project.py:236-237 `current_branch` returns `git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD`, which is the literal 'HEAD' when detached. project.py:261-269 stores it: `_git(app, "config", f"branch.{branch}.factory-base", base)`. merge_fast_forward then runs `_git(app, "switch", "-q", base)` (project.py:282).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this; the code matches the finding. In project.py:236-237, current_branch returns `git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD`, which prints the literal "HEAD" on a detached checkout. begin_change has no guard against that. Its only check is `base.startswith("factory/")` (project.py:263-264), and prepare_project (project.py:82-115) never checks for a detached HEAD either. So begin_change stores "HEAD" as the base: `_git(app, "config", f"branch.{branch}.factory-base", base)` at project.py:269. merge_fast_forward then reads that value back (project.py:278) and runs `_git(app, "switch", "-q", base)` at project.py:282, outside the try block. `git switch HEAD` without --detach is refused because "HEAD" is not a branch, so the call raises ProjectError with a raw git message. Even if the switch somehow succeeded, the app would stay on the factory branch, and the later `branch -d` of the checked-out branch (project.py:290) would fail. On resume, the fallback at project.py:259 handles a missing base, but it does not fix merge. Lines 319-321 and 352 show the same pattern elsewhere. The impact is small: the change branch is intact, the error is a clear git failure, and the user can merge by hand. The severity stays low.
2. I confirmed this from code I read remotely. In src/factory/project.py, current_branch (236-237) returns `git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD`. begin_change sets `base = current_branch(app)` at line 261. The only guard (262-263) rejects a base that starts with 'factory/', and prepare_project (82-115) has no detached-HEAD check. Line 269 stores `branch.factory/<slug>.factory-base = HEAD`. merge_fast_forward reads that value back at line 279 and runs `git switch -q HEAD` at line 282. I reproduced the sequence in a throwaway scratch repo: detach HEAD, `git switch -c factory/x`, then `git switch -q HEAD`. Git exits with code 128 and prints "fatal: a branch is expected, got 'refs/heads/factory/x'". The merge therefore fails, and the error is raw git output, not the ProjectError message the code would otherwise give. abandon_change also reads the same config value (line 319). The precondition, an app left on a detached HEAD, is uncommon, so low severity fits.

### A156. Network git (push/fetch) runs with no timeout and with interactive prompts allowed

- **Location:** `src/factory/project.py:342` · **category:** timeouts · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** `factory publish` pushes to the https URL from create_private_repo (delivery.py:101) on a machine with no git credential helper for github.com (e.g. gh set up with ssh). git waits on a username prompt, or a stalled connection hangs, and `factory publish` never returns. The repo was already created and saved, so the user cannot tell what state it is in.
- **Suggested fix:** Run network git with a timeout and env GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0 (and GCM_INTERACTIVE=never), and turn the TimeoutExpired into ProjectError.

**Evidence**

> push_branch -> `_git(app, 'push', '-q', '-u', remote, branch)` (project.py:342). sync_merged_base -> `_git(app, 'fetch', '-q', 'origin')` (project.py:350). _git_raw calls subprocess.run without timeout and without GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0 (project.py:61-63). By contrast, GhCli caps every gh call at 120 s (delivery.py:78).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I could not refute this. The code says what the finding claims. project.py:61-63 shows that `_git_raw` calls `subprocess.run(["git", *args], cwd=app, capture_output=True, ...)` with no `timeout` and no `env`, so nothing sets GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0. Network calls go through this path: `push_branch` at project.py:342 runs `git push -q -u remote branch`, and `sync_merged_base` at project.py:350 runs `git fetch -q origin`. Their callers are foreman.py:1118, 1183 and 1237. I searched every .py, .toml, .sh and justfile in the repo for GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT and found nothing. That rules out a guard elsewhere that I could see. By contrast, GhCli._exec in delivery.py passes `timeout=120` and turns TimeoutExpired into DeliveryError. project.py itself uses timeout=300 for another subprocess near line 449. So the missing timeout on network git is an inconsistency, not a documented choice. Of the two failure paths, a stalled connection hanging forever is the more certain one. The credential-prompt path depends on the machine: git prompts on /dev/tty, so the captured output does not stop it. A missing credential helper is plausible but not guaranteed, because macOS git usually ships osxkeychain. The impact is that the CLI hangs, with no data corruption, so low severity is right.
2. I read the code remotely and it matches the finding. src/factory/project.py:61-63: _git_raw calls subprocess.run(["git", ...], cwd=app, capture_output=True, ...). It sets no timeout and no env, so GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT is not set (a search of the repo for GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT returns nothing). push_branch at project.py:342 and the fetch in sync_merged_base at project.py:350 both go through it. By contrast, GhCli._exec sets timeout=120 at delivery.py:78 and turns TimeoutExpired into DeliveryError. delivery.py:101 returns an https URL from create_private_repo. One correction to the scenario: git asks for credentials on /dev/tty, so the user does see the prompt; nothing hangs silently. On GitHub that prompt can only lead to an auth failure, which then surfaces as a ProjectError. The real unbounded hang is a stalled network connection during push or fetch, and that code path has no timeout. This is a real robustness gap, not intended or documented, but it depends on network conditions, so severity stays low.

### A157. Alias collisions between radar entries are silently resolved to the first entry

- **Location:** `src/factory/radar.py:81` · **category:** validation · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** governance
- **Failure scenario:** IT adds a hold entry whose match repeats an alias already listed under an earlier adopt entry. find() keeps returning the adopt tech, so the hold is never enforced in manifests, and the radar loads without error.
- **Suggested fix:** Report an error in load_radar when a normalized alias maps to two different techs.

**Evidence**

> radar.py:80-81 `self._by_name.setdefault(normalize(alias), t)`. load_radar (129-171) checks duplicate ids but not duplicate aliases.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I confirmed this from the code. In src/factory/radar.py:79-81, `Radar.__post_init__` builds `_by_name` with `self._by_name.setdefault(normalize(alias), t)`, so the first tech to claim a normalized alias keeps it. `find()` (radar.py:98-100) returns that first tech. `load_radar` (radar.py:126-171) checks for missing fields, bad rings, duplicate ids (137-139), version specs, license format and `replaced_by` targets. It never checks for duplicate normalized aliases across techs, so a radar.toml with a collision loads without error. The collision also matters at runtime: guard.py:198 calls `radar.find(f.name)` for manifest dependencies, and `verdict(tech, maturity)` at guard.py:201 then uses the first tech's ring. If a later hold entry repeats an alias from an earlier adopt entry, a dependency with that name gets ALLOW instead of BLOCK. design.py:173, 233 and 303 and foreman.py:528 use the same lookup. The free-text path (`scan_text`, radar.py:102-104) would still report both techs, so only exact-name manifest and import lookups are affected. The current radar.toml shows no collision in the part I read (the listing was truncated), and the problem needs a data-entry mistake by IT. So this is a real but low-impact validation gap, and "low" is the right severity.
2. The code supports the finding. At src/factory/radar.py:79-81, `__post_init__` builds `_by_name` with `self._by_name.setdefault(normalize(alias), t)`, so when two techs share a normalized alias, the first one in file order wins. `find()` at radar.py:98-100 returns `_by_name.get(normalize(name))`, so the later tech can never be reached through that alias. `load_radar` at radar.py:126-171 rejects duplicate ids (137-138), bad rings, bad version specs, bad licences and dangling replaced_by values, but it never checks for duplicate aliases. A radar with a conflicting alias therefore loads with no error.  The scenario is realistic. The radar.toml header says IT owns and edits this file, and "match" names detect techs in manifests, imports and Docker images. Example: an adopt entry lists `"motor"` and a later hold entry also matches `"motor"`. `find("motor")` then returns the adopt entry, so `verdict()` (radar.py:110-115) returns ALLOW where the hold ring should BLOCK.  Nothing in tests/test_radar.py covers or documents first-wins as intended (a grep for alias/duplicate/first found nothing). The current radar.toml has no collision, so this is a latent validation gap, not a live bug.  One limit: `scan_text` (radar.py:82-93, 102-104) compiles a pattern per tech, so free-text scanning still reports both techs. Only the exact-name `find()` path (manifests, imports) is affected. Severity low stands.

### A158. [sandbox] proxy is decoupled from the proxy container whose health is checked (hardcoded lab-egress-proxy)

- **Location:** `src/factory/sandbox.py:49` · **category:** config-mismatch · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** gap5-config-keys-vs-consumers
- **Failure scenario:** IT points [sandbox] proxy at its own allowlist proxy, e.g. `http://corp-egress:3128`. Two outcomes are possible. If no lab-egress-proxy container runs, problems() reports 'the egress proxy container lab-egress-proxy is not running' and MVP/prod builds are refused even though the configured proxy is fine. If an old lab-egress-proxy container is still running, the check passes without ever verifying the proxy the agents actually use (sandbox.py:89-92).
- **Suggested fix:** Derive proxy_container from the host part of the proxy URL, or add a [sandbox] proxy_container key.

**Evidence**

> factory.toml:53 `proxy = "http://lab-egress-proxy:8888"` is loaded at config.py:126 and passed at cli.py:39 `Sandbox(image=..., network=..., proxy=cfg.sandbox_proxy)`. The readiness check inspects `self.proxy_container` (sandbox.py:72), which is a hardcoded default `proxy_container: str = "lab-egress-proxy"` (sandbox.py:49) that no config key sets.

**Skeptic notes**

1. Confirmed from the code. src/factory/sandbox.py:48-49 sets two separate defaults, `proxy` and `proxy_container = "lab-egress-proxy"`. The readiness check at sandbox.py:72-74 inspects only `self.proxy_container`. The container env at sandbox.py:89-92 and claudo_env at :125 use only `self.proxy`. config.py:54-56 and :124-126 read [sandbox] image, network and proxy, with no proxy_container key. cli.py:39 builds `Sandbox(image=..., network=..., proxy=cfg.sandbox_proxy)` and never sets proxy_container. factory.toml:51-53 exposes `proxy` as a user-editable key. So if `proxy` is changed to another host, problems() still checks the hardcoded lab-egress-proxy container. It either refuses the build with a false "not running" message, or passes without checking the proxy the agents actually use. Severity stays low for two reasons: the default setup.sh infrastructure matches the hardcoded name, and the failure is a false refusal or a weaker check, not an escape. The network-internal check at :67-71 still holds.
2. The code matches the finding. sandbox.py:49 hardcodes `proxy_container: str = "lab-egress-proxy"`, and nothing sets it: a search of every .py/.toml/.md/.sh file in the repo finds proxy_container only in sandbox.py (lines 49, 72, 74). cli.py:39 builds the Sandbox with image, network and proxy only. config.py:126 reads the [sandbox] proxy key (factory.toml:55) and has no key for the container. problems() (sandbox.py:72-74) inspects the hardcoded container, never the configured proxy URL. foreman.py:1411-1425 (_containment) turns any problem into a refusal for MVP/prod builds, and POC/POV builds fall back to running on the host. Pointing proxy at another host therefore leads to one of two results. With no lab-egress-proxy container running, MVP/prod builds are refused even though the configured proxy works. With a stale lab-egress-proxy container still up, the check passes without testing the proxy that claudo_env (sandbox.py:125) actually passes to the agents. Severity stays low: the documented setup (Claudo's setup.sh, per factory.toml:47-49) creates exactly lab-egress-proxy, so the defect only shows when someone uses the exposed proxy key to point at a different proxy.

### A159. Model credentials go in the docker command line and into every gate container

- **Location:** `src/factory/sandbox.py:112` · **category:** security · **found:** medium · **verified:** low · **finder:** execution
- **Failure scenario:** (1) While a build runs, any local user or process can read the API key or OAuth token in `ps -ef` / /proc/<pid>/cmdline of the docker client. (2) The gates run agent-written tests and package install scripts with the model credential in their environment. Through the proxy, which allows package registries, that code can exfiltrate the credential (e.g. an npm postinstall). The gates never need that credential.
- **Suggested fix:** Pass `-e NAME` without a value and give subprocess.run an env holding the secret, so docker reads it from its own environment; or use `--env-file` with a 0600 temp file. Do not forward CREDENTIALS in executor().

**Evidence**

> sandbox.py:112-113 `for key, value in sorted({**self.env(), ...}.items()): argv += ['-e', f'{key}={value}']`. env() (sandbox.py:83) copies ANTHROPIC_API_KEY / CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN (CREDENTIALS, sandbox.py:24-25). executor (sandbox.py:129) uses the same argv for gate commands such as `uv run pytest` / `npm ci`.

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it confirms the finding. sandbox.py:82-83: env() copies every os.environ key in FORWARDED, and FORWARDED includes CREDENTIALS (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN; lines 24-25). sandbox.py:112-113 then puts each one on the docker command line as `-e KEY=VALUE`, so the secret value is visible in the docker client's argv. That process lives as long as the run, up to the 1800 s runner timeout, and `ps` shows it.  executor() (sandbox.py:126-129) builds its command with the same self.argv(), so gate commands such as `uv run pytest` and `npm ci` also get the model credential, and the gates never use it. The executor really is used for gates: foreman.py:1415-1418 _gate_executor returns self.sandbox.executor when build_where == "sandbox", and foreman.py:1651 passes it to the gates.  Mitigating context: the build agent itself runs in the same kind of container with the credential (SandboxedRunner, sandbox.py:161-167). The module docstring says the model credential is meant to enter the container; only the approval secret is kept out. So the extra risk from the gates is third-party install scripts and agent-written tests, which reach the outside only through the allowlisted proxy. The command-line exposure is a separate issue and holds regardless. It is a real least-privilege and secret-handling defect. Medium is defensible, though low would also be reasonable on a single-user dev machine.
2. I could not refute it; the code does what the finding says. sandbox.py:24-25 defines CREDENTIALS as part of FORWARDED. env() at sandbox.py:83 copies every set FORWARDED variable from os.environ. argv() then adds each env entry to the docker command line as `-e KEY=VALUE`, with the secret value in plain text (read in the argv() loop just before `return [*argv, self.image, *command]`). executor() at sandbox.py:129 builds gate commands (`uv run pytest`, `npm ci`) through the same argv(), so gate containers get the credential too. The test suite asserts this: tests/test_sandbox.py:65 checks `"CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN=oat-test" in argv`. The module docstring (sandbox.py:8) confirms the egress proxy allows package registries. Both parts of the scenario hold: the token shows up in the host process list for the docker client, and third-party install scripts run in gates with the credential in their environment. Two things lower the severity. First, the model credential is meant to enter the agent container (comment at sandbox.py:23), and the agent there can already run arbitrary shell with it. So for agent-written code, the gate path adds little; the new exposure is third-party package scripts plus the host process list. Second, this is a local single-user dev tool, so the cmdline leak only matters to other local users or processes. The defect is real but low impact.

### A160. Model credentials passed as `docker run -e KEY=VALUE` are visible in the host process list

- **Location:** `src/factory/sandbox.py:112` · **category:** security · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** claims
- **Failure scenario:** While a sandboxed build runs, any local user or process that can read /proc/<pid>/cmdline (or `ps -ef`) sees the API key or OAuth token in the docker CLI arguments.
- **Suggested fix:** Pass `-e KEY` (name only) with the value in the subprocess env, or use --env-file on a 0600 temp file.

**Evidence**

> argv appends `-e {key}={value}` for every forwarded variable, including ANTHROPIC_API_KEY / CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN (24-25, 112-113).

**Skeptic notes**

1. I read the code and it confirms the finding. In src/factory/sandbox.py, lines 24-25 put ANTHROPIC_API_KEY and CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN into FORWARDED. Line 83 copies those values from os.environ in env(). Lines 112-113 then append `-e KEY=VALUE` for every entry to the `docker run` argv. That argv runs as a subprocess in executor() (line 129) and in SandboxedRunner.run() (line 167), so the token sits in the docker CLI's command line for the whole run, which can last up to 1800 s by default. Nothing guards against this: the argv carries the values themselves, not just the variable names, and the subprocess is not given its own env. Exposure depends on the host. On a default Linux host, other users can read /proc/<pid>/cmdline unless /proc is mounted with hidepid. On macOS, processes running as the same user can read it. Docker's own docs advise against passing secrets this way, and the module's docstring says it exists to keep secrets away from untrusted code. Low severity is right: an attacker needs local access to the host, and the token is a credential the agent already holds inside the container.
2. I couldn't refute it. I read src/factory/sandbox.py through raw.githubusercontent because the gh API was rate-limited, and the code does what the finding says. Line 24 sets CREDENTIALS = ("ANTHROPIC_API_KEY", "CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN"). Line 25 puts both credentials in FORWARDED. Line 83 env() copies every set FORWARDED variable with its value. Lines 112-113 add `-e f"{key}={value}"` to the docker run argv for each one. That argv goes to subprocess.run at line 131 (executor) and line 167 (SandboxedRunner.run). So the docker CLI process's command line holds the plaintext key or token for the whole run, which defaults to 1800s (lines 52 and 146). On a shared host, `ps -ef` or /proc/<pid>/cmdline shows it to other local users. The fix is valid: pass `-e KEY` with the value in the subprocess env. Nothing in the code or the module docstring guards against this or says it's intended. The docstring only promises that the approval secret never enters the container. Low severity is right. The exposure needs a local observer, and anyone with Docker access could already read the values through `docker inspect` (Config.Env) of the running container.

### A161. Model credentials passed on the docker command line, visible in the process list

- **Location:** `src/factory/sandbox.py:113` · **category:** secret-leakage · **found:** low · **verified:** low · **finder:** security
